# The fifty psalms

## Psalms 1
**Summary:** The Psalter opens with a fork in the road: the blessed man does not walk, stand, or sit with the wicked, but delights in the LORD's law and meditates on it day and night. He is like a tree planted by the streams of water — fruitful, unwithering — while the wicked are chaff the wind drives away. The closing line hangs over the whole book: the LORD knows the way of the righteous, but the way of the wicked shall perish. It is a wisdom psalm, and it stands as the doorway into everything that follows.
**Tags:** `delight-in-the-word` — "his delight is in the LORD's law. On his law he meditates day and night" (1:2; existing anchor), and the rooted-tree fruitfulness that follows (1:3); `blessing` — the Psalter opens on beatitude-blessedness: "Blessed is the man who doesn't walk in the counsel of the wicked," flourishing "like a tree planted by the streams of water" (1:1–3; existing anchor); `divine-judgment` — "the wicked shall not stand in the judgment… the way of the wicked shall perish" (1:5–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 2
**Summary:** The nations rage; the kings of the earth take a stand and the rulers take counsel together, against the LORD and against his Anointed — and heaven laughs. God has set his King on Zion, his holy hill, and tells of the decree: "You are my son. Today I have become your father," with the nations promised as his inheritance. The psalm ends by counseling the world's rulers to be wise, serve the LORD with fear, and give sincere homage to the Son — "Blessed are all those who take refuge in him." This royal psalm has no superscription; the New Testament later quotes it repeatedly of Jesus as the LORD's Anointed (Acts 4:25–26; 13:33) — a note for the reader, not a claim the psalm's own text makes.
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — the rebel kings face wrath: "He who sits in the heavens will laugh… he will speak to them in his anger," and the Son will "dash them in pieces like a potter's vessel" (2:4–5, 9, 12); `god-reigns` — heaven's throne over the raging nations: "He who sits in the heavens will laugh" at the kings' counsel, and God has set his King on Zion, his holy hill (2:1–6); `messianic-prophecy` — the decree "You are my son. Today I have become your father" (2:7), with the psalm's Anointed and Son quoted repeatedly of Jesus in the New Testament (Acts 4:25–26; 13:33) — reported as the NT's later application, exactly as the summary signposts. (`divine-judgment` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary; the kingship and messianic registers, logged then as vocabulary gaps, are carried by the concepts adopted 2026-08-25.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 3
**Summary:** A psalm by David "when he fled from Absalom his son" — the first psalm with a stated crisis behind it. Adversaries have multiplied, and many say there is no help for him in God; David answers that the LORD is a shield around him, his glory, the one who lifts up his head. Because the LORD sustains him he can lie down, sleep, and wake, unafraid of tens of thousands set against him on every side. The psalm closes where it insists all rescue starts: "Salvation belongs to the LORD."
**Tags:** `gods-protection` — "you, LORD, are a shield around me," and he sleeps and wakes because "the LORD sustains me" (3:3, 5); `trust-in-god` — surrounded, yet "I will not be afraid of tens of thousands of people who have set themselves against me" (3:2–3, 6); `prayer` — "I cry to the LORD with my voice, and he answers me out of his holy hill" (3:4, 7)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 4
**Summary:** An evening psalm by David: he calls on the God of his righteousness for relief and mercy, then turns to the men who love vanity and seek after falsehood, telling them to stand in awe, search their hearts on their beds, be still, and put their trust in the LORD. Against the many who ask "Who will show us any good?" David testifies that God has put more gladness in his heart than a bumper harvest of grain and new wine ever brings. The psalm settles into one of Scripture's calmest lines: in peace he will both lay himself down and sleep, for the LORD alone makes him live in safety.
**Tags:** `peace-of-god` — "In peace I will both lay myself down and sleep, for you alone, LORD, make me live in safety" (4:8); `prayer` — "Answer me when I call… Have mercy on me, and hear my prayer," and "The LORD will hear when I call to him" (4:1, 3); `trust-in-god` — "Offer the sacrifices of righteousness. Put your trust in the LORD" (4:5); `joy-in-the-lord` — "You have put gladness in my heart, more than when their grain and their new wine are increased" (4:7)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 5
**Summary:** A morning prayer by David: "in the morning I will lay my requests before you, and will watch expectantly." He grounds his plea in who God is — a God with no pleasure in wickedness, who will not let the arrogant stand and who abhors the bloodthirsty and deceitful — while David himself comes into God's house in the abundance of his loving kindness. He asks to be led in God's righteousness, his way made straight, because enemies with open-tomb throats and flattering tongues wait for a misstep; he asks God to hold them guilty and let them fall by their own counsels. The last word belongs to the sheltered: all who take refuge in the LORD rejoice, surrounded by favor as with a shield.
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Give ear to my words, LORD… in the morning I will lay my requests before you, and will watch expectantly" (5:1–3); `guidance` — "Lead me, LORD, in your righteousness because of my enemies. Make your way straight before my face" (5:8); `refuge-in-trouble` — "let all those who take refuge in you rejoice… because you defend them," and the righteous surrounded "with favor as with a shield" (5:11–12)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 6
**Summary:** The first of the Psalter's penitential laments: David pleads that the LORD not rebuke him in anger nor discipline him in wrath, for he is faint — bones troubled, soul in anguish, "But you, LORD — how long?" He is weary with groaning — "Every night I flood my bed. I drench my couch with my tears" — his eye wasting away because of grief. Then the psalm pivots on being heard: the LORD has heard the voice of his weeping, heard his supplication, accepted his prayer — and the workers of iniquity can depart, for shame now belongs to his enemies.
**Tags:** `the-lords-discipline` — "LORD, don't rebuke me in your anger, neither discipline me in your wrath" (6:1); `pastoral-prayer-for-healing` — "Have mercy on me, LORD, for I am faint. LORD, heal me, for my bones are troubled" (6:2); `pastoral-god-sees-my-suffering` — "the LORD has heard the voice of my weeping. The LORD has heard my supplication" (6:8–9); `lament` — the Psalter's first penitential lament, complaint carried straight to God: "But you, LORD — how long?" and "Every night I flood my bed. I drench my couch with my tears" (6:3, 6–7)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 7
**Summary:** A meditation by David "concerning the words of Cush, the Benjamite": accused, he takes refuge in the LORD and opens his own conduct to inspection — if there is iniquity in his hands, let the enemy overtake him. Since there is not, he appeals upward: God is a righteous judge who searches minds and hearts, administers judgment to the peoples, and has indignation every day. The psalm sketches evil's boomerang — the wicked man travails with iniquity, digs a pit, and falls into it, his violence coming down on his own head — and ends with thanks to the LORD Most High according to his righteousness.
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — "LORD, my God, I take refuge in you. Save me from all those who pursue me" (7:1); `divine-judgment` — "God is a righteous judge," who "administers judgment to the peoples" and readies sword and bow against the unrepentant (7:8, 11–13); `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — the slandered man asks the Judge of minds and hearts to "establish the righteous" and end the wickedness of the wicked (7:8–10); `sin` — sin's recoil: "He has dug a hole, and has fallen into the pit which he made… The trouble he causes shall return to his own head" (7:14–16); `slander-and-false-accusation` — a psalm sung "concerning the words of Cush, the Benjamite": the accused man opens his conduct to God's inspection — if there is iniquity in his hands, let the enemy overtake him — and asks the Judge of minds and hearts to vindicate him (7:1, 3–5, 8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 8
**Summary:** A hymn framed by one refrain: "LORD, our Lord, how majestic is your name in all the earth!" Between its two soundings, David looks up at the moon and stars — the work of God's fingers — and asks the question the night sky forces: what is man, that you think of him? The answer is staggering dignity: God has made man a little lower than the angels, crowned him with glory and honor, and set him as ruler over the works of his hands — sheep, cattle, beasts, birds, and fish. Even babies' lips are enlisted to silence God's enemies.
**Tags:** `creation` — "When I consider your heavens, the work of your fingers, the moon and the stars, which you have ordained" (8:3, with the creatures of land, sky, and sea, 8:7–8); `image-of-god` — human dignity under God: "What is man, that you think of him?… you have made him a little lower than the angels, and crowned him with glory and honor. You make him ruler over the works of your hands" (8:4–6); `praise` — the framing refrain, "LORD, our Lord, how majestic is your name in all the earth!" (8:1, 9); `creation-testifies` — the sky-witness producing right creaturely response: "When I consider your heavens, the work of your fingers, the moon and the stars, which you have ordained" — answered by "What is man, that you think of him?" (8:3–4; the witness register beside `creation`'s making register; vv. 5–6's human dignity stays with `image-of-god`, the map's deliberate split)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 9
**Summary:** David gives thanks with his whole heart and sings to the Most High, because God has maintained his just cause — enemies stumble and perish before the One who sits on the throne judging righteously. The psalm widens from personal rescue to a settled world order: the LORD reigns forever, will judge the world in righteousness, and is a high tower for the oppressed, who find that he does not forget the cry of the afflicted. From "the gates of death" David asks for mercy so that he can praise again in the gates of Zion, and the nations who dug pits fall into them. The needy will not always be forgotten, nor the hope of the poor perish forever.
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — "I will give thanks to the LORD with my whole heart. I will tell of all your marvelous works" (9:1); `divine-judgment` — "He has prepared his throne for judgment. He will judge the world in righteousness" (9:7–8, 16, 19–20; existing anchor); `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — "The LORD will also be a high tower for the oppressed," who "doesn't forget the cry of the afflicted" and remembers the needy and the poor (9:9, 12, 18); `trust-in-god` — "Those who know your name will put their trust in you, for you, LORD, have not forsaken those who seek you" (9:10); `god-reigns` — "But the LORD reigns forever. He has prepared his throne for judgment" — the enthroned King who "will judge the world in righteousness" (9:4, 7–8); `justice-and-oppression` — the societal register the enthroned Judge answers: "He will administer judgment to the peoples in uprightness," and "the needy shall not always be forgotten, nor the hope of the poor perish forever" (9:8, 18)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 10
**Summary:** The psalm opens with the ache of God's apparent absence — "Why do you stand far off, LORD? Why do you hide yourself in times of trouble?" — then holds a long mirror to the wicked man: arrogant, prosperous, sneering, hunting the helpless from ambush like a lion, certain in his heart that "God has forgotten… He will never see it." Verse 14 breaks the illusion: "But you do see trouble and grief," and the psalmist calls on God to arise, break the arm of the wicked, and not forget the helpless. It ends in confidence: the LORD is King forever and ever, hears the desire of the humble, and will judge for the fatherless and the oppressed so that mere men of the earth terrify no more.
**Tags:** `pastoral-god-sees-my-suffering` — against "God has forgotten," the turn: "But you do see trouble and grief… You help the victim and the fatherless," and "you have heard the desire of the humble" (10:11, 14, 17); `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — God arises "to judge the fatherless and the oppressed, that man who is of the earth may terrify no more" (10:12, 17–18); `sin` — the anatomy of the wicked: boasting, cursing, deceit, oppression, murder from ambush, and a heart with "no room in his thoughts for God" (10:2–11); `god-reigns` — the psalm's resolution rests on the throne: "The LORD is King forever and ever! The nations will perish out of his land" (10:16–18)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 11
**Summary:** Advisers tell David to flee as a bird to the mountain — the wicked have bent their bows to shoot in darkness at the upright, and "if the foundations are destroyed, what can the righteous do?" David's answer is where he already stands: "In the LORD, I take refuge." The LORD is in his holy temple and on his throne in heaven; "his eyes examine the children of men," "the LORD examines the righteous," and "his soul hates the wicked and him who loves violence" — on the wicked he will rain fire, sulfur, and scorching wind. The psalm closes with the upright's reward: they shall see his face.
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — the answer to "flee as a bird": "In the LORD, I take refuge" (11:1); `divine-judgment` — from his heavenly throne the LORD examines all and "on the wicked he will rain blazing coals; fire, sulfur, and scorching wind" (11:4–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 12
**Summary:** "Help, LORD" — the godly man is disappearing, and what remains is a society where everyone lies to his neighbor with flattering lips and a double heart, boasting "Our lips are our own. Who is lord over us?" Against them the LORD himself rises: "Because of the oppression of the weak and because of the groaning of the needy, I will now arise… I will set him in safety." The psalm then sets the contrast at its center — human words are flattery and vileness, but "the LORD's words are flawless words, as silver refined in a clay furnace, purified seven times" — and trusts God to preserve his own in a generation where the vile are exalted.
**Tags:** `honesty` — a world of falsehood, drawn in full: "Everyone lies to his neighbor. They speak with flattering lips, and with a double heart" (12:2–4); `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — "Because of the oppression of the weak and because of the groaning of the needy, I will now arise, says the LORD" (12:5); `justice-and-oppression` — society-wide oppression answered from heaven, in a generation "when what is vile is exalted among the sons of men" (12:1–5, 8); `trustworthiness-of-scripture` — the contrast at the psalm's center: against flattering human lips, "the LORD's words are flawless words, as silver refined in a clay furnace, purified seven times" (12:6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 13
**Summary:** Four times in two verses David asks "How long?" — will the LORD forget him forever, hide his face, leave him to daily sorrow and a triumphant enemy? He prays for even the smallest reversal: "Give light to my eyes, lest I sleep in death," and lest his adversaries rejoice when he falls. Then, without the circumstances changing, the psalm turns: "But I trust in your loving kindness. My heart rejoices in your salvation. I will sing to the LORD, because he has been good to me." Its compact shape — complaint, petition, trust — becomes a pattern for praying through darkness.
**Tags:** `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — from "How long, LORD? Will you forget me forever?" to "But I trust in your loving kindness" — despair prayed all the way through to hope (13:1–2, 5); `trust-in-god` — "But I trust in your loving kindness. My heart rejoices in your salvation" (13:5–6); `prayer` — "Behold, and answer me, LORD, my God. Give light to my eyes" (13:3); `lament` — the fourfold "How long?" complaint prayed without disguise: "How long, LORD? Will you forget me forever?" (13:1–2)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 14
**Summary:** "The fool has said in his heart, 'There is no God'" — and the life that follows that heart-verdict is corrupt and does abominable deeds. The LORD looks down from heaven to see whether anyone understands and seeks after God, and the survey returns empty: all have gone aside, together become corrupt, "no one who does good, no, not one" — the verses Paul later gathers into his indictment of the whole human race (Romans 3:10–12). Yet the evildoers who eat up God's people like bread will meet great fear, "for God is in the generation of the righteous," and the psalm ends leaning toward Zion: oh that salvation would come, that the LORD would restore the fortunes of his people.
**Tags:** `sin` — the universal audit: "They have all gone aside. They have together become corrupt. There is no one who does good, no, not one" (14:1–3). (Only one honest tag from the current vocabulary.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 15
**Summary:** A question-and-answer liturgy: LORD, who shall dwell in your sanctuary — who shall live on your holy hill? The answer is a portrait of integrity: one who walks blamelessly, does what is right, and speaks truth in his heart; who does not slander, harm his friend, or cast slurs against his fellow man; who honors those who fear the LORD, keeps an oath even when it hurts, lends without usury, and takes no bribe against the innocent. The psalm's last word is stability: whoever does these things shall never be shaken.
**Tags:** `holiness` — the character required to dwell on God's holy hill: "He who walks blamelessly and does what is right" (15:1–2); `honesty` — "speaks truth in his heart… doesn't slander with his tongue," and "keeps an oath even when it hurts, and doesn't change" (15:2–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 16
**Summary:** A poem by David that stakes everything on one confession: "You are my Lord. Apart from you I have no good thing." Refusing the multiplied sorrows of those who give gifts to another god, David takes the LORD himself as his assigned portion and cup — "the lines have fallen to me in pleasant places" — and blesses the LORD "who has given me counsel," his heart instructing him "in the night seasons." With the LORD always at his right hand he will not be moved — "my heart is glad, and my tongue rejoices. My body shall also dwell in safety" — for God will not leave his soul in Sheol nor allow his holy one to see corruption. The path ends in presence: fullness of joy, pleasures forever more. (Peter later preaches these closing verses at Pentecost as reaching beyond David to resurrection, Acts 2:25–28 — a New Testament application; the psalm's own text speaks as David's confidence in God.)
**Tags:** `joy-in-the-lord` — "In your presence is fullness of joy. In your right hand there are pleasures forever more" (16:9, 11; existing anchor); `presence-of-god` — "I have set the LORD always before me. Because he is at my right hand, I shall not be moved" (16:8, 11); `contentment` — "The LORD assigned my portion and my cup… The lines have fallen to me in pleasant places. Yes, I have a good inheritance" (16:5–6); `trust-in-god` — "Preserve me, God, for I take refuge in you," and "Apart from you I have no good thing" (16:1–2); `messianic-prophecy` — "For you will not leave my soul in Sheol, neither will you allow your holy one to see corruption" (16:10) — the verses Peter preaches at Pentecost as reaching beyond David to resurrection (Acts 2:25–28), reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 17
**Summary:** A prayer by David from a man whose conscience is clear: he asks God — "You have proved my heart. You have visited me in the night. You have tried me, and found nothing" — to hear a plea that comes from undeceitful lips, and to let his sentence come from God's own presence. The petitions reach for tenderness inside danger: "Keep me as the apple of your eye. Hide me under the shadow of your wings," away from the deadly enemies who surround him like a lion greedy for prey. He asks God to confront and bring down the wicked — men of the world whose portion is in this life — while his own portion lies elsewhere: "I shall be satisfied, when I awake, with seeing your form."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Hear, LORD, my righteous plea. Give ear to my prayer… I have called on you, for you will answer me, God" (17:1, 6); `gods-protection` — "Keep me as the apple of your eye. Hide me under the shadow of your wings" (17:8–9); `hope-in-god` — beyond this-life portions: "I shall be satisfied, when I awake, with seeing your form" (17:14–15)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 18
**Summary:** The longest psalm of Book I, sung by David "in the day that the LORD delivered him from the hand of all his enemies, and from the hand of Saul." It opens in a cascade of names — my rock, my fortress, my deliverer, my shield, the horn of my salvation, my high tower — then relives the rescue as a storm theophany: the cords of death surrounded him, he cried out, and God bowed the heavens and came down, thundering, scattering enemies with lightning, and drawing him out of many waters "because he delighted in me." David credits God for every strength — feet like a deer's, hands taught for war, a lamp lit in darkness — and for the gentleness that made him great. The psalm closes in public thanksgiving: the LORD lives, and he shows loving kindness to his anointed, to David and his offspring forever.
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — "The LORD is my rock, my fortress, and my deliverer… in whom I take refuge" (18:1–2); `gods-protection` — "He is a shield to all those who take refuge in him," the whole rescue narrative from the cords of death to "he drew me out of many waters" (18:4–6, 16–19, 30); `praise` — "I call on the LORD, who is worthy to be praised… The LORD lives! Blessed be my rock" (18:3, 46); `thanksgiving` — "Therefore I will give thanks to you, LORD, among the nations, and will sing praises to your name" (18:49); `gods-faithfulness` — covenant loyalty stretching forward: "he… shows loving kindness to his anointed, to David and to his offspring, forever more" (18:50)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings
## Psalms 19
**Summary:** Two revelations meet in one psalm. First the sky: the heavens declare the glory of God, day after day pouring out speech, their voice going out through all the earth and their words to the end of the world, with the sun crossing its circuit as a bridegroom coming out of his room, "like a strong man rejoicing to run his course." Then the scroll: the LORD's law is perfect, restoring the soul; his precepts right, his commandment pure, his ordinances true — more desirable than much fine gold, sweeter than honey. The psalm ends where revelation lands, in the reader: a prayer for forgiveness of hidden errors, restraint from presumptuous sins, and words and thoughts acceptable to "my rock, and my redeemer."
**Tags:** `creation` — "The heavens declare the glory of God. The expanse shows his handiwork" (19:1–6; existing anchor); `delight-in-the-word` — the LORD's words "more to be desired than gold… sweeter also than honey," perfect and soul-restoring (19:7–10); `obedience-to-the-word` — "your servant is warned by them. In keeping them there is great reward" (19:11); `trustworthiness-of-scripture` — the scroll's reliability stated point by point: "The LORD's law is perfect, restoring the soul. The LORD's covenant is sure, making wise the simple," and "The LORD's ordinances are true, and righteous altogether" (19:7–9); `creation-testifies` — the wordless, continuous cosmic testimony and its every-person reach: "Day after day they pour out speech, and night after night they display knowledge." and "There is no speech nor language where their voice is not heard." (19:2–3, fixture-verified; the witness register beside `creation`'s making register on 19:1 — the concept's namesake text)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 20
**Summary:** A congregation prays over its king before battle: may the LORD answer you in the day of trouble, send help from the sanctuary, remember your offerings, and grant your heart's desire — "may the LORD grant all your requests." Confidence answers the prayer: now I know that the LORD saves his anointed and will answer from his holy heaven. The famous contrast follows — some trust in chariots and some in horses, "but we trust in the name of the LORD our God. They are bowed down and fallen, but we rise up, and stand upright." The psalm closes as it began, asking the LORD to save and answer.
**Tags:** `praying-for-leaders` — a whole congregation interceding for the LORD's anointed king: "May the LORD answer you in the day of trouble… Save, LORD! Let the King answer us when we call!" (20:1–5, 9); `trust-in-god` — "Some trust in chariots, and some in horses, but we trust in the name of the LORD our God" (20:7–8); `prayer` — petition and assurance of answer: "the LORD saves his anointed. He will answer him from his holy heaven" (20:4–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 21
**Summary:** The companion to Psalm 20: the battle is over, and the king rejoices in the LORD's strength and salvation. God has given his heart's desire, met him with the blessings of goodness, set a crown of fine gold on his head, and answered his request for life with length of days — the king's security resting not on his crown but on trust: "the king trusts in the LORD. Through the loving kindness of the Most High, he shall not be moved." The second half looks ahead: the king's hand will find out all God's enemies, whose plots cannot succeed, and the psalm ends in song: "Be exalted, LORD, in your strength."
**Tags:** `praise` — "The king rejoices in your strength, LORD!… Be exalted, LORD, in your strength, so we will sing and praise your power" (21:1, 13); `blessing` — "you meet him with the blessings of goodness. You set a crown of fine gold on his head… you make him most blessed forever" (21:3, 6); `trust-in-god` — "the king trusts in the LORD. Through the loving kindness of the Most High, he shall not be moved" (21:7)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 22
**Summary:** One of the Psalter's deepest laments opens with abandonment — "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" — prayed by a sufferer who is mocked ("He trusts in the LORD. Let him deliver him"), surrounded like prey by bulls, lions, and dogs, poured out like water, with pierced hands and feet and garments divided by lot. Yet even the complaint keeps hold of God: you are holy; our fathers trusted you and were delivered; you brought me out of the womb. At verse 22 the psalm turns wholly to praise: God "has not despised nor abhorred the affliction of the afflicted," and the deliverance ripples outward — the assembly, the humble who eat and are satisfied, all the ends of the earth turning to the LORD, and "a people that shall be born." The New Testament reads this psalm at the cross — Jesus prays its first line there (Matthew 27:46), and the Gospels echo its details — an application the reader should know, alongside what the text itself is: a righteous sufferer's lament that ends in world-sized praise.
**Tags:** `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — from "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" through to "You are my help" and answered praise (22:1–2, 19, 24); `pastoral-god-sees-my-suffering` — "he has not despised nor abhorred the affliction of the afflicted… when he cried to him, he heard" (22:24); `praise` — "Among the assembly, I will praise you. You who fear the LORD, praise him!" (22:22–23, 25–26); `worship` — "All the ends of the earth shall remember and turn to the LORD. All the relatives of the nations shall worship before you" (22:27–29); `lament` — the abandonment complaint that opens the psalm and holds through mockery and encirclement: "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" (22:1–2); `messianic-prophecy` — the psalm the New Testament reads at the cross: Jesus prays "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" there (Matthew 27:46), and the Gospels echo its details — the mockery, the pierced hands and feet, the garments divided by lot (22:1, 7–8, 16–18) — reported as the NT's application, as the summary signposts
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 23
**Summary:** David's shepherd psalm: because the LORD is his shepherd, he lacks nothing — green pastures, still waters, a restored soul, and guidance in paths of righteousness for his name's sake. Even the valley of the shadow of death holds no evil to fear, "for you are with me"; the shepherd's rod and staff comfort him. The scene shifts from pasture to table: a feast prepared in the presence of enemies, an anointed head, an overflowing cup — and a future in which goodness and loving kindness follow him all his days, and he dwells in the LORD's house forever.
**Tags:** `gods-provision` — "The LORD is my shepherd; I shall lack nothing," through pasture, water, and a table prepared before enemies (23:1–2, 5); `restoration` — "He restores my soul" (23:3; existing anchor); `guidance` — "He guides me in the paths of righteousness for his name's sake" (23:2–3); `pastoral-serious-illness` — the valley psalm itself: "Even though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil" (23:4; existing anchor); `presence-of-god` — the valley's whole comfort: "for you are with me" (23:4); `fear-not` — "I will fear no evil, for you are with me. Your rod and your staff, they comfort me" (23:4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 24
**Summary:** The earth is the LORD's with its fullness — he founded it on the seas — so the question is who may climb his hill and stand in his holy place. The answer: clean hands and a pure heart, no soul lifted to falsehood, no deceitful oath; such a seeker receives blessing and righteousness from the God of his salvation. Then the psalm becomes a processional: gates and everlasting doors are told to lift up their heads for the King of glory — and twice the question rings, "Who is the King of glory?" — answered by the LORD strong and mighty, the LORD of Armies.
**Tags:** `creation` — "The earth is the LORD's, with its fullness… he has founded it on the seas, and established it on the floods" (24:1–2); `holiness` — the ascent requirement: "He who has clean hands and a pure heart" (24:3–4); `worship` — the entrance liturgy of the King of glory: "Lift up your heads, you gates!… The LORD of Armies is the King of glory!" (24:7–10); `god-reigns` — the processional's whole point is the King's entry: "Who is the King of glory? The LORD strong and mighty, the LORD mighty in battle" (24:7–10)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 25
**Summary:** An extended prayer by David that keeps circling three requests: teach me, forgive me, deliver me. He lifts his soul to the LORD, waits for him all day long, and asks to be shown God's ways and guided in his truth — confident that the good and upright LORD instructs sinners and guides the humble. Forgiveness runs just as deep: don't remember the sins of my youth; "pardon my iniquity, for it is great"; forgive all my sins. Along the way the psalm names the intimacy on offer — "The friendship of the LORD is with those who fear him. He will show them his covenant" — and ends with the lonely, afflicted psalmist asking God to keep his soul, and to redeem Israel out of all its troubles.
**Tags:** `guidance` — "Show me your ways, LORD. Teach me your paths. Guide me in your truth, and teach me" (25:4–5, 8–9, 12; existing anchor); `forgiveness-of-sins` — "Don't remember the sins of my youth… pardon my iniquity, for it is great… Forgive all my sins" (25:7, 11, 18); `trust-in-god` — "My God, I have trusted in you… no one who waits for you will be shamed," and "I wait for you" (25:2–3, 5, 21); `covenant` — "All the paths of the LORD are loving kindness and truth to such as keep his covenant… He will show them his covenant" (25:10, 14); `loneliness` — "Turn to me, and have mercy on me, for I am desolate and afflicted" (25:16)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 26
**Summary:** David asks God to judge him — a startling request made possible by a clear conscience: he has walked in his integrity and trusted without wavering, so he invites the full audit: "Examine me, LORD, and prove me. Try my heart and my mind." He has kept clear of deceitful men, hypocrites, and the assembly of evildoers; instead he washes his hands in innocence, goes about God's altar with the voice of thanksgiving, and loves the house where God's glory dwells. He asks that his soul not be gathered with sinners and bloodthirsty men whose right hands are full of bribes, and takes his stand in an even place, blessing the LORD in the congregations.
**Tags:** `honesty` — "I have walked in my integrity," refusing the company of the deceitful and hypocrites (26:1, 4–5, 11); `worship` — "I will wash my hands in innocence, so I will go about your altar… LORD, I love the habitation of your house, the place where your glory dwells" (26:6–8, 12); `testing` — the invited examination: "Examine me, LORD, and prove me. Try my heart and my mind" (26:2)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 27
**Summary:** "The LORD is my light and my salvation. Whom shall I fear?" David has watched evildoers stumble, and even a besieging army cannot shake his confidence. What he actually wants is one thing: to dwell in the LORD's house all his life, to see the LORD's beauty — for in the day of trouble God will hide him in his pavilion and lift him up on a rock. The second half prays through the fear the first half defies: don't hide your face, don't abandon me — even if father and mother forsake me, the LORD will take me up — and don't hand me to false witnesses breathing out cruelty. The psalm lands on stubborn hope: I will see the goodness of the LORD in the land of the living; wait for the LORD, be strong, take courage.
**Tags:** `fear-not` — "Whom shall I fear?… Though an army should encamp against me, my heart shall not fear" (27:1, 3); `trust-in-god` — "Wait for the LORD. Be strong, and let your heart take courage. Yes, wait for the LORD" (27:14; existing anchor); `hunger-for-god` — "One thing I have asked… to see the LORD's beauty," and "'Seek my face,' my heart said to you, 'I will seek your face, LORD'" (27:4, 8); `loneliness` — "When my father and my mother forsake me, then the LORD will take me up" (27:10; existing anchor); `gods-protection` — "in the day of trouble, he will keep me secretly in his pavilion… he will hide me. He will lift me up on a rock" (27:5); `hope-in-god` — "I am still confident of this: I will see the goodness of the LORD in the land of the living" (27:13)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 28
**Summary:** David calls to his rock and pleads not to be met with silence — "lest, if you are silent to me, I would become like those who go down into the pit" — lifting his hands toward the Most Holy Place. He pleads, "Don't draw me away with the wicked, with the workers of iniquity who speak peace with their neighbors, but mischief is in their hearts," and asks God to give them according to their work and bring back on them what they deserve, since they ignore the LORD's works. Then the psalm turns on a hinge of answered prayer: "Blessed be the LORD, because he has heard the voice of my petitions" — the LORD is his strength and shield, his heart trusted and was helped, and song replaces pleading. It closes by widening to the whole flock: save your people, bless your inheritance, be their shepherd, and bear them up forever.
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Hear the voice of my petitions, when I cry to you," answered: "Blessed be the LORD, because he has heard the voice of my petitions" (28:2, 6); `trust-in-god` — "My heart has trusted in him, and I am helped. Therefore my heart greatly rejoices" (28:7); `gods-protection` — "The LORD is my strength and my shield… He is a stronghold of salvation to his anointed" (28:7–8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 29
**Summary:** David summons the heavenly beings — "you sons of the mighty" — to ascribe to the LORD "the glory due to his name" and to "worship the LORD in holy array." The psalm then rides a thunderstorm: seven times "the LORD's voice" sounds — over the waters, powerful and majestic, breaking the cedars of Lebanon, striking with lightning, shaking the wilderness, stripping the forests — while in his temple everything says, "Glory!" The LORD sat enthroned at the Flood and sits as King forever; and the God of all that thunder turns to his people with strength and blesses them with peace.
**Tags:** `worship` — "Ascribe to the LORD the glory due to his name. Worship the LORD in holy array" (29:1–2); `praise` — "In his temple everything says, 'Glory!'" as the LORD's voice fills the whole psalm (29:3–9); `god-reigns` — the storm hymn crowns its theophany with enthronement: the LORD sat enthroned at the Flood and sits as King forever, blessing his people with peace (29:10–11)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 30
**Summary:** A psalm of thanksgiving "for the Dedication of the Temple": David extols the LORD who raised him up, healed him when he cried out, and brought his soul up from Sheol. He invites the saints to sing, for God's anger is but for a moment and his favor for a lifetime — "weeping may stay for the night, but joy comes in the morning." David retells his own arc: prosperity bred the boast "I shall never be moved," God's hidden face undid it, and his plea — what profit is there in my destruction? shall the dust praise you? — was answered beyond asking: "You have turned my mourning into dancing for me," sackcloth exchanged for gladness, so that his heart sings and cannot be silent.
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — "Give thanks to his holy name… LORD my God, I will give thanks to you forever!" (30:4, 12); `pastoral-prayer-for-healing` — "LORD my God, I cried to you, and you have healed me" (30:2); `joy-in-the-lord` — "Weeping may stay for the night, but joy comes in the morning" (30:5; existing anchor); `restoration` — "You have turned my mourning into dancing for me. You have removed my sackcloth, and clothed me with gladness" (30:11); `praise` — "Sing praise to the LORD, you saints of his," so "that my heart may sing praise to you, and not be silent" (30:4, 12)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 31
**Summary:** David takes refuge in the LORD his rock and fortress, and hands everything over in the line Jesus would later pray from the cross (Luke 23:46): "Into your hand I commend my spirit." The psalm swings between confidence and distress — God has seen his affliction and set his feet in a large place, yet grief wastes his eye and soul, neighbors treat him as contemptible, and slander and conspiracy close in until he feels "forgotten… like a dead man… like broken pottery." The anchor holds: "But I trust in you, LORD… My times are in your hand." It ends celebrating the goodness God has laid up for those who fear him — hidden in the shelter of his presence — and urging all the saints: love the LORD, be strong, take courage, all you who hope in the LORD.
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — "In you, LORD, I take refuge… you are my rock and my fortress," and "In the shelter of your presence you will hide them" (31:1–4, 20); `trust-in-god` — "But I trust in you, LORD. I said, 'You are my God.' My times are in your hand" (31:14–15); `surrender-to-god` — "Into your hand I commend my spirit. You redeem me, LORD, God of truth" (31:5); `pastoral-god-sees-my-suffering` — "you have seen my affliction. You have known my soul in adversities" (31:7); `hope-in-god` — "Be strong, and let your heart take courage, all you who hope in the LORD" (31:24)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 32
**Summary:** A contemplative psalm by David on the happiness of the forgiven: "Blessed is he whose disobedience is forgiven, whose sin is covered" — blessed the man to whom the LORD does not impute iniquity. David knows both sides: while he kept silence his bones wasted away under God's heavy hand; when he acknowledged his sin and stopped hiding it — "I will confess my transgressions to the LORD" — God forgave the iniquity of his sin. Out of that mercy come counsel and warning: pray while God may be found; don't be a bridle-and-bit mule; loving kindness surrounds the one who trusts. Paul later quotes the opening beatitude when teaching righteousness credited apart from works (Romans 4:6–8). The psalm ends in the gladness forgiveness makes possible: "Be glad in the LORD, and rejoice, you righteous! Shout for joy, all you who are upright in heart!"
**Tags:** `forgiveness-of-sins` — "Blessed is he whose disobedience is forgiven, whose sin is covered," and "you forgave the iniquity of my sin" (32:1–2, 5; existing anchors); `repentance` — "I acknowledged my sin to you. I didn't hide my iniquity… I will confess my transgressions to the LORD" (32:5); `the-lords-discipline` — the weight of unconfessed sin: "day and night your hand was heavy on me. My strength was sapped" (32:3–4); `guidance` — "I will instruct you and teach you in the way which you shall go. I will counsel you with my eye on you" (32:8; existing anchor); `joy-in-the-lord` — "Be glad in the LORD, and rejoice, you righteous! Shout for joy" (32:11; existing anchor)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 33
**Summary:** An untitled call to praise: rejoice, you righteous — sing a new song with lyre and ten-stringed harp — for the LORD's word is right and all his work is done in faithfulness. The reasons pile up: by the LORD's word the heavens were made — "he spoke, and it was done"; he overturns the counsel of nations while his own counsel stands fast to all generations; from heaven he sees every human heart he fashioned. Armies, strength, and horses save no one; the LORD's eye is on those who fear him and hope in his loving kindness, to deliver their soul from death and keep them alive in famine. The psalm ends as a corporate confession of waiting and hope: he is our help and our shield.
**Tags:** `praise` — "Rejoice in the LORD, you righteous!… Sing to him a new song" (33:1–3); `creation` — "By the LORD's word, the heavens were made… For he spoke, and it was done" (33:6–9); `providence` — "The LORD brings the counsel of the nations to nothing… The counsel of the LORD stands fast forever," and he "fashions all of their hearts; and he considers all of their works" (33:10–11, 13–15); `hope-in-god` — "Our soul has waited for the LORD… we have hoped in you" (33:18, 20–22); `gods-protection` — "the LORD's eye is on those who fear him… to deliver their soul from death, to keep them alive in famine" (33:18–19); `trustworthiness-of-scripture` — "For the LORD's word is right. All his work is done in faithfulness" — the word that made the heavens and the counsel that "stands fast forever" (33:4, 6, 11)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 34
**Summary:** By David "when he pretended to be insane before Abimelech, who drove him away, and he departed" — and out of that narrow escape comes a psalm of testimony and teaching. He will bless the LORD at all times: he sought the LORD and was delivered from all his fears; "this poor man cried, and the LORD heard him"; the LORD's angel encamps around those who fear him. The invitation is sensory — "Oh taste and see that the LORD is good" — and the fear of the LORD is teachable: keep your tongue from evil, depart from evil, do good, seek peace. The psalm is honest about affliction — the righteous have many — but the LORD delivers out of them all, and stays "near to those who have a broken heart."
**Tags:** `praise` — "I will bless the LORD at all times. His praise will always be in my mouth… Let's exalt his name together" (34:1–3); `pastoral-near-to-the-brokenhearted` — "The LORD is near to those who have a broken heart, and saves those who have a crushed spirit" (34:18; existing anchor); `gods-protection` — "The LORD's angel encamps around those who fear him, and delivers them," and "Many are the afflictions of the righteous, but the LORD delivers him out of them all" (34:7, 17, 19); `taming-the-tongue` — "Keep your tongue from evil, and your lips from speaking lies" (34:13; existing anchor); `gods-provision` — "those who seek the LORD shall not lack any good thing" (34:9–10); `hunger-for-god` — "Oh taste and see that the LORD is good" (34:8; existing anchor); `angels` — the encamping-angel text itself: "The LORD's angel encamps around those who fear him, and delivers them" (34:7); `suffering-of-the-righteous` — both halves in one verse: "Many are the afflictions of the righteous, but the LORD delivers him out of them all" (34:19) — the psalm names the afflictions as real and many, and its confidence is testimony of God's faithfulness, not a formula of exemption
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings
## Psalms 35
**Summary:** David asks God to take up arms on his behalf — "Contend, LORD, with those who contend with me" — against enemies who hid a net for him without cause. His prayers against them are frank and severe: let them be disappointed, turned back, chaff before the wind, their own net catching them — judgment asked of God, not taken into his own hands. The wound underneath is betrayed kindness: when they were sick he fasted and mourned as for a brother or a mother, but "in my adversity, they rejoiced, and gathered themselves together," and false witnesses ask him about things he does not know. He pleads for vindication from the God who has seen it all — "You have seen it, LORD. Don't keep silent" — and promises thanks in the great assembly, his tongue talking of God's righteousness all day long.
**Tags:** `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — "LORD, who is like you, who delivers the poor from him who is too strong for him," and the plea "Vindicate me, LORD my God, according to your righteousness" (35:10, 23–24); `prayer` — sustained petition from "Contend, LORD" to "Rescue my soul from their destruction" (35:1–3, 17, 22–25); `thanksgiving` — "I will give you thanks in the great assembly. I will praise you among many people" (35:18, 28); `honesty` — "Unrighteous witnesses rise up. They ask me about things that I don't know about," and "they devise deceitful words against those who are quiet in the land" (35:11, 20–21); `slander-and-false-accusation` — the target-of-lies register: false witnesses ask him about things he does not know, and "they devise deceitful words against those who are quiet in the land" (35:11, 20–21); `vengeance` — "Contend, LORD, with those who contend with me" — the frank petitions that his attackers be disappointed, turned back, and caught in their own net are judgment asked of God, not taken into his own hands (35:1–8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 36
**Summary:** The psalm opens inside the wicked man's heart: no fear of God before his eyes, a self-flattery too thick to detect his own sin, words of iniquity and deceit, and plans plotted on his bed. Then the frame explodes: your loving kindness, LORD, is in the heavens; your faithfulness reaches to the skies; your righteousness is like the mountains of God and your judgments a great deep — you preserve man and animal. Under the shadow of those wings humanity takes refuge, abundantly satisfied with the abundance of God's house and drinking the river of his pleasures, "for with you is the spring of life. In your light we will see light." A closing prayer asks that pride's foot never arrive, and glances at the workers of iniquity already fallen.
**Tags:** `sin` — the portrait from inside: "There is no fear of God before his eyes. For he flatters himself in his own eyes, too much to detect and hate his sin" (36:1–4); `gods-love` — "Your loving kindness, LORD, is in the heavens… How precious is your loving kindness, God!" (36:5, 7, 10); `refuge-in-trouble` — "The children of men take refuge under the shadow of your wings" (36:7); `gods-provision` — "abundantly satisfied with the abundance of your house… You will make them drink of the river of your pleasures. For with you is the spring of life" (36:8–9)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 37
**Summary:** A wisdom psalm by David for people wearied by the sight of wicked success: don't fret because of evildoers, and don't envy them — they will soon wither like grass. The alternative is a string of quiet imperatives: trust in the LORD and do good; delight yourself in the LORD and receive your heart's desires; commit your way to him; rest and wait patiently; cease from anger, for fretting leads only to evildoing. The psalm keeps setting two futures side by side — the wicked's sword enters his own heart, while the humble inherit the land and delight themselves in the abundance of peace; better the righteous man's little than many wicked men's abundance. The aged psalmist adds his own testimony — "I have not seen the righteous forsaken, nor his children begging for bread" — and ends with salvation and stronghold for those who take refuge in the LORD.
**Tags:** `trust-in-god` — "Trust in the LORD, and do good… Commit your way to the LORD. Trust also in him" (37:3, 5; existing anchor); `peace-of-god` — "Rest in the LORD, and wait patiently for him. Don't fret" (37:1, 7–8; existing anchors); `envy-and-jealousy` — "neither be envious against those who work unrighteousness" (37:1); `contentment` — "Better is a little that the righteous has, than the abundance of many wicked" (37:16); `guidance` — "A man's steps are established by the LORD. He delights in his way" (37:23, 31; existing anchor); `gods-provision` — "In the days of famine they shall be satisfied," and "I have not seen the righteous forsaken, nor his children begging for bread" (37:19, 25); `prosperity-of-the-wicked` — the wisdom-psalm's own frame: the prosperity is real but transient, met with counsel ("Don't fret because of evildoers," 37:1) and eyewitness observation: "I have seen the wicked in great power, spreading himself like a green tree in its native soil. But he passed away, and behold, he was not. Yes, I sought him, but he could not be found" (37:35–36, verified byte-identical against the pinned fixture; 37:1 per the fixture with this doc's straight-apostrophe typography)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 38
**Summary:** A penitential psalm "for a memorial": David is under God's arrows and pressing hand, and he does not pretend otherwise — there is no soundness in his flesh because of God's indignation, no health in his bones because of his sin, and his iniquities have gone over his head — as a heavy burden, too heavy for him. The suffering is total: wounds loathsome and corrupt, burning pain, failing strength and eyesight — while friends stand aloof from his plague and enemies lay snares. He goes silent as a mute before his accusers, because his hope is elsewhere: "For I hope in you, LORD. You will answer." The psalm ends with confession without excuses — "I will declare my iniquity. I will be sorry for my sin" — and a plea: don't forsake me; hurry to help me, Lord, my salvation.
**Tags:** `the-lords-discipline` — "LORD, don't rebuke me in your wrath, neither chasten me in your hot displeasure. For your arrows have pierced me, your hand presses hard on me" (38:1–2); `sin` — "my iniquities have gone over my head. As a heavy burden, they are too heavy for me" (38:3–5, 18); `repentance` — "I will declare my iniquity. I will be sorry for my sin" (38:18); `pastoral-serious-illness` — prayed from a failing body: "There is no soundness in my flesh… I am faint and severely bruised," strength and even eyesight going (38:3, 5–8, 10); `hope-in-god` — "For I hope in you, LORD. You will answer, Lord my God" (38:15); `loneliness` — "My lovers and my friends stand aloof from my plague. My kinsmen stand far away" (38:11)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 39
**Summary:** David resolves to keep his mouth with a bridle while the wicked are present, but silence only stokes the fire, and what finally bursts out is a prayer about mortality: "LORD, show me my end, what is the measure of my days. Let me know how frail I am." His days are hand widths; every man stands as a breath, walks like a shadow, heaps up and doesn't know who shall gather. So the question turns Godward: "Now, Lord, what do I wait for? My hope is in you" — with pleas for deliverance from transgression and relief from God's correcting scourge, under which human glory is consumed like a moth's work. The psalm ends quietly and rawly: hear my prayer, don't be silent at my tears — I am a stranger with you, a foreigner as all my fathers were — spare me before I go away and exist no more.
**Tags:** `taming-the-tongue` — "I will watch my ways, so that I don't sin with my tongue. I will keep my mouth with a bridle" (39:1; existing anchor); `hope-in-god` — "Now, Lord, what do I wait for? My hope is in you" (39:7; existing anchor); `the-lords-discipline` — "When you rebuke and correct man for iniquity, you consume his wealth like a moth," and "Remove your scourge away from me" (39:10–11); `mortality` — "LORD, show me my end, what is the measure of my days. Let me know how frail I am," and the verdict, "Surely every man stands as a breath" (39:4–6, 11); `sojourners-and-strangers` — the pray-er's own self-description before God: "For I am a stranger with you, a foreigner, as all my fathers were" (39:12)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 40
**Summary:** David tells the story of a rescue that made a singer: he waited patiently for the LORD, who turned, heard his cry, brought him up "out of a horrible pit, out of the miry clay," set his feet on a rock, and put a new song in his mouth — a testimony many will see, and fear, and trust the LORD. God's wonders and thoughts toward us outnumber counting, and the response God wants outruns ritual: sacrifice and offering you didn't desire — "I delight to do your will, my God. Yes, your law is within my heart" (words Hebrews 10:5–7 later takes up of Christ). David has not sealed his lips: he has proclaimed glad news of righteousness in the great assembly and hidden nothing of God's faithfulness. Then the psalm turns back into need — innumerable evils, his own overtaking iniquities, mockers saying "Aha! Aha!" — and ends as the poor and needy man's plea: you are my help and my deliverer; don't delay.
**Tags:** `trust-in-god` — "I waited patiently for the LORD," and "Blessed is the man who makes the LORD his trust" (40:1, 4); `praise` — "He has put a new song in my mouth, even praise to our God" (40:3, 16); `obedience-to-the-word` — "I delight to do your will, my God. Yes, your law is within my heart" (40:8); `sharing-your-faith` — "I have proclaimed glad news of righteousness in the great assembly… I have not concealed your loving kindness and your truth" (40:9–10); `messianic-prophecy` — "Sacrifice and offering you didn't desire," and "I delight to do your will, my God" (40:6–8) — the words Hebrews 10:5–7 later takes up of Christ, reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 41
**Summary:** Book I's final psalm opens with a beatitude for the merciful: blessed is he who considers the poor — the LORD will deliver him in the day of evil, preserve him, and sustain him on his sickbed. David then prays from that sickbed: "Heal me, for I have sinned against you," while enemies wait for his death — "If he comes to see me, he speaks falsehood. His heart gathers iniquity to itself. When he goes abroad, he tells it" — and whisperers imagine the worst. The deepest cut is closest to home: "my own familiar friend, in whom I trusted, who ate bread with me, has lifted up his heel against me" — a verse Jesus later applies to his betrayer (John 13:18). David asks to be raised up, reads God's delight in the fact that his enemy has not triumphed, and stands upheld in his integrity — and the psalm closes Book I with its doxology: "Blessed be the LORD, the God of Israel, from everlasting and to everlasting! Amen and amen."
**Tags:** `generosity` — "Blessed is he who considers the poor. The LORD will deliver him in the day of evil" (41:1–3); `pastoral-prayer-for-healing` — "LORD, have mercy on me! Heal me," and the LORD who sustains and restores on the bed of illness (41:3–4, 10); `pastoral-betrayal-and-marriage-crisis` — betrayal at the table: "my own familiar friend, in whom I trusted, who ate bread with me, has lifted up his heel against me" (41:9); `slander-and-false-accusation` — sickbed visitors and whisperers imagining the worst: "If he comes to see me, he speaks falsehood. His heart gathers iniquity to itself. When he goes abroad, he tells it" (41:5–9); `messianic-prophecy` — the betrayal verse of 41:9, which Jesus later applies to his betrayer (John 13:18) — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** mhcc; bsb-headings *(the batch's Brooks copy is missing its Psalm 41 entry — see Decisions record)*

## Psalms 42
**Summary:** Book II opens with a contemplation by the sons of Korah, sung far from the sanctuary: as the deer pants for the water brooks, so the psalmist's soul pants and thirsts for God, the living God — while tears are his food and the taunt drums on, "Where is your God?" He remembers leading the joyful crowd to God's house, and the memory sharpens the loss. Twice he turns and interrogates his own soul — "Why are you in despair, my soul?… Hope in God! For I shall still praise him" — while deep calls to deep and God's waves sweep over him. Between the breakers, faith keeps its footing: by day the LORD commands his loving kindness, and at night his song remains — a prayer to the God of my life.
**Tags:** `hunger-for-god` — "As the deer pants for the water brooks, so my soul pants after you, God. My soul thirsts for God, for the living God" (42:1–2; existing anchor); `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — despair named and answered within the same breath: "Why are you in despair, my soul?… Hope in God! For I shall still praise him" (42:5, 11); `hope-in-god` — the refrain's resolve, "Hope in God!", held under the taunts and the waves (42:5, 8, 11); `lament` — "My tears have been my food day and night," and the complaint carried to God himself: "Why have you forgotten me? Why do I go mourning because of the oppression of the enemy?" (42:3, 9–10)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 43
**Summary:** A short untitled prayer that continues Psalm 42's situation and ends with its exact refrain. The psalmist asks God to vindicate him and plead his cause against an ungodly nation and deceitful men, pressing the question of 42 one turn further: "You are the God of my strength. Why have you rejected me?" The central petition is a homing prayer: send out your light and your truth; let them lead me to your holy hill — then I will go to the altar of God, "to God, my exceeding joy," and praise him on the harp. The refrain answers the soul's despair one more time: Hope in God, my Savior, my helper, and my God.
**Tags:** `guidance` — "Oh, send out your light and your truth. Let them lead me. Let them bring me to your holy hill" (43:3); `hope-in-god` — "Why are you in despair, my soul?… Hope in God! For I shall still praise him" (43:5); `worship` — "Then I will go to the altar of God, to God, my exceeding joy. I will praise you on the harp" (43:4); `lament` — continuing Psalm 42's complaint one turn further: "You are the God of my strength. Why have you rejected me?" (43:2)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 44
**Summary:** A corporate lament by the sons of Korah that begins with the fathers' stories: God drove out nations and planted Israel — they got the land not by their own sword but by God's right hand and the light of his face. On that history the community stakes its present trust — "God, you are my King," and "I will not trust in my bow, neither will my sword save me" — which makes the present incomprehensible: now God has rejected them, scattered them like sheep for slaughter, sold his people for nothing, made them a byword among the nations. The sting of the psalm is its integrity claim: all this came "yet we haven't forgotten you. We haven't been false to your covenant" — suffering the psalm cannot trace to any apostasy (Paul later quotes verse 22 of persecuted believers, Romans 8:36). It ends not in resolution but in a shout at heaven's silence: "Wake up! Why do you sleep, Lord?… Rise up to help us. Redeem us for your loving kindness' sake."
**Tags:** `prayer` — corporate protest carried honestly to God: "Wake up!… Why do you hide your face?… Rise up to help us" (44:23–26); `covenant` — the community's plea rests on covenant fidelity: "we haven't forgotten you. We haven't been false to your covenant" (44:17); `lament` — the corporate lament register itself, the community's protest at heaven's silence: "Wake up! Why do you sleep, Lord?… Rise up to help us. Redeem us for your loving kindness' sake" (44:9–14, 23–26); `suffering-of-the-righteous` — a whole-psalm protest that covenant faithfulness did not prevent national catastrophe: all this came "yet we haven't forgotten you. We haven't been false to your covenant" (44:17), the community regarded as sheep for slaughter (44:22) — innocent national suffering, left unanswered within the psalm (Paul's later use of v. 22 stays an attributed NT fact, per the summary's signpost). *(No pastoral-\* tag: national-scale corporate lament — see Decisions record item 17.)*
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 45
**Summary:** A wedding song for the king by the sons of Korah — the poet's heart overflows, his tongue like a skillful writer's pen. The first half praises the royal bridegroom: most excellent of men, grace-anointed lips, a sword strapped in splendor, called in his majesty to "ride on victoriously on behalf of truth, humility, and righteousness" — and then the address rises startlingly high: "Your throne, God, is forever and ever," anointed with the oil of gladness above his fellows (words Hebrews 1:8–9 later applies to the Son). The second half turns to the bride: forget your own people and your father's house; honor the king; and she is led in embroidered gold, with gladness and rejoicing, into the palace. The closing promise looks past one wedding day: sons who become princes, and a name remembered in all generations.
**Tags:** `blessing` — "Grace has anointed your lips, therefore God has blessed you forever," and the anointing "with the oil of gladness above your fellows" (45:2, 7); `messianic-prophecy` — "Your throne, God, is forever and ever" (45:6–7), the address Hebrews 1:8–9 later applies to the Son — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost. (`blessing` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary — a royal wedding ode, not marriage teaching (see Decisions record); the Hebrews-quoted throne address, logged then as a vocabulary gap, is carried by the concept adopted 2026-08-25.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 46
**Summary:** The Korahite psalm of unshakable confidence: God is our refuge and strength, a very present help in trouble — therefore we won't be afraid, though the earth changes and the mountains are shaken into the heart of the seas. Against the roaring waters stands a river whose streams make the city of God glad: God is within her; she shall not be moved; he helps her at dawn. Nations rage and kingdoms are moved, but "he lifted his voice and the earth melted," and the refrain plants its flag twice: "The LORD of Armies is with us. The God of Jacob is our refuge." The psalm ends with God's own voice stilling the tumult: "Be still, and know that I am God. I will be exalted among the nations."
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — "God is our refuge and strength, a very present help in trouble" (46:1, 7, 11; existing anchor); `fear-not` — "Therefore we won't be afraid, though the earth changes, though the mountains are shaken into the heart of the seas" (46:2–3); `presence-of-god` — "God is within her… The LORD of Armies is with us" (46:5, 7, 11); `peace-of-god` — "Be still, and know that I am God," from the God who "makes wars cease to the end of the earth" (46:9–10); `zion-city-of-god` — the city at the psalm's center: "There is a river, the streams of which make the city of God glad, the holy place of the tents of the Most High," and "God is within her. She shall not be moved" (46:4–5)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 47
**Summary:** All the nations are told to clap their hands and shout to God with the voice of triumph, for the LORD Most High is awesome — a great King over all the earth who subdued peoples under Israel and chose their inheritance, "the glory of Jacob whom he loved." God has gone up with a shout and a trumpet blast, and the psalm nearly runs out of breath repeating its one command: "Sing praises to God! Sing praises! Sing praises to our King! Sing praises!" — with understanding, for God reigns over the nations from his holy throne. Even the princes of the peoples gather as the people of the God of Abraham: the shields of the earth belong to God, who is greatly exalted.
**Tags:** `praise` — "Sing praises to God! Sing praises! Sing praises to our King! Sing praises!… Sing praises with understanding" (47:6–7); `worship` — the summons to all peoples: "Oh clap your hands, all you nations. Shout to God with the voice of triumph!" before the great King on his holy throne (47:1–2, 8); `god-reigns` — the psalm is a coronation shout: a great King over all the earth, gone up with a shout, who reigns over the nations from his holy throne — "Sing praises to our King! Sing praises!" and "Sing praises with understanding" (47:2, 5–8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 48
**Summary:** A Korahite song about the city where God's greatness can be walked around and counted: great is the LORD, greatly to be praised in the city of our God — Mount Zion, beautiful in elevation, the joy of the whole earth, where God has shown himself in her citadels as a refuge. Hostile kings assembled, saw, were amazed, and hurried away — trembling took hold of them there, pain as of a woman in travail — before the God who breaks the ships of Tarshish with the east wind. Inside the temple the song turns to God directly: "We have thought about your loving kindness, God, in the middle of your temple," and "as is your name, God, so is your praise to the ends of the earth." The tour of towers, bulwarks, and palaces has one purpose — "that you may tell it to the next generation. For this God is our God forever and ever. He will be our guide even to death."
**Tags:** `praise` — "Great is the LORD, and greatly to be praised, in the city of our God… As is your name, God, so is your praise to the ends of the earth" (48:1, 10); `worship` — "We have thought about your loving kindness, God, in the middle of your temple" (48:9); `gods-protection` — "God has shown himself in her citadels as a refuge," and the kings who fled dismayed: "God will establish it forever" (48:3–8); `zion-city-of-god` — the psalm walks the city itself: "Great is the LORD, and greatly to be praised, in the city of our God" — Mount Zion, whose towers, bulwarks, and palaces are counted so the next generation can be told "this God is our God forever and ever" (48:1–2, 12–14)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 49
**Summary:** A Korahite wisdom psalm addressed to everyone, low and high, rich and poor: the singer inclines his ear to a proverb and solves his riddle on the harp. The riddle is money and death: those who trust in their wealth cannot by any means redeem a brother — the redemption of a life is too costly, no payment is ever enough — and the wise and foolish alike die and leave their wealth to others, whatever names they gave their lands. "Man, despite his riches, doesn't endure. He is like the animals that perish"; death shepherds the self-assured to Sheol. One hope breaks the pattern: "But God will redeem my soul from the power of Sheol, for he will receive me." So don't be afraid when a man gets rich — he will carry nothing away when he dies.
**Tags:** `contentment` — the money-riddle: "Those who trust in their wealth… none of them can by any means redeem his brother," and "Don't be afraid when a man is made rich… for when he dies he will carry nothing away" (49:5–9, 16–17); `hope-in-god` — the psalm's one exception to death's shepherd: "But God will redeem my soul from the power of Sheol, for he will receive me" (49:15); `mortality` — the riddle's floor: "Man, despite his riches, doesn't endure. He is like the animals that perish" — wise and foolish alike die and leave their wealth to others (49:10–12, 17–20)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 50
**Summary:** The batch's one psalm by Asaph is a courtroom scene: the Mighty One, God, the LORD, speaks, and calls the earth from sunrise to sunset; out of Zion he shines, fire devouring before him, and he calls heaven and earth as witnesses to judge his own people — "God himself is judge." His first word is to the devout: he does not need their bulls and goats — every animal of the forest is his, and the livestock on a thousand hills — what he wants is the sacrifice of thanksgiving, vows paid, and dependence: "Call on me in the day of trouble. I will deliver you, and you will honor me." His second word is to the wicked who take his covenant on their lips while hating instruction — consenting with thieves, joining adulterers, framing deceit, slandering their own mother's son — and mistaking God's silence for approval: "You thought that I was just like you." The verdict closes both addresses: whoever offers thanksgiving glorifies God, and to him God shows his salvation.
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — "Our God comes, and does not keep silent. A fire devours before him… God himself is judge," and the rebuke set "in front of your eyes" (50:3–6, 21–22); `thanksgiving` — "Offer to God the sacrifice of thanksgiving… Whoever offers the sacrifice of thanksgiving glorifies me" (50:14, 23); `worship` — worship God actually wants: not bulls from the stall — "If I were hungry, I would not tell you, for the world is mine" — but thanksgiving, paid vows, and honor (50:8–15); `prayer` — "Call on me in the day of trouble. I will deliver you, and you will honor me" (50:15); `covenant` — "Gather my saints together to me, those who have made a covenant with me by sacrifice," against those who take "my covenant on your lips" while hating instruction (50:5, 16); `sin` — the indictment: consent with thieves, adultery, deceit, slander of a brother (50:16–21)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings
---
