# Decisions record (delegated defaults — reversible on Jesse's word)

1. **No subdivisions in Psalms 1–50.** CONVENTIONS §4 takes section boundaries strictly from BSB headings (or structural markers the book itself provides), and the public-domain BSB places exactly **one** heading per psalm in this range — all fifty at verse 1 (verified against the ebible.org engbsb USFM, 2026-08-08 edition). With no BSB-provided internal boundaries, and the psalms thematically unified at chapter scale, every psalm stays whole. Even the composite-feeling ones (19's sky/scroll, 22's lament-to-praise, 40's praise-to-plea) are held together by their own structure; MHCC's verse-range outlines were consulted for understanding but are not BSB headings and were not used as boundaries. The Psalter's own Book I/II division (41→42) is handled in prose, not sections.
2. **Vocabulary ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The taggable vocabulary is the 131 YAML file **basenames** under `ontology/concepts/` on main at b3f491d — including the 14 `pastoral-*` files whose internal `id:` fields differ (e.g., the file `pastoral-refuge-and-justice.yaml` carries inner id `refuge-and-justice-for-the-oppressed`; the tag used here is the basename, per CONVENTIONS §5's own example). The 9 ids newly merged via PR #43 (asking-in-gods-will, baptism, benediction, caring-for-aging-parents, christ-the-cornerstone, doubt, incarnation, justification-by-faith, trinity) are usable but **none is used in this batch** — each was considered and none met the honest-presence bar here (see items 4–6).
3. **Superscriptions as text, attribution only as stated.** WEB folds psalm headings into verse 1; they are treated as Scripture and used exactly as far as they state (Psalm 3's Absalom, Psalm 18's deliverance from Saul, Psalm 34's Abimelech incident). Psalms without superscriptions (1, 2, 10, 33, 43) are left anonymous — Psalm 2 is not called Davidic even though Acts 4:25 attributes it, since the tag/attribution policy follows the psalm's own text; the NT attribution is signposted in the summary instead.
4. **Psalm 16 and `resurrection` — not tagged.** Acts 2:25–28 preaches 16:8–11 as resurrection prophecy, and the summary signposts that. But the concept `resurrection` is a New Testament category; the psalm's own text states David's confidence that God will not abandon him to Sheol. Tagging it would read later revelation back as a present category — the same reasoning that kept `identity-in-christ` off Genesis 1.
5. **Psalm 32 and `justification-by-faith` — not tagged.** Romans 4:6–8 quotes 32:1–2 for righteousness credited apart from works, and the summary signposts that. The psalm itself states forgiveness and non-imputation; the by-faith element is Paul's frame, and the concept's own pack does not anchor Psalm 32. `forgiveness-of-sins` and `repentance` carry the chapter honestly. (Also a PR #43 id — see item 2.)
6. **Psalm 41:13 / Psalm 28:9 and `benediction` — not tagged.** 41:13 is a doxology closing Book I (praise ascribed to God), and 28:9 is an intercession addressed to God; neither is a benediction pronounced over people, which is that locator concept's substance. Noted in prose instead. (PR #43 id — see item 2.)
7. **Psalm 45 and `godly-marriage` — not tagged.** The psalm is a royal wedding ode (and Hebrews 1:8–9 applies vv. 6–7 to the Son, signposted); it celebrates a wedding but teaches nothing about marriage as such. A "godly marriage" searcher landing here would find court poetry — the same topic-touch-versus-substance line the Genesis pilot drew at `resisting-the-devil` on Genesis 3. Psalm 45 carries the single honest tag `blessing`.
8. **Psalm 33 and `praying-for-leaders` — not tagged despite the pack's anchor at 33:12.** The concept pack anchors "Blessed is the nation whose God is the LORD" for search, but the chapter depicts no praying for leaders or the nation — it states national blessedness. Search anchors serve queries; display tags require the chapter to depict the concept's substance. Psalm 20, by contrast, *is* a congregation praying over its leader and carries the tag.
9. **Psalm 2 and `refuge-in-trouble` — not tagged.** "Blessed are all those who take refuge in him" (2:12) is one closing clause; the psalm's substance is the LORD's Anointed enthroned over raging nations. One clause does not meet the honest-substantial-presence bar.
10. **Psalm 44 and `doubt` — not tagged.** The psalm protests God's apparent rejection ("Why do you sleep, Lord?") but never doubts God — it argues *with* him from covenant fidelity. `prayer` and `covenant` carry it. (PR #43 id — see item 2.)
11. **Psalm 12 and `taming-the-tongue` — not tagged.** The psalm depicts the *wicked's* tongues (flattery, boasting) and God's answer; it does not teach controlling one's own tongue. Psalms 34:13 and 39:1 — where the discipline is taught or practiced — carry that tag instead; Psalm 12 carries `honesty` for its portrait of a lying society (the Genesis 27/34 precedent of tagging honesty on deceit texts).
12. **Psalm 39 and `stewardship-of-days` — not tagged.** "Show me my end, what is the measure of my days" (39:4) is mortality-awareness, not the redeeming-the-time register that concept carries (its own pack notes Psalm 90:12 as the natural companion anchor — a later batch's question). Logged instead as refs to append to the `mortality` gap row.
13. **Imprecatory language — described, not endorsed.** Psalms 5:10, 10:15, 28:4, 31:17, 35:4–8, 26 and 40:14–15 ask God to judge, shame, or destroy enemies. Summaries state what the psalmist prays and its in-text shape — the wronged handing judgment to God rather than taking revenge — without presenting the curses as a model beyond what the text supports, and without sanitizing them. No `vengeance`-style tag exists; refs are logged for the existing gap row.
14. **New Testament quotations — signposted, never asserted as the psalm's own claim.** Where the NT quotes a psalm in this batch (2; 8:2 not noted in-summary; 14 → Rom 3; 16 → Acts 2; 22 → Matt 27/John 19; 31:5 → Luke 23:46; 32 → Rom 4; 40:6–8 → Heb 10; 41:9 → John 13:18; 44:22 → Rom 8:36; 45:6–7 → Heb 1), the summary marks it explicitly as a later application ("the New Testament later quotes/applies…"), per CONVENTIONS §6's signposting rule. No NT-doctrine tag is placed on any psalm on the strength of a quotation alone.
15. **Psalm 41's Sources line omits brooks-1919.** The batch's OCR copy of Brooks' *Summarized Bible* (archive.org scan) is missing its Psalm 41 entry (page/OCR loss between the chapters marked FORTY and FORTY-TWO); rather than claim a consultation that did not happen, Psalm 41 lists only mhcc and bsb-headings. Every other chapter consulted both.
16. **Provenance split stated per chapter-set, not silently pooled.** 19 chapters verified against the pinned-snapshot fixture (byte-identical to current); 31 chapters current-edition only — see the header. No claim of pinned-text verification is made for the 31.
17. **Pastoral-register ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The `pastoral-*` packs are the personal-crisis register only and are NOT tagged on national-scale material; national and societal-scale themes go to the tag-gap log instead. Applied in this batch's round-1 revision: `pastoral-hope-in-despair` **dropped from Psalm 44**, the batch's clearest national-scale corporate lament ("you… have scattered us among the nations… You make us a byword among the nations," 44:11, 13–14); the chapter keeps `prayer` and `covenant`, which carry it honestly, and its despair vocabulary remains described in the summary prose. All other pastoral-* tags in the batch sit on individual-crisis texts (Pss 6, 7, 9, 10, 12, 13, 22, 23, 30, 31, 34, 35, 38, 41, 42 — with 9:9–10, 10:14, 17–18, and 12:5 standing on the justice pack's own anchor ground and 12:5's personal-safety pledge inside its abuse-safety register). Motif item 4 is qualified accordingly, routing the societal strand to the shared log's landed `justice-and-oppression` row (Micah / 2026-08-23), which carries this same register-ruling rationale — this batch appends Ps 9:7–8, 16–20 and 12:1–4, 8 to that row (see Tag gaps) rather than duplicating it.
18. **Divine-name rendering ruling recorded (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The pinned corpus renders the divine name as "the LORD," not "Yahweh"; every quotation and all prose in this batch follow the corpus rendering. "Yahweh" appears nowhere in this document outside this ruling note.
19. **Psalm 1 and `blessing` — tagged (round-1 revision).** The draft's v1 left `blessing` off Psalm 1 without recording the call. On review: the pack anchors 1:1–3 directly, and beatitude-blessedness is the concept's own substance (its anchor set spans Matthew 5:3–12 and Psalm 34:8), which Psalm 1 depicts substantially — "Blessed is the man…" opening the chapter and the flourishing tree (1:1–3). The tag is added; reversible like every call here.

# Notes toward the book summary (for the book-summary worker)

Recurring notes building across Psalms 1–50 that the book summary will likely want:

- **Psalms 1 and 2 as a double gateway:** the blessed man rooted in torah (1) and the LORD's Anointed enthroned on Zion (2) — wisdom and kingship — open the whole Psalter; both end on "blessed"/refuge lines.
- **The lament-to-praise arc** is the batch's dominant shape (3, 6, 13, 22, 28, 31, and the 42–43 refrain): complaint and petition turning, usually before circumstances change, on being heard.
- **Refuge vocabulary** (rock, fortress, shield, wings, high tower, hiding place) is the batch's most repeated image of God.
- **"Loving kindness"** (the WEB's regular rendering of the covenant-love word) is the batch's most repeated attribute — a gloss the book summary should make once for lay readers.
- **A penitential strand** (6, 32, 38; 51 opens the next batch) runs alongside psalms of clean-conscience protest (7, 17, 26) — the Psalter prays from both postures without contradiction.
- **Book seams:** Book I (1–41, almost entirely "by David") closes with the doxology at 41:13; Book II opens with the Korahite run (42–49, the untitled 43 continuing 42) and Asaph's first psalm (50), and prefers "God" where Book I says "LORD."
- **Genres represented in 1–50:** wisdom (1, 15, 37, 49), royal (2, 18, 20, 21, 45), individual lament (3–7, 13, 17, 22, 25–28, 31, 35, 38–39, 42–43), corporate lament (12, 44), thanksgiving (9, 18, 30, 32, 34, 40 in part), hymn/praise (8, 19, 24, 29, 33, 46–48), penitential (6, 32, 38), prophetic covenant-lawsuit (50).
- **The king and the LORD's Anointed** (2, 18, 20–21, 45) — handled in-text as Israel's king, with NT applications signposted; the book summary will need the same discipline at 72, 89, 110.
- **God's sight and hearing of the sufferer** (6:8–9; 10:14, 17; 22:24; 31:7; 34:15, 17) — the Psalter's pastoral core, already well served by the pastoral-* concepts.

# Round-2 revision note (translation-echo sweep)

Round 2 identified a defect class — unquoted summary prose tracking a specific verse phrase-for-phrase while carrying another translation's rendering (against CONVENTIONS §2) — and cited eight instances, all fixed in v3 and carried forward here: Ps 2 "installed"→"set" (2:6); Ps 19 "speech without words" (an NIV line the WEB's own 19:3–4 contradicts) → rewritten to WEB's sense with "their words to the end of the world"; Ps 26 "level ground"→"an even place" (26:12); Ps 27 "gazing at"→"to see the LORD's beauty" (27:4); Ps 30 "my death"→"my destruction," "can"→"shall the dust praise you" (30:9); Ps 39 "handbreadths"→"hand widths" (39:5); Ps 46 "make glad the city of God"→"make the city of God glad" (46:4); Ps 50 "summons"→"calls the earth" (50:1).

The drafter's own full-batch sweep for the same class then caught fifteen further wording corrections, also fixed (Psalm 39's sentence contributes three): Ps 3 "the lifter of his head"→"the one who lifts up his head" (3:3); Ps 9 "sits enthroned, judging righteously"→"sits on the throne judging righteously" (9:4); Ps 15 "smear his neighbor"→"cast slurs against his fellow man" (15:3); Ps 16 "run after another god"→"give gifts to another god" (16:4); Ps 28 "repay them as their deeds deserve"→"give them according to their work and bring back on them what they deserve" (28:4); Ps 29 "Above the flood… sits enthroned"→"sat enthroned at the Flood and sits as King forever" (29:10); Ps 36 "feasting on the abundance"→"abundantly satisfied with the abundance" (36:8); Ps 38 "festering wounds"→"wounds loathsome and corrupt" (38:5); Ps 39 "guard his mouth"→"keep his mouth" (39:1), "walks as a shadow"→"walks like a shadow" and "heaps up wealth without knowing who will gather it"→"heaps up and doesn't know who shall gather" (39:6); Ps 41 "collect gossip"→"hearts gathering iniquity to tell abroad" (41:6); Ps 46 "kingdoms totter"→"kingdoms are moved" (46:6); Ps 48 "trembling like a woman in labor"→"trembling took hold of them there, pain as of a woman in travail" (48:6); Ps 50 "recite his covenant"→"take his covenant on their lips" (50:16). Every replacement was mechanically verified as the WEB's own wording.

# Round-3 revision note (exhaustive echo-closure pass)

Round 3 confirmed all 23 prior fixes hold and cited thirteen further instances of the same defect class, all fixed in this v4 (each replacement mechanically verified against the extracted WEB text): Ps 2 → "tells of the decree" (2:7); Ps 11 (two instances) → "flee as a bird to the mountain" and "have bent their bows to shoot in darkness at the upright" (11:1–2); Ps 16 (an ESV rendering) → "will not leave his soul in Sheol nor allow his holy one to see corruption" (16:10); Ps 19 → "hidden errors" and "presumptuous sins" (19:12–13); Ps 22 → the WEB's own quoted "a people that shall be born" (22:31); Ps 25 → "keep his soul" and "redeem Israel out of all its troubles" (25:20, 22); Ps 26 → "not be gathered with sinners," "right hands are full of bribes," "an even place" (26:9–12); Ps 28 (an NIV-verbatim line) → "be their shepherd, and bear them up forever" (28:9); Ps 29 → the summary now quotes the WEB directly: "the glory due to his name," "worship the LORD in holy array" (29:2); Ps 31 → "the goodness God has laid up for those who fear him" (31:19); Ps 37 → "delight themselves in the abundance of peace" (37:11); Ps 39 → "a foreigner as all my fathers were — spare me" (39:12–13). The round's borderline note on Ps 41 was also taken — but the v4 rewrite itself introduced a rival's marker ("tell it when they go out" against WEB's "when he goes abroad, he tells it"); round 4 caught the regression, and v5 now quotes 41:6 verbatim instead.

Because three manual sweeps had not closed the class, this v4 then applied a stricter mechanical rule exhaustively: every summary sentence was re-read against its verses, and every phrase tracking a specific verse was converted to either (a) a verbatim, quote-marked WEB quotation, mechanically verified, or (b) plain structural description that does not reuse a memorable rival rendering's distinctive vocabulary — quoting WEB when in doubt. The pass was assisted by a fuzzy near-quote detector (within-verse WEB n-grams matched against draft prose allowing one word substitution or deletion, pronouns normalized), whose every flag was manually triaged. It produced **18 further conversions** across fifteen psalms: Ps 1 "planted by the streams of water" (1:3); Ps 2 "the kings of the earth take a stand and the rulers take counsel together" (2:2) and "give sincere homage to the Son" (2:12); Ps 4 "the men who love vanity and seek after falsehood" (4:2) and "in peace he will both lay himself down and sleep" (4:8); Ps 5 "in the abundance of his loving kindness" (5:7) and "let them fall by their own counsels" (5:10); Ps 6 "wasting away because of grief" (6:7); Ps 13 "rejoice when he falls" (13:4); Ps 17 "like a lion greedy for prey" (17:12); Ps 19 "as a bridegroom coming out of his room" (19:5); Ps 26 "goes about God's altar" (26:6); Ps 27 "lift him up on a rock" (27:5); Ps 32 "kept silence" (32:3); Ps 38 "gone over his head — as a heavy burden, too heavy for him" (38:4); Ps 39 "go away and exist no more" (39:13); Ps 43 "praise him on the harp" (43:4); Ps 48 (48:10 — this conversion was itself defective: the pass's paraphrase supplied the NIV's verb "reaches," which the WEB verse does not contain; round 4 caught it, and v5 quotes 48:10 verbatim instead). Corrected tally: of the pass's 18 conversions, 17 held under round-4 review and 1 (48:10 here) was itself a rival-rendering regression; the round's other regression (41:6, above) came from the borderline rewrite. Both are fixed quote-first in v5.

# Round-4 revision note (quote-first corrections)

Round 4 verified all 15 round-3 resolutions and every mechanical claim, then cited eight further instances of the echo class — six survivors present since v1 (11:5, 16:9, 20:8, 28:3, 35:15, 48:9) and two regressions introduced by v4's own rewrites (41:6, 48:10), which established that crafting fresh paraphrases regenerates the defect. All eight were therefore fixed in v5 (carried forward here) **quote-first** — each corrected phrase is a verbatim, quote-marked WEB quotation, no new paraphrase was crafted, and every quotation was mechanically re-verified as a WEB substring:

1. Ps 11 "he tests the righteous" (ESV verb) → quotes 11:4–5: "his eyes examine the children of men," "the LORD examines the righteous," "his soul hates the wicked and him who loves violence."
2. Ps 16 "heart, tongue, and body rest secure" (NIV "rest secure") → quotes 16:9: "my heart is glad, and my tongue rejoices. My body shall also dwell in safety."
3. Ps 20 "they collapse, we rise and stand upright" (ESV markers) → the existing 20:7 quotation is extended verbatim through 20:8: "but we trust in the name of the LORD our God. They are bowed down and fallen, but we rise up, and stand upright."
4. Ps 28 "dragged off with the wicked" (ESV/NIV) → quotes 28:3 in full: "Don't draw me away with the wicked, with the workers of iniquity who speak peace with their neighbors, but mischief is in their hearts."
5. Ps 35 "in his stumbling they gathered" (ESV "stumbling") → quotes 35:15: "in my adversity, they rejoiced, and gathered themselves together."
6. Ps 41 "tell it when they go out" (ESV/NIV "go out"; a v4 regression) → quotes 41:6 in full: "If he comes to see me, he speaks falsehood. His heart gathers iniquity to itself. When he goes abroad, he tells it." The round-3 revision note's sentence is corrected in place.
7. Ps 48 "meditate on God's loving kindness" (NIV verb) → quotes 48:9 in full: "We have thought about your loving kindness, God, in the middle of your temple."
8. Ps 48 "his praise reaches to the ends of the earth" (NIV verb "reaches"; a v4 regression) → quotes 48:10 verbatim: "as is your name, God, so is your praise to the ends of the earth." The round-3 note's conversion list and the header's exhaustive-pass claim are corrected accordingly.

Round 4 also confirmed as safe (no change made): Ps 43:1 "vindicate him and plead his cause" — "Vindicate me" is the WEB's own opening word at 43:1.

# Round-5 revision note (quote-first corrections and tag-gap supersessions)

Round 5 verified all eight round-4 fixes verbatim (the quote-first method produced no regressions) and every mechanical claim, then cited three remaining echo-class instances — all v1 survivors that no prior round had examined. All three were fixed in v6 (carried forward here) quote-first, each quotation mechanically re-verified as a WEB substring:

1. Ps 16 "counsels him even at night" (two NIV markers) → quotes 16:7: blesses the LORD "who has given me counsel," his heart instructing him "in the night seasons" — also restoring the verse's own assignment of the night instruction to the heart.
2. Ps 17 "tested his heart by night and found nothing" (rival verb "tested"; ESV "by night") → quotes 17:3 in full: "You have proved my heart. You have visited me in the night. You have tried me, and found nothing."
3. Ps 45 "riding out victoriously for" (ESV particle and preposition) → quotes 45:4: called in his majesty to "ride on victoriously on behalf of truth, humility, and righteousness."

Round 5 also issued two tag-gap write-time advisories, applied in v6's Tag gaps section (carried forward here): the proposed `god-reigns` row is converted to an append to the landed Isaiah `god-reigns` row (whose rationale already anticipates the enthronement psalms), and the proposed `messiah-in-the-psalms` row is converted to an append to the landed Zechariah `messianic-prophecy` row (same query family, same locator design); the parenthetical alternative id `the-lords-anointed` is withdrawn because it now names a live 1 Samuel row of a different register. Motif items 8 and 13 are repointed accordingly. `zion-city-of-god`, `slander-and-false-accusation`, and `trustworthiness-of-scripture` remain collision-free proposals at check time.

# Round-6 revision note (final echo-class corrections)

Round 6 verified all of v6's round-5 fixes and both supersession appends clean, then cited the last two instances of the echo class its sweep could find — both v1-survivor ESV renderings, both fixed in this v7 quote-first, each quotation mechanically re-verified as a WEB substring; nothing else changed:

1. Ps 32 "rejoice and shout for joy, all you upright in heart" (an eight-word contiguous ESV match) → quotes 32:11 in full: "Be glad in the LORD, and rejoice, you righteous! Shout for joy, all you who are upright in heart!"
2. Ps 6 "flooding his bed with tears every night" (the ESV's construction — the WEB attaches the tears to the couch) → quotes 6:6: "Every night I flood my bed. I drench my couch with my tears."

# Originality check (CONVENTIONS §7)

Method: every chapter summary was drafted fresh from the WEB text; after drafting, a mechanical normalized 5-gram scan (lowercased, punctuation stripped) was run over this whole document against the staged full texts of the two public-domain references actually consulted — Brooks' *Summarized Bible* (Psalms section, archive.org OCR) and *Matthew Henry's Concise Commentary* (Psalms 1–50, CCEL) — with direct WEB scripture quotations excluded from the comparison.

- **biblesummary.info:** not consulted for this batch at all; no text was available to copy. Zero exposure, zero overlap.
- **BibleProject:** not consulted for this batch at all; no framing or wording drawn from it (`bibleproject-framing` accordingly appears in no Sources line). Zero exposure, zero overlap.
- **brooks-1919:** 3 shared 5-grams after the round-3 and round-4 fixes and the exhaustive pass (scan re-run on this v7): "those who trust in their" / "who trust in their wealth" — consecutive windows of the single Psalm 49:6 phrase both texts describe in the WEB's own words — and "Zion the city of God," the Psalter's own Zion motif (48:1–2). No sentence lifted, no archaic wording carried over. Two earlier shared grams have been removed by revision rather than defended: v2's "glad the city of God" was KJV word order (fixed in round 2, the Psalm 46 sentence now follows the WEB's order), and v3's "the glory due his name" — which v3 had defended as shared Bible phrasing — was itself a rival rendering's wording (KJV/ESV drop the WEB's "due *to* his name"); round 3 flagged it, and the Psalm 29 sentence now quotes the WEB verbatim, which the scan excludes as marked quotation.
- **mhcc:** 23 shared 5-grams (scan re-run on this v7), all of the same kind — unquoted scripture wording flowing through both texts ("valley of the shadow of death", "little lower than the angels", "declare the glory of God", "joy of the whole earth", "trust in the LORD and") plus generic stock ("the fear of the LORD", "the goodness of the LORD"). The longest runs are consecutive windows of single scriptural phrases, not sentences. No sentence lifted, no archaic wording carried over. (One drafting-stage overlap — a conjoined paraphrase "horrible pit and miry clay" matching MHCC's wording — was caught by this scan and replaced with the WEB's own quoted words before delivery.)

Archaism check: the draft was scanned for KJV-era forms (unto, thereof, hath, shalt, thee, thou, ye, doth, saith); none appear outside this sentence. The WEB's own renderings are used throughout (e.g., "loving kindness," "Sheol," "the LORD of Armies").

> **Assembly divider — Batch 2 of 3 (Psalms 51–100), approved draft v5, included byte-identical below.** The batch's Decisions record and revision notes are its audit trail; its Tag gaps section is superseded by the consolidated plan at the end of this document.

# Psalms 51–100 — chapter summaries with tags (batch 2 of 3, draft v5)

**Date:** 2026-08-23 · **Status:** drafting-worker output for the Psalms book thread, revised after harsh-critic round 4 (the single minor objection resolved — see `critique-51-100-round4.md`: Ps 74's ESV/NASB "meeting places" echo replaced with the quoted verbatim WEB 74:8 wording "all the places in the land where God was worshiped"). Round 3's 4 fixes (all verified genuine — see `critique-51-100-round3.md`: the Tag gaps section re-deduplicated against the shared tag-gaps.md as it stands after the Matthew–Jude blocks landed, adding five newly-owed appends to landed rows — `prosperity-of-the-wicked`, `unanswered-prayer`, `hardness-of-heart`, `leviathan-and-behemoth`, `confession-of-sin` — plus two surviving translation echoes fixed to WEB wording in Ps 68 and 76 and Decision 5's Hebrews 4:7 quote corrected to the WEB's "through David") and rounds 1–2's 13 fixes and translation-echo sweeps are carried forward from v2–v4. Nothing enters the engine or the repo. The book-level summary and Psalms 1–50 / 101–150 are other workers' batches; this file carries batch-level notes for the eventual book summary.

## How this batch was made

Every summary was drafted fresh from the WEB Bible text of Psalms 51–100, with two public-domain references consulted per chapter — Keith Brooks' *Summarized Bible* (1919) and *Matthew Henry's Concise Commentary* — and the public-domain Berean Standard Bible headings consulted for structure. BibleProject materials and biblesummary.info were not consulted for this batch at all. Nothing was copied from any source; a mechanical normalized 5-gram originality scan against Brooks and Matthew Henry (and a statement of non-consultation for the steering-only sources) is reported at the end of this file.

**WEB text provenance (per CONVENTIONS §3):** the batch text was extracted from the ebible.org engwebp verse-per-line publication declared in `pipeline/manifests/web.json` (the copy staged for this book thread, downloaded 2026-08-23). The downloaded zip's sha256 (`b6f55cc787b1…`) differs from the pinned snapshot's (`3458ca3442…`) — the known upstream drift. Verification against the pinned snapshot was therefore done through `pipeline/fixtures/web-subset.json` (generated from the pinned 2026-07-29 source): the fixture witnesses 13 of this batch's 50 chapters in full — **Psalms 51, 55, 56, 62, 65, 73, 82, 86, 88, 91, 92, 95, 100** — and every witnessed verse (198 verses) is byte-identical between the pinned fixture and the current edition. Those 13 chapters are verified against the pinned text; **the other 37 chapters are checked against the current 2026-08-23 edition only.**

## Batch-level notes

- **Book seams.** Two of the Psalter's own divisions fall inside this batch. Book II (42–72) closes at Psalm 72 with a doxology — "Praise be to the LORD God, the God of Israel, who alone does marvelous deeds… Amen and amen" (72:18–19) — and an editorial line unique in the Psalter: "This ends the prayers by David, the son of Jesse" (72:20). Book III (73–89) is dominated by Asaph (73–83) and the sons of Korah (84–85, 87–88), and closes at Psalm 89 with its own doxology, "Blessed be the LORD forever more. Amen, and Amen" (89:52). Book IV opens at Psalm 90 with the Psalter's one psalm of Moses; **the batch seam at 100/101 falls inside Book IV**, which runs to 106 — the next batch continues it mid-book.
- **Superscriptions.** As in batch 1, the WEB folds a psalm's ancient heading into verse 1; headings are treated as text and used for attribution and setting exactly as far as they state — Psalm 51 is "when Nathan the prophet came to him, after he had gone in to Bathsheba" because verse 1 says so; Psalm 72 is "By Solomon"; Psalm 90 is "A Prayer by Moses, the man of God." Psalms without any superscription (71, 91, 93, 94, 95, 96, 97, 99) and psalms whose headings name no author (66, 67, 92, 98, 100) are left anonymous even where later tradition names one.
- **No subdivisions.** No psalm in 51–100 is subdivided — see the Decisions record.
- **Imprecatory language.** This batch contains the Psalter's heaviest concentrations of prayers against enemies (55:15; 58:6–10; 59:11–13; 69:22–28; 79:6, 10–12; 83:9–17, among others). As in batch 1, summaries describe these prayers as what they are in the text — the wronged handing judgment to God rather than taking it, sometimes with a stated hope that the judged "may seek your name" (83:16) — without either sanitizing them or presenting them as a model beyond what the text supports.

---

# The fifty psalms

## Psalms 51
**Summary:** The Psalter's most famous penitential psalm, by David "when Nathan the prophet came to him, after he had gone in to Bathsheba." David asks for mercy on the ground of God's own loving kindness — blot out, wash, cleanse — and hides nothing: "I know my transgressions. My sin is constantly before me," sinned against God first and deepest, and traced back to his very conception. What he asks for goes beyond pardon to remaking: "Create in me a clean heart, O God. Renew a right spirit within me," with the joy of salvation restored and a willing spirit to uphold him. The forgiven man promises to teach transgressors God's ways, and names the offering that outweighs every bull on the altar: "The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit. O God, you will not despise a broken and contrite heart." (Paul later quotes 51:4, "so you may be proved right when you speak," in Romans 3:4 — a New Testament application.)
**Tags:** `repentance` — confession without excuses: "For I know my transgressions. My sin is constantly before me" (51:3), and "The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit" (51:17); `forgiveness-of-sins` — "blot out my transgressions… Wash me, and I will be whiter than snow" (51:1–2, 7, 9); `sin` — sin named as against God and traced to the root: "Against you, and you only, I have sinned," and "I was born in iniquity" (51:4–5); `restoration` — "Create in me a clean heart, O God… Restore to me the joy of your salvation" (51:10–12); `sharing-your-faith` — the restored man's vow: "Then I will teach transgressors your ways. Sinners will be converted to you," and "My mouth will declare your praise" (51:13–15); `confession-of-sin` — the Psalter's canonical confession: "For I know my transgressions. My sin is constantly before me. Against you, and you only, I have sinned" (51:3–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 52
**Summary:** A contemplation by David "when Doeg the Edomite came and told Saul, 'David has come to Ahimelech's house'" — a psalm addressed to a "mighty man" who boasts of mischief. His weapon is his tongue, "like a sharp razor, working deceitfully"; he loves evil more than good and lying rather than speaking the truth. David announces God's verdict — uprooted from the tent, plucked "out of the land of the living" — while the righteous watch and learn the lesson: behold the man who made riches his strength instead of God. David's own position is the opposite: "I am like a green olive tree in God's house. I trust in God's loving kindness forever and ever."
**Tags:** `honesty` — the portrait of the deceitful tongue: "Your tongue plots destruction, like a sharp razor, working deceitfully," loving "lying rather than speaking the truth" (52:2–4); `trust-in-god` — the contrast of the man who "trusted in the abundance of his riches" with "I trust in God's loving kindness forever and ever" (52:7–8); `divine-judgment` — "God will likewise destroy you forever… and root you out of the land of the living" (52:5); `thanksgiving` — "I will give you thanks forever, because you have done it" (52:9); `slander-and-false-accusation` — a psalm addressed to the informer whose weapon is his tongue: "Your tongue plots destruction, like a sharp razor, working deceitfully," loving "lying rather than speaking the truth" (52:2–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 53
**Summary:** A near-twin of Psalm 14, now set "to the tune of 'Mahalath'": the fool has said in his heart, "There is no God," and the life downstream of that verdict is corrupt and abominable. God looks down from heaven for anyone who understands and seeks him, and finds none — "There is no one who does good, no, not one." Where Psalm 14 spoke of the LORD, this Elohistic version says "God" throughout, and its middle verses differ: here God scatters the bones of the encamped enemy and puts them to shame. It ends with the same leaning toward Zion — oh that salvation would come, that God would bring back his people.
**Tags:** `sin` — the repeated universal audit: "They are corrupt… There is no one who does good, no, not one" (53:1–3). (Only one honest tag from the current vocabulary.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 54
**Summary:** A short prayer by David "when the Ziphites came and said to Saul, 'Isn't David hiding himself among us?'" Betrayed to his hunter, David asks God to save him by his name and vindicate him in his might, for strangers and violent men are after his life — men who "haven't set God before them." The center of the psalm is its turn: "Behold, God is my helper. The Lord is the one who sustains my soul." It ends ahead of the rescue, promising a freewill offering and thanks to the name that is good — "For he has delivered me out of all trouble."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Hear my prayer, God. Listen to the words of my mouth" (54:2); `gods-protection` — "Behold, God is my helper. The Lord is the one who sustains my soul" (54:4, 7); `thanksgiving` — "I will give thanks to your name, LORD, for it is good" (54:6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 55
**Summary:** A lament by David under a double weight: a violent, lying city — "Day and night they prowl around on its walls" — and, far worse, treachery from inside. The betrayer was no enemy, "but it was you, a man like me, my companion, and my familiar friend," a man whose mouth was smooth as butter while his heart was war. David's first wish is escape — "Oh that I had wings like a dove! Then I would fly away, and be at rest" — but where he actually lands is prayer: evening, morning, and at noon he will cry out, and the LORD will hear. Out of that comes the psalm's gift to every burdened reader: "Cast your burden on the LORD and he will sustain you." He also asks God to bring the treacherous down to the pit — judgment handed over, not taken — and closes, "but I will trust in you."
**Tags:** `pastoral-betrayal-and-marriage-crisis` — betrayal by an intimate: "it was you, a man like me, my companion, and my familiar friend. We took sweet fellowship together" (55:12–14, 20–21); `prayer` — "Evening, morning, and at noon, I will cry out in distress. He will hear my voice" (55:1–2, 16–17); `trust-in-god` — "Cast your burden on the LORD and he will sustain you… but I will trust in you" (55:22–23); `lament` — "I am restless in my complaint, and moan" — terror, trembling, and the wish for a dove's wings to fly from it all (55:1–8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 56
**Summary:** A poem by David "when the Philistines seized him in Gath" — pressed all day by men who want to swallow him up, twist his words, and watch his steps. Its heartbeat is a refrain of fear mastered: "When I am afraid, I will put my trust in you… I will not be afraid. What can flesh do to me?" In between, one of Scripture's tenderest pictures of God's attention: "You count my wanderings. You put my tears into your container. Aren't they in your book?" David knows one thing for certain — "that God is for me" — and ends bound by his vows, walking before God in the light of the living, his soul delivered from death.
**Tags:** `trust-in-god` — the refrain: "When I am afraid, I will put my trust in you," and "I have put my trust in God" (56:3–4, 11); `fear-not` — "I will not be afraid. What can man do to me?" (56:4, 11); `pastoral-god-sees-my-suffering` — "You count my wanderings. You put my tears into your container. Aren't they in your book?" (56:8); `thanksgiving` — "Your vows are on me, God. I will give thank offerings to you" (56:12–13); `slander-and-false-accusation` — the day-long verbal siege: "All day long they twist my words. All their thoughts are against me for evil" (56:5–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 57
**Summary:** By David "when he fled from Saul, in the cave": be merciful, be merciful, "for my soul takes refuge in you. Yes, in the shadow of your wings, I will take refuge, until disaster has passed." He lies among lions — men whose teeth are spears and whose tongue is a sharp sword — yet the psalm keeps rising out of the cave to its refrain: "Be exalted, God, above the heavens! Let your glory be above all the earth!" The net his enemies prepared catches them instead. Then the mood breaks open into music: "My heart is steadfast, God… I will wake up the dawn," singing among the nations of a loving kindness that reaches to the heavens.
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — "in the shadow of your wings, I will take refuge, until disaster has passed" (57:1); `praise` — "My heart is steadfast, God. My heart is steadfast. I will sing, yes, I will sing praises," and the refrain "Be exalted, God, above the heavens!" (57:5, 7–11); `gods-love` — "your great loving kindness reaches to the heavens, and your truth to the skies" (57:3, 10)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 58
**Summary:** An imprecatory poem by David that opens with a cross-examination of unjust rulers: "Do you indeed speak righteousness, silent ones?" — no, in the heart they plot injustice and weigh out violence. The wicked are drawn as venomous from birth, "wayward as soon as they are born, speaking lies," deaf as a cobra to every charmer. Then come the curses: break their teeth, let them vanish like water, like a snail that melts, like a stillborn child — prayers for the unmaking of the violent, asked of God rather than carried out by hand. The end the psalm wants is public and theological: the righteous vindicated, "so that men shall say… 'Most certainly there is a God who judges the earth.'"
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — the psalm's goal-line: "Most certainly there is a reward for the righteous. Most certainly there is a God who judges the earth" (58:9–11); `sin` — injustice from the heart outward: "in your heart you plot injustice," and "The wicked go astray from the womb" (58:2–5); `vengeance` — the imprecations — teeth broken, melting away like water — are prayers for the unmaking of the violent, asked of God rather than carried out by hand, aimed at the public verdict "Most certainly there is a God who judges the earth" (58:6–11). (The imprecations of 58:6–8 are described in the summary per the batch policy; the 2026-08-25 `vengeance` addition sits inside that same frame.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 59
**Summary:** By David "when Saul sent, and they watched the house to kill him." He asks deliverance from bloodthirsty men who return each evening "howling like dogs" and prowl the city, swords in their lips — and he insists on his innocence: "not for my disobedience, nor for my sin, LORD… I have done no wrong, yet they are ready to attack me." Over against their snarling stands a watchman's confidence: "Oh, my Strength, I watch for you, for God is my high tower." He asks God to scatter rather than instantly kill them, "or my people may forget," and to let their own cursing lips trap them. The psalm resolves into morning music: "I will sing aloud of your loving kindness in the morning. For you have been my high tower, a refuge in the day of my distress."
**Tags:** `gods-protection` — "God is my high tower… a refuge in the day of my distress" (59:9, 16–17); `prayer` — "Deliver me from my enemies, my God. Set me on high from those who rise up against me" (59:1–2); `praise` — "But I will sing of your strength. Yes, I will sing aloud of your loving kindness in the morning" (59:16–17); `vengeance` — judgment handed to God with its reason attached: scatter rather than instantly kill them, "or my people may forget," and let their own cursing lips trap them (59:11–13)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 60
**Summary:** A national psalm by David from the Edomite wars, sung after a setback: "God, you have rejected us. You have broken us down… Restore us, again." The land itself quakes with the defeat, and God has made his people "drink the wine that makes us stagger" — yet he has also "given a banner to those who fear you." At the center God speaks from his sanctuary, parceling out the land as its owner — Shechem, Succoth, Gilead, Manasseh, Ephraim, Judah — with Moab his wash basin and Edom under his sandal. The psalm ends where a chastened army must: "Give us help against the adversary, for the help of man is vain. Through God we will do valiantly."
**Tags:** `prayer` — a community praying through defeat: "Restore us, again," and "save with your right hand, and answer us" (60:1, 5, 11); `restoration` — "God, you have rejected us… Restore us, again," and "Mend its fractures, for it quakes" (60:1–2); `trust-in-god` — "the help of man is vain. Through God we will do valiantly" (60:11–12); `lament` — a national lament after a setback: "God, you have rejected us. You have broken us down," the land quaking and the people made to "drink the wine that makes us stagger" (60:1–3)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 61
**Summary:** A prayer by David from the far edge of things: "From the end of the earth, I will call to you when my heart is overwhelmed. Lead me to the rock that is higher than I." God has been a refuge and "a strong tower from the enemy," so David asks to dwell in God's tent forever, sheltered under his wings. He remembers that God has heard his vows and given him the heritage of those who fear his name, then prays for the king's life to be prolonged, enthroned in God's presence under loving kindness and truth. The end is a singer's resolve: praise forever, vows fulfilled daily.
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — "Lead me to the rock that is higher than I. For you have been a refuge for me, a strong tower from the enemy" (61:2–4); `prayer` — "Hear my cry, God. Listen to my prayer" prayed from an overwhelmed heart (61:1–2); `oaths-and-vows` — vows heard and kept frame the psalm: God has heard his vows and given him the heritage of those who fear his name, and the singer will fulfill his vows daily (61:5, 8). (The vows of 61:5, 8 are logged with the oaths-and-vows gap refs. 2026-08-25: that row's concept was adopted as a display tag and is applied above.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 62
**Summary:** A psalm by David built on the word "alone": "My soul rests in God alone. My salvation is from him. He alone is my rock, my salvation, and my fortress." Assaulted by men who would topple him like a leaning wall — blessing with their mouths while cursing inwardly — he steadies himself with self-address: "My soul, wait in silence for God alone." Then he turns teacher: "Trust in him at all times, you people. Pour out your heart before him"; men high and low together weigh lighter than a breath, so don't trust oppression or robbery, and "If riches increase, don't set your heart on them." The close gathers what God has spoken: power belongs to God, loving kindness belongs to the Lord, and he rewards every man according to his work.
**Tags:** `trust-in-god` — "Trust in him at all times, you people. Pour out your heart before him. God is a refuge for us" (62:8); `refuge-in-trouble` — "He alone is my rock, my salvation, and my fortress. I will never be greatly shaken" (62:2, 6–7); `hope-in-god` — "My soul, wait in silence for God alone, for my expectation is from him" (62:1, 5); `contentment` — "men of low degree are just a breath… If riches increase, don't set your heart on them" (62:9–10)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 63
**Summary:** A psalm by David "when he was in the desert of Judah," where the landscape becomes the prayer: "My soul thirsts for you. My flesh longs for you, in a dry and weary land, where there is no water." What he thirsts for is what he once saw in the sanctuary — God's power and glory — and the verdict that drives the whole psalm is "your loving kindness is better than life." So the parched man is somehow feasting: satisfied as with the richest food, remembering God on his bed through the night watches, rejoicing in the shadow of his wings, held up by God's right hand. The enemies seeking his soul will go down to the sword and the jackals, "but the king shall rejoice in God."
**Tags:** `hunger-for-god` — "My soul thirsts for you. My flesh longs for you, in a dry and weary land" (63:1); `gods-love` — "your loving kindness is better than life" (63:3); `joy-in-the-lord` — "My soul shall be satisfied as with the richest food… I will rejoice in the shadow of your wings" (63:5, 7, 11); `praise` — "my lips shall praise you. So I will bless you while I live. I will lift up my hands in your name" (63:3–5)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 64
**Summary:** A psalm by David against the conspiracy of the wicked, whose weapons are all verbal: they "sharpen their tongue like a sword, and aim their arrows, deadly words," shooting at the innocent from ambush and encouraging each other with "Who will see them?" The answer is the psalm's pivot — the archers are out-shot: "But God will shoot at them. They will be suddenly struck down with an arrow," and "Their own tongues shall ruin them." The spectacle teaches the watching world; all mankind declares the work of God and ponders what he has done. The righteous end where they began — glad in the LORD, taking refuge in him.
**Tags:** `gods-protection` — "Hide me from the conspiracy of the wicked," answered in God's sudden reversal (64:1–2, 7); `divine-judgment` — "But God will shoot at them… Their own tongues shall ruin them," so that "All mankind shall be afraid. They shall declare the work of God" (64:7–9); `slander-and-false-accusation` — a conspiracy whose weapons are words: they "sharpen their tongue like a sword, and aim their arrows, deadly words," shooting from ambush at the innocent (64:3–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 65
**Summary:** A psalm and song by David that moves outward in three widening rings. In Zion, praise waits for the God who hears prayer, atones for overwhelming sins, and satisfies the chosen with the goodness of his house. Beyond the temple, he is "the hope of all the ends of the earth," forming mountains by his power and stilling the roaring of the seas and the turmoil of the nations, so that the world's far edges fear his wonders. Then the psalm walks into the farmland: God visits the earth and waters it, drenches its furrows, and crowns the year with his bounty — carts overflowing, hills clothed with gladness, valleys covered in grain, until the landscape itself shouts for joy and sings.
**Tags:** `gods-provision` — "You visit the earth, and water it… You crown the year with your bounty. Your carts overflow with abundance" (65:9–13); `prayer` — "You who hear prayer, all men will come to you" (65:2); `forgiveness-of-sins` — "Sins overwhelmed me, but you atoned for our transgressions" (65:3); `praise` — "Praise waits for you, God, in Zion" (65:1, 8); `creation` — "By your power, you form the mountains… You still the roaring of the seas" (65:6–7)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 66
**Summary:** An anonymous song that starts at maximum width — "Make a joyful shout to God, all the earth!" — and narrows step by step to one man's testimony. All the earth is summoned to see God's awesome deeds, with the exodus as the standing exhibit: "He turned the sea into dry land." Then the "we" of Israel testifies to a harder mercy: "For you, God, have tested us. You have refined us, as silver is refined," through prison, burdens, fire, and water — "but you brought us to the place of abundance." Finally a single worshiper steps forward with vows to pay and a story to tell: "Come and hear, all you who fear God. I will declare what he has done for my soul" — adding the sober condition that cherished sin would have closed heaven's ear, "But most certainly, God has listened."
**Tags:** `praise` — "Make a joyful shout to God, all the earth! Sing to the glory of his name!" (66:1–4, 8); `testing` — "For you, God, have tested us. You have refined us, as silver is refined… but you brought us to the place of abundance" (66:10–12); `prayer` — heard prayer with its honest condition: "If I cherished sin in my heart, the Lord wouldn't have listened. But most certainly, God has listened" (66:17–20); `sharing-your-faith` — "Come and hear, all you who fear God. I will declare what he has done for my soul" (66:16); `worship` — "I will come into your temple with burnt offerings. I will pay my vows to you" (66:13–15); `oaths-and-vows` — trouble-vows paid in the temple: "I will pay my vows to you," the vows his lips uttered in distress (66:13–14)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 67
**Summary:** A short anonymous psalm that turns the priestly blessing outward: "May God be merciful to us, bless us, and cause his face to shine on us" — and immediately says why — "That your way may be known on earth, and your salvation among all nations." Its refrain wants a planet-wide choir: "Let the peoples praise you, God. Let all the peoples praise you," with the nations glad because God judges the peoples with equity and governs them. The harvest already in ("The earth has yielded its increase") is read as a down payment: "God will bless us. All the ends of the earth shall fear him." Israel is blessed in order to be a blessing — the psalm prays the logic of the promise to Abraham.
**Tags:** `blessing` — blessing asked and expected, with its outward purpose: "May God be merciful to us, bless us… God will bless us. All the ends of the earth shall fear him" (67:1, 6–7); `nations-and-peoples` — "Let all the peoples praise you… you will judge the peoples with equity, and govern the nations on earth" (67:2–5); `praise` — the doubled refrain, "Let the peoples praise you, God. Let all the peoples praise you" (67:3, 5)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 68
**Summary:** A processional psalm by David on the grandest scale: "Let God arise! Let his enemies be scattered!" — wicked melting like wax, while the righteous rejoice. This mighty God stoops: he is "a father of the fatherless, and a defender of the widows," he "sets the lonely in families" and brings out prisoners with singing. The psalm then marches through Israel's story — Sinai trembling, rain on a weary inheritance, kings fleeing, the LORD ascending the mountain he chose with thousands of chariots, leading away captives (words Ephesians 4:8 later applies to Christ's ascension — a New Testament application). The procession arrives — singers, minstrels, tambourines, the tribes in order — and widens to the horizon: kings bringing presents to Jerusalem, Egypt and Ethiopia stretching out hands to God, all kingdoms summoned to sing to him "who rides on the heaven of heavens, which are of old." Along the way comes one of the Psalter's most portable blessings: "Blessed be the Lord, who daily bears our burdens."
**Tags:** `praise` — "Sing to God! Sing praises to his name!… Sing to God, you kingdoms of the earth!" (68:4, 32–35); `loneliness` — "God sets the lonely in families. He brings out the prisoners with singing" (68:6); `gods-provision` — "You, God, sent a plentiful rain… You, God, prepared your goodness for the poor," and the Lord "who daily bears our burdens" (68:9–10, 19); `worship` — "They have seen your processions, God… my King, into the sanctuary," with singers, minstrels, and the assembly blessing God (68:24–26); `justice-and-oppression` — the mighty God who stoops for the defenseless: "a father of the fatherless, and a defender of the widows," who "brings out the prisoners with singing" (68:5–6, 10); `zion-city-of-god` — the mountain God chose for his dwelling and the procession into it, with kings bringing presents to Jerusalem (68:16–18, 24, 29); `messianic-prophecy` — the LORD's ascent leading away captives (68:18), the words Ephesians 4:8 later applies to Christ's ascension — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 69
**Summary:** One of the Psalter's deepest laments, by David: "Save me, God, for the waters have come up to my neck!" — sinking in mire, weary with crying, hated without cause by enemies beyond counting, forced to restore what he did not take. He owns his folly before God, yet the suffering here is for God's sake: "the zeal of your house consumes me," and the reproaches aimed at God have landed on him — he is a stranger to his own brothers, the song of the drunkards, given poison for food and vinegar for his thirst, with no comforter found. He prays on — "my prayer is to you, LORD, in an acceptable time" — and asks God to bring on his persecutors the judgment they have earned, even blotting from the book of life. The psalm ends singing: praise pleases the LORD better than an ox, "For the LORD hears the needy," and God will save Zion. (The New Testament quotes this psalm repeatedly of Jesus — his zeal for the temple, John 2:17; hatred without cause, John 15:25; bearing reproach, Romans 15:3; the vinegar, Matthew 27:34, 48; the deserted habitation applied to Judas, Acts 1:20; the darkened eyes, Romans 11:9–10 — applications the reader should know, alongside what the text itself is: a righteous sufferer's lament.)
**Tags:** `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — from "the waters have come up to my neck" and "I looked for some to take pity, but there was none" through to "I will praise the name of God with a song" (69:1–3, 20, 29–30); `prayer` — "But as for me, my prayer is to you, LORD, in an acceptable time… Answer me speedily!" (69:13–18); `pastoral-god-sees-my-suffering` — "You know my reproach, my shame, and my dishonor," and "the LORD hears the needy" (69:19, 33); `thanksgiving` — "I will praise the name of God with a song, and will magnify him with thanksgiving" (69:30–31); `lament` — "Save me, God, for the waters have come up to my neck!" — sinking, weary with crying, and no comforter found (69:1–3, 20); `vengeance` — the psalm asks God to bring on his persecutors the judgment they have earned, even blotting from the book of life — repayment handed to the Judge, not taken (69:22–28); `messianic-prophecy` — the psalm the New Testament quotes repeatedly of Jesus: "the zeal of your house consumes me" (John 2:17), hatred without cause (John 15:25), the vinegar (Matthew 27:34, 48), the deserted habitation (Acts 1:20) — reported as later applications, exactly as the summary signposts (69:4, 9, 21, 25)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 70
**Summary:** By David, "a reminder" — a five-verse alarm bell, nearly identical to the closing verses of Psalm 40 (40:13–17): "Hurry, God, to deliver me. Come quickly to help me, LORD." Those who seek his life should be turned back in disgrace, and the mockers who say "Aha! Aha!" turned around by their own shame. Opposite them, a different company: "Let all those who seek you rejoice and be glad in you," continually saying, "Let God be exalted!" The psalmist locates himself without pretense — "But I am poor and needy… You are my help and my deliverer. LORD, don't delay."
**Tags:** `prayer` — the whole psalm is urgent petition: "Hurry, God, to deliver me. Come quickly to help me, LORD… don't delay" (70:1, 5); `joy-in-the-lord` — "Let all those who seek you rejoice and be glad in you" (70:4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 71
**Summary:** An anonymous prayer from old age, looking back over a whole lifetime of leaning on God: "For you are my hope, Lord GOD, my confidence from my youth. I have relied on you from the womb." The petition matches the season: "Don't reject me in my old age. Don't forsake me when my strength fails," while enemies read his weakness as abandonment — "God has forsaken him. Pursue and take him." He asks God to be "a rock of refuge to which I may always go," and stakes out his remaining assignment: "even when I am old and gray-haired, God, don't forsake me, until I have declared your strength to the next generation." Trouble is not denied — "You, who have shown us many and bitter troubles, you will let me live" — and the psalm ends with harp and lyre, lips shouting for joy, a tongue talking of righteousness all day long.
**Tags:** `hope-in-god` — "For you are my hope, Lord GOD, my confidence from my youth," and "But I will always hope" (71:5, 14); `refuge-in-trouble` — "In you, LORD, I take refuge… Be to me a rock of refuge to which I may always go" (71:1, 3); `praise` — "My mouth shall be filled with your praise, with your honor all day long" (71:8, 22–24); `gods-faithfulness` — "I will also praise you with the harp for your faithfulness, my God," from the womb to gray hair (71:6, 17–18, 22); `sharing-your-faith` — testimony as the old man's calling: "until I have declared your strength to the next generation, your might to everyone who is to come" (71:15–18); `aging-and-old-age` — the aging believer's own petition: "Don't reject me in my old age. Don't forsake me when my strength fails," and "even when I am old and gray-haired, God, don't forsake me" (71:9, 17–18); `slander-and-false-accusation` — enemies who read his weakness as abandonment and say of him, "God has forsaken him. Pursue and take him" (71:10–11, 13)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 72
**Summary:** The one psalm headed "By Solomon" — a royal prayer that closes Book II. It asks God to give the king his own justice, so that the king judges the poor rightly, saves the children of the needy, and breaks the oppressor; under such a reign the righteous flourish and peace abounds "until the moon is no more." The vision keeps expanding: dominion from sea to sea, kings of Tarshish and Sheba bringing tribute, all nations serving him — and the reason given is not conquest but compassion: "For he will deliver the needy when he cries; the poor, who has no helper." Abundant grain fills the land, its fruit swaying like Lebanon; his name endures like the sun, and "All nations will call him blessed" — the Abraham promise in royal dress. (Christian readers have long heard in this ideal king the pattern of Christ's kingdom — a reading beyond the psalm's own text, which prays for Israel's king.) Book II then closes with a doxology — "Let the whole earth be filled with his glory! Amen and amen" — and the editorial seal: "This ends the prayers by David, the son of Jesse."
**Tags:** `praise` — the closing doxology: "Praise be to the LORD God, the God of Israel, who alone does marvelous deeds… Blessed be his glorious name forever!" (72:18–19); `blessing` — "Men shall be blessed by him. All nations will call him blessed" (72:17); `nations-and-peoples` — "all kings shall fall down before him. All nations shall serve him," from Tarshish to Sheba (72:8–11, 17); `justice-and-oppression` — the royal prayer's core: a king who judges the poor rightly and breaks the oppressor, "For he will deliver the needy when he cries; the poor, who has no helper" (72:2, 4, 12–14); `messianic-prophecy` — the traditionally messianic royal psalm (no direct New Testament quotation): Christian readers have long heard in this ideal king the pattern of Christ's kingdom — reported as a reading beyond the psalm's own text, which prays for Israel's king, exactly as the summary signposts (72:8–11, 17). (The king's justice for the poor and needy, 72:2, 4, 12–14, is national-scale material logged with the justice-and-oppression gap refs per the project ruling. 2026-08-25: that row's concept was adopted as a display tag and is applied above.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 73
**Summary:** Book III opens with Asaph's confession of a near-fall: "Surely God is good to Israel" — but "my feet were almost gone… For I was envious of the arrogant, when I saw the prosperity of the wicked." He draws their portrait — no struggles, firm strength, free from burdens, scoffing, asking "How does God know?" — until serving God seemed pointless: "Surely I have cleansed my heart in vain." The turning point is a doorway: "until I entered God's sanctuary, and considered their latter end" — set in slippery places, swept away like a dream on waking. What remains is the psalm's treasure, and one of the Psalter's summits: "Nevertheless, I am continually with you. You have held my right hand… Whom do I have in heaven? There is no one on earth whom I desire besides you." Flesh and heart fail, "but God is the strength of my heart and my portion forever."
**Tags:** `envy-and-jealousy` — "For I was envious of the arrogant, when I saw the prosperity of the wicked," envy owned later as senseless and brutish (73:2–3, 21–22); `presence-of-god` — "Nevertheless, I am continually with you. You have held my right hand," and "it is good for me to come close to God" (73:23, 28); `hunger-for-god` — "Whom do I have in heaven? There is no one on earth whom I desire besides you" (73:25–26); `divine-judgment` — the sanctuary's answer: "Surely you set them in slippery places. You throw them down to destruction" (73:17–20, 27); `guidance` — "You will guide me with your counsel, and afterward receive me to glory" (73:24); `prosperity-of-the-wicked` — the Psalter's full-length wrestling with the question: "For I was envious of the arrogant, when I saw the prosperity of the wicked" — always at ease, increasing in riches — resolved only in the sanctuary (73:3–12, 17)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 74
**Summary:** A communal lament by Asaph over a destroyed sanctuary: "God, why have you rejected us forever? Why does your anger smolder against the sheep of your pasture?" The wreckage is described axe-stroke by axe-stroke — carved work smashed, the sanctuary burned to the ground, "all the places in the land where God was worshiped" burned up — and the deeper famine named: "There is no longer any prophet, neither is there among us anyone who knows how long." Against the ruin the community sets who God is: "Yet God is my King of old," who divided the sea, broke Leviathan, owns day and night, and fixed the boundaries and seasons of the earth. So the psalm dares its petitions: remember your congregation, "Honor your covenant," don't forget your poor, "Arise, God! Plead your own cause."
**Tags:** `prayer` — corporate protest carried to God: "Why have you rejected us forever?… Arise, God! Plead your own cause" (74:1, 18–23); `covenant` — "Remember your congregation, which you purchased of old," and "Honor your covenant" (74:2, 20); `creation` — "The day is yours, the night is also yours… You have set all the boundaries of the earth. You have made summer and winter" (74:16–17); `lament` — a communal lament over a destroyed sanctuary: "God, why have you rejected us forever? Why does your anger smolder against the sheep of your pasture?" — with no prophet left and no one who knows how long (74:1, 9–11); `leviathan-and-behemoth` — the named-figure text itself: "You broke the heads of Leviathan in pieces" — God's primeval victory recited against the ruin (74:13–14)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 75
**Summary:** A psalm by Asaph in which thanksgiving frames a courtroom announcement. The congregation gives thanks that God's Name is near — and then God himself speaks: "When I choose the appointed time, I will judge blamelessly"; when the earth quakes, "I firmly hold its pillars." The arrogant are told to stop lifting the horn and stiffening the neck, because "neither from the east, nor from the west, nor yet from the south, comes exaltation" — "But God is the judge. He puts down one, and lifts up another." In the LORD's hand is a cup of foaming spiced wine that the wicked of the earth must drink to the dregs; the psalmist, meanwhile, will sing to the God of Jacob, under whom every wicked horn is cut off and "the horns of the righteous shall be lifted up."
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — "When I choose the appointed time, I will judge blamelessly," and the cup drained to its dregs (75:2, 7–8); `humble-exaltation` — "Don't lift up your horn on high… But God is the judge. He puts down one, and lifts up another" (75:4–7, 10); `thanksgiving` — "We give thanks to you, God. We give thanks, for your Name is near" (75:1)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 76
**Summary:** A psalm by Asaph celebrating God's victory at his own doorstep: "In Judah, God is known. His name is great in Israel," his dwelling in Zion — and "There he broke the flaming arrows of the bow, the shield, and the sword." The invading valiant "have slept their last sleep"; at the rebuke of the God of Jacob, both chariot and horse are "cast into a dead sleep." From that field the psalm draws its awe: "You, even you, are to be feared. Who can stand in your sight when you are angry?" — for God's judgment from heaven falls "to save all the afflicted ones of the earth," and even "the wrath of man praises you." The fitting response is to make vows to this God and keep them, before the King whom the kings of the earth fear.
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — "You pronounced judgment from heaven. The earth feared, and was silent, when God arose to judgment" (76:7–9, 12); `gods-protection` — Zion's defense told in past tense: "There he broke the flaming arrows of the bow, the shield, and the sword" (76:3–6); `zion-city-of-god` — the victory is at God's own address: "In Judah, God is known. His name is great in Israel," his dwelling place in Zion (76:1–3); `oaths-and-vows` — the response the field of victory demands: "Make vows to the LORD your God, and fulfill them!" (76:11)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 77
**Summary:** Asaph in the sleepless dark: "In the day of my trouble I sought the Lord… My soul refused to be comforted." Remembering God only makes him groan, and the night fills with terrible questions: "Will the Lord reject us forever?… Has his loving kindness vanished forever?… Has God forgotten to be gracious?" The turn is a deliberate act of memory: "I will remember the LORD's deeds; for I will remember your wonders of old" — and the psalm walks into the exodus, where the waters saw God and writhed, thunder rolled, and "Your way was through the sea… Your footsteps were not known." It ends without announcing that the feelings changed, only that the ground has: "You led your people like a flock, by the hand of Moses and Aaron."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "My cry goes to God!… In the day of my trouble I sought the Lord" through a night of stretched-out hands (77:1–2); `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — the questions of a comfortless soul ("Has God forgotten to be gracious?") answered by deliberate remembering: "I will remember the LORD's deeds" (77:2–11); `gods-faithfulness` — the case built from history: "You are the God who does wonders… You have redeemed your people with your arm" (77:11–15, 20); `lament` — a comfortless night of complaint: "Will the Lord reject us forever?… Has his loving kindness vanished forever?… Has God forgotten to be gracious?" (77:1–9)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 78
**Summary:** The Psalter's great history lesson, a contemplation by Asaph: "I will open my mouth in a parable. I will utter dark sayings of old" (quoted of Jesus' parables in Matthew 13:35 — a New Testament application). The opening states the method: each generation must tell the next "the praises of the LORD… that they might set their hope in God, and not forget God's deeds," and not repeat the fathers' stubbornness. Then the long story runs: the sea split, water from rocks, manna — "the bread of angels" — and quail; and against it all, relentless unbelief: "Can God prepare a table in the wilderness?"; craving, lying flattery, covenant-breaking, and tested patience, met again and again by a God who, "being merciful, forgave iniquity, and didn't destroy them," remembering "that they were but flesh." The plagues on Egypt are retold, the settlement in Canaan, the treachery at the high places, Shiloh abandoned and God's strength "into captivity" — until God wakes "as one out of sleep," rejects Ephraim, chooses Judah, Zion, and David, taking him "from the sheepfolds" to shepherd Jacob "according to the integrity of his heart."
**Tags:** `parenting` — the charge to tell the children: "telling to the generation to come the praises of the LORD… that they should make them known to their children" (78:4–8); `covenant` — "he established a covenant in Jacob," broken by the fathers who "didn't keep God's covenant" (78:5, 10, 37); `sin` — the anatomy of a stubborn and rebellious generation: "How often they rebelled against him in the wilderness" (78:8, 17–20, 32, 40–42, 56–58); `gods-provision` — "He rained down manna on them to eat… Man ate the bread of angels," with water from the rocks and meat like dust (78:15–16, 23–29); `gods-faithfulness` — "But he, being merciful, forgave iniquity, and didn't destroy them," and led his people like a flock (78:38–39, 52–55); `divine-judgment` — "the LORD heard, and was angry. A fire was kindled against Jacob" — the plagues of Egypt and the wrath that fell on unbelief, to Shiloh's abandonment (78:21, 31, 44–51, 59–64); `leadership` — the psalm's destination: David taken "from the sheepfolds" to shepherd Jacob "according to the integrity of his heart" (78:70–72); `messianic-prophecy` — "I will open my mouth in a parable. I will utter dark sayings of old" (78:2), quoted of Jesus' parables in Matthew 13:35 — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 79
**Summary:** A communal lament by Asaph from the smoking ruins: "God, the nations have come into your inheritance. They have defiled your holy temple. They have laid Jerusalem in heaps," with the bodies of God's servants left as food for birds and beasts and no one to bury them. The community has become a scoffing to its neighbors, and the questions burn: "How long, LORD? Will you be angry forever?" The psalm asks God to deal with the nations who devoured Jacob — "Why should the nations say, 'Where is their God?'" — and to let the prisoner's sighing and the sentence of death come before him. But its center of gravity is confession: "Don't hold the iniquities of our forefathers against us… Deliver us, and forgive our sins, for your name's sake." It ends with the flock promising praise: "So we, your people and sheep of your pasture, will give you thanks forever."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Help us, God of our salvation, for the glory of your name," prayed from national ruin (79:5, 9–11); `forgiveness-of-sins` — "Don't hold the iniquities of our forefathers against us… Deliver us, and forgive our sins, for your name's sake" (79:8–9); `thanksgiving` — "we, your people and sheep of your pasture, will give you thanks forever" (79:13); `lament` — a communal lament from the smoking ruins: "How long, LORD? Will you be angry forever?" prayed over a defiled temple and unburied servants (79:1–5); `vengeance` — the pay-back petitions ask the Judge to answer the nations' taunt — "Why should the nations say, 'Where is their God?'" — repayment left with God (79:6, 10, 12). (The pay-back petitions of 79:6, 10, 12 are described per the batch's imprecatory policy; the 2026-08-25 `vengeance` addition sits inside that same frame.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 80
**Summary:** A communal lament by Asaph addressed to the "Shepherd of Israel, you who lead Joseph like a flock, you who sit above the cherubim" — with a refrain that returns three times, each time with a fuller divine name: "Turn us again, God. Cause your face to shine, and we will be saved." The people eat "the bread of tears" while God seems angry at their very prayers and neighbors laugh. The psalm's centerpiece is a parable told back to its planter: "You brought a vine out of Egypt" — cleared ground, deep roots, boughs like cedars to the sea and the River — "Why have you broken down its walls?" Now the boar ravages it and it burns. The final petitions ask God to visit his vine and to lay his hand "on the man of your right hand, on the son of man whom you made strong for yourself," with a vow attached: "Revive us, and we will call on your name."
**Tags:** `restoration` — the rising refrain: "Turn us again, God. Cause your face to shine, and we will be saved," and "Revive us, and we will call on your name" (80:3, 7, 14, 18–19); `prayer` — "Hear us, Shepherd of Israel… how long will you be angry against the prayer of your people?" (80:1, 4); `lament` — a communal lament under the Shepherd's anger: the people eat "the bread of tears" while neighbors laugh, and the vine parable ends "Why have you broken down its walls?" (80:4–6, 12); `unanswered-prayer` — prayer itself seemingly met by anger: "how long will you be angry against the prayer of your people?" (80:4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 81
**Summary:** By Asaph, a festival psalm that changes voice halfway through. It opens with the orchestra: "Sing aloud to God, our strength!" — tambourine, lyre, harp, and the trumpet blown at the new moon and full moon, a statute from the exodus itself. Then God takes the microphone and remembers: "I removed his shoulder from the burden… You called in trouble, and I delivered you," and repeats Sinai's first demand — no strange god, no foreign god, "I am the LORD, your God, who brought you up out of the land of Egypt. Open your mouth wide, and I will fill it." The grief follows: "But my people didn't listen to my voice… So I let them go after the stubbornness of their hearts." The psalm ends with God still longing: "Oh that my people would listen to me" — enemies would be subdued, and Israel fed "with the finest of the wheat" and honey out of the rock.
**Tags:** `worship` — "Sing aloud to God, our strength!… Blow the trumpet at the New Moon," worship kept as an appointed statute (81:1–5); `obedience-to-the-word` — "Oh that my people would listen to me, that Israel would walk in my ways!" against "But my people didn't listen to my voice" (81:8, 11–13); `gods-provision` — "Open your mouth wide, and I will fill it," and the promised "finest of the wheat" and "honey out of the rock" (81:10, 16); `idolatry` — Sinai's first demand repeated in the festival: "There shall be no strange god in you" — no worship of a foreign god (81:9); `hardness-of-heart` — "But my people didn't listen to my voice… So I let them go after the stubbornness of their hearts" (81:11–13)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 82
**Summary:** A courtroom psalm by Asaph, one scene long: "God presides in the great assembly. He judges among the gods" — and the ones on trial are the powerful who judge. The indictment: "How long will you judge unjustly, and show partiality to the wicked?" The standing orders they ignored: "Defend the weak, the poor, and the fatherless… Rescue the weak and needy. Deliver them out of the hand of the wicked." Because they walk on in darkness while the earth's foundations shake, the sentence falls on the "gods": "You are gods, all of you are sons of the Most High. Nevertheless you shall die like men" (Jesus quotes this verse in John 10:34 — a New Testament application). The psalm ends by appealing past every failed bench: "Arise, God, judge the earth, for you inherit all of the nations."
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — "God presides in the great assembly. He judges among the gods," and the closing plea, "Arise, God, judge the earth" (82:1, 6–8); `justice-and-oppression` — the bench's ignored standing orders: "Defend the weak, the poor, and the fatherless… Rescue the weak and needy. Deliver them out of the hand of the wicked" (82:2–4); `messianic-prophecy` — "You are gods, all of you are sons of the Most High" (82:6), the verse Jesus quotes in John 10:34 — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost. (`divine-judgment` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary; the defend-the-poor charge, logged then to the justice-and-oppression gap row per the project ruling, is carried by that concept — adopted 2026-08-25 — above, alongside the John 10:34 locator.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 83
**Summary:** The last Asaph psalm is a national emergency prayer: "God, don't keep silent" — for a ten-nation confederacy (Edom, the Ishmaelites, Moab, the Hagrites, Gebal, Ammon, Amalek, Philistia, Tyre, with Assyria lending its arm) has one mind and one aim: "let's destroy them as a nation, that the name of Israel may be remembered no more." The psalm asks God to fight as he fought before — "Do to them as you did to Midian, as to Sisera" — naming Gideon's and Deborah's old victories, and prays the enemies become tumbleweed and chaff before God's storm. Even its severity carries a missionary edge: "Fill their faces with confusion, that they may seek your name, LORD." The end states the goal of it all: "that they may know that you alone, whose name is the LORD, are the Most High over all the earth."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "God, don't keep silent. Don't keep silent, and don't be still, God," prayed against a conspiracy "against your cherished ones" (83:1–5); `divine-judgment` — judgment asked from history's Judge — "Do to them as you did to Midian" — with its stated end, "that they may know that you alone… are the Most High over all the earth" (83:9–18); `vengeance` — "Do to them as you did to Midian" — the storm-and-chaff petitions hand the confederacy to history's Judge, with even their confusion aimed at "that they may seek your name, LORD" (83:9–17). (The imprecations are described per the batch policy; note the repentance-shaped clause at 83:16, which the 2026-08-25 `vengeance` addition keeps visible.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 84
**Summary:** The sons of Korah return with the Psalter's great pilgrimage song: "How lovely are your dwellings, LORD of Armies! My soul longs, and even faints for the courts of the LORD." Even the sparrow and swallow nest by God's altars — and the psalm pronounces its blessings in threes: blessed are those who dwell in God's house, blessed "those whose strength is in you, who have set their hearts on a pilgrimage," blessed the man who trusts. On the road, "Passing through the valley of Weeping, they make it a place of springs," going "from strength to strength" until each appears before God in Zion. The pilgrim's arithmetic settles it: "For a day in your courts is better than a thousand. I would rather be a doorkeeper in the house of my God, than to dwell in the tents of wickedness" — for the LORD is sun and shield, giving grace and glory, withholding "no good thing from those who walk blamelessly."
**Tags:** `hunger-for-god` — "My soul longs, and even faints for the courts of the LORD.
