My heart and my flesh cry out for the living God" (84:2); `worship` — "Blessed are those who dwell in your house. They are always praising you," and the doorkeeper's choice (84:4, 10); `blessing` — the psalm's three beatitudes and "The LORD will give grace and glory. He withholds no good thing from those who walk blamelessly" (84:4–5, 11–12); `trust-in-god` — "LORD of Armies, blessed is the man who trusts in you" (84:12); `gods-protection` — "For the LORD God is a sun and a shield," and "God our shield" (84:9, 11); `zion-city-of-god` — the pilgrim psalm of the city: "How lovely are your dwellings, LORD of Armies!" — every road runs to appearing before God in Zion, where "a day in your courts is better than a thousand" (84:1–2, 7, 10)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 85
**Summary:** A Korahite psalm that prays from memory toward hope. It begins with what God has done before: favored the land, restored Jacob's fortunes, "forgiven the iniquity of your people… covered all their sin," and turned from his anger. So the community asks him to do it again: "Turn us, God of our salvation… Won't you revive us again, that your people may rejoice in you?" Then the psalmist goes quiet to listen — "I will hear what God, the LORD, will speak, for he will speak peace to his people" — with the caution that they not turn again to folly. What he hears becomes one of Scripture's loveliest pictures of salvation's wholeness: "Mercy and truth meet together. Righteousness and peace have kissed each other," truth springing from the earth, righteousness looking down from heaven, and the LORD giving what is good.
**Tags:** `restoration` — "You have restored the fortunes of Jacob," prayed forward: "Won't you revive us again, that your people may rejoice in you?" (85:1, 4–6); `forgiveness-of-sins` — "You have forgiven the iniquity of your people. You have covered all their sin" (85:2); `peace-of-god` — "he will speak peace to his people," and "Righteousness and peace have kissed each other" (85:8, 10); `gods-love` — "Show us your loving kindness, LORD," answered where "Mercy and truth meet together" (85:7, 10)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 86
**Summary:** The one Davidic psalm of Book III, headed simply "A Prayer by David": "Hear, LORD, and answer me, for I am poor and needy." It is stitched together from the vocabulary of grace — "you, Lord, are good, and ready to forgive, abundant in loving kindness to all those who call on you" — and from Sinai's own self-description: "you, Lord, are a merciful and gracious God, slow to anger, and abundant in loving kindness and truth." Among the petitions stands one of the Psalter's best prayers for a whole heart: "Teach me your way, LORD. I will walk in your truth. Make my heart undivided to fear your name." David prays it while proud and violent men seek his life, remembering deliverance "from the lowest Sheol," and holding the promise that "All nations you have made will come and worship before you, Lord." He asks last for a visible sign of goodness, "because you, LORD, have helped me, and comforted me."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Hear, LORD, and answer me… In the day of my trouble I will call on you, for you will answer me" (86:1, 6–7); `gods-love` — "abundant in loving kindness to all those who call on you," and "a merciful and gracious God, slow to anger" (86:5, 13, 15); `guidance` — "Teach me your way, LORD. I will walk in your truth. Make my heart undivided to fear your name" (86:11); `praise` — "I will praise you, Lord my God, with my whole heart. I will glorify your name forever more" (86:12); `trust-in-god` — "save your servant who trusts in you" (86:2)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 87
**Summary:** A short Korahite song about Zion's surprising birth registry. The city God founded on the holy mountains he also loves — "The LORD loves the gates of Zion more than all the dwellings of Jacob. Glorious things are spoken about you, city of God." Then the register opens, and the names in it are the old enemies: Rahab (Egypt) and Babylon, Philistia, Tyre, Ethiopia — each entered with the line "This one was born there." Of Zion it will be said that this one and that one was born in her, and "The LORD will count, when he writes up the peoples, 'This one was born there'" — former outsiders recorded as native-born citizens. Singers and dancers alike say of the city, "All my springs are in you."
**Tags:** `nations-and-peoples` — enemy nations written into Zion's birth register: "I will record Rahab and Babylon among those who acknowledge me… 'This one was born there'" (87:4–6); `zion-city-of-god` — the psalm is Zion's own registry: "The LORD loves the gates of Zion more than all the dwellings of Jacob. Glorious things are spoken about you, city of God" (87:1–3). (`nations-and-peoples` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary; the Zion register, logged then to the zion-city-of-god gap row, is carried by the concept adopted 2026-08-25.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 88
**Summary:** The darkest psalm in the Psalter — a Korahite psalm, "a contemplation by Heman, the Ezrahite" — a lament that never brightens. Its opening line holds the one thread: "LORD, the God of my salvation, I have cried day and night before you." From there it descends: a soul full of troubles, a life at Sheol's edge, "laid… in the lowest pit, in the darkest depths," God's wrath lying heavy, friends removed and the sufferer made an abomination to them — "I am afflicted and ready to die from my youth up." The psalmist argues from the grave's silence — "Do you show wonders to the dead?… Is your loving kindness declared in the grave?" — and keeps praying anyway: "In the morning, my prayer comes before you," while asking the unanswered question, "LORD, why do you reject my soul?" The last word is literally darkness: "You have put lover and friend far from me, and my friends into darkness." Scripture keeps this psalm as it is — proof that unrelieved anguish can still be prayer, addressed to "the God of my salvation."
**Tags:** `prayer` — prayer that persists without relief: "I have cried day and night before you… In the morning, my prayer comes before you" (88:1–2, 9, 13); `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — words for despair that stays: the psalm's only light is that every dark line is still spoken to "the God of my salvation" (88:1, 13–14); `loneliness` — "You have taken my friends from me… You have put lover and friend far from me" (88:8, 18); `pastoral-serious-illness` — a life lived at death's door: "My life draws near to Sheol… I am afflicted and ready to die from my youth up" (88:3–5, 15); `lament` — the whole psalm: the Psalter's darkest lament, the one that never brightens, every dark line still spoken to "the God of my salvation" (88:1–18); `unanswered-prayer` — the paradigm of prayer without relief: "I have cried day and night before you," yet "LORD, why do you reject my soul? Why do you hide your face from me?" (88:1–2, 13–14)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 89
**Summary:** Book III closes with Ethan the Ezrahite's contemplation — a psalm that sings the covenant and then stares at its apparent wreck. The first movement is all confidence: "I will sing of the loving kindness of the LORD forever," for God has sworn to David, "I will establish your offspring forever, and build up your throne to all generations"; heaven's council fears him, he rules "the pride of the sea," calming its rising waves, and "Righteousness and justice are the foundation of your throne." God's oath is quoted at length — David's line will call him Father, be kept as firstborn, "the highest of the kings of the earth"; sins will be punished with the rod, "But I will not completely take my loving kindness from him… I will not break my covenant." Then the turn: "But you have rejected and spurned… You have renounced the covenant of your servant. You have defiled his crown in the dust" — hedges broken down, strongholds in ruin, the throne thrown to the ground, the anointed's footsteps mocked. The psalm ends holding both truths in the dark: "Lord, where are your former loving kindnesses, which you swore to David in your faithfulness?" — followed by Book III's doxology, "Blessed be the LORD forever more. Amen, and Amen." (The New Testament announces David's promised offspring in Jesus — e.g. Acts 13:22–23 — an application to note alongside the psalm's own unresolved cry.)
**Tags:** `covenant` — "I have made a covenant with my chosen one, I have sworn to David, my servant," held against "You have renounced the covenant of your servant" (89:3–4, 28–39); `gods-faithfulness` — "With my mouth, I will make known your faithfulness to all generations," and "I will not break my covenant, nor alter what my lips have uttered" (89:1–2, 8, 33–34); `praise` — "The heavens will praise your wonders, LORD," and the closing doxology (89:5, 52); `blessing` — "Blessed are the people who learn to acclaim you. They walk in the light of your presence, LORD" (89:15–17); `lament` — the covenant sung and then protested: "But you have rejected and spurned… You have renounced the covenant of your servant. You have defiled his crown in the dust," down to "Lord, where are your former loving kindnesses, which you swore to David in your faithfulness?" (89:38–46, 49); `messianic-prophecy` — the sworn offspring and throne "to all generations" (89:3–4, 26–27, 35–37), which the New Testament announces as kept in Jesus (Acts 13:22–23) — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 90
**Summary:** Book IV opens with the Psalter's only psalm headed as Moses' — "A Prayer by Moses, the man of God" — setting eternity and mortality side by side. "Lord, you have been our dwelling place for all generations… from everlasting to everlasting, you are God," for whom a thousand years are like yesterday; man, meanwhile, is morning grass that withers by evening. The psalm is honest about why: "You have set our iniquities before you, our secret sins in the light of your presence," and our seventy or eighty years pass under that light "as a sigh," their pride "but labor and sorrow." From that hard arithmetic comes the prayer the psalm is famous for: "So teach us to count our days, that we may gain a heart of wisdom." The closing petitions turn the sigh into intercession: relent, have compassion, "Satisfy us in the morning with your loving kindness," gladden us in proportion to our affliction — and "Establish the work of our hands for us."
**Tags:** `stewardship-of-days` — "So teach us to count our days, that we may gain a heart of wisdom" (90:12), with the accounting of 90:9–10 behind it; `sin` — "You have set our iniquities before you, our secret sins in the light of your presence" (90:7–8); `prayer` — "Relent, LORD! How long? Have compassion on your servants!… Satisfy us in the morning with your loving kindness" (90:13–17); `mortality` — eternity set against morning grass: man returns to dust, his seventy or eighty years passing "as a sigh," their pride "but labor and sorrow" (90:3–10); `creation` — the Maker himself is unmade: before the mountains were born or the earth and the world were formed, "from everlasting to everlasting, you are God" (90:2) — the psalm's own answer to the who-made-God question; `gods-unchanging-nature` — the same verse in its deity-without-beginning register, beside `creation` per the both-tags ruling: "from everlasting to everlasting, you are God" (90:2) — there is no direction of time in which he is other than God. (The mortality material of 90:3–10 is logged with the mortality gap refs. 2026-08-25: that row's concept was adopted as a display tag and is applied above.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 91
**Summary:** An anonymous psalm of refuge, spoken from inside the shelter it describes: "He who dwells in the secret place of the Most High will rest in the shadow of the Almighty." The psalmist's own confession — "He is my refuge and my fortress; my God, in whom I trust" — opens into promises for whoever makes the Most High his dwelling: rescue from snare and pestilence, cover "with his feathers," faithfulness as shield and rampart, no fear of "the terror by night, nor of the arrow that flies by day," even when a thousand fall at your side. "For he will put his angels in charge of you, to guard you in all your ways" — the verse the devil later quotes to Jesus in the wilderness, and Jesus answers, "You shall not test the Lord, your God" (Matthew 4:6–7 — a caution the psalm's own shape supports: this is confidence for those who dwell with God, not a formula to presume upon). The psalm ends with God himself speaking over the one who has set his love on him: "He will call on me, and I will answer him. I will be with him in trouble… and show him my salvation."
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — "He who dwells in the secret place of the Most High will rest in the shadow of the Almighty… my refuge and my fortress" (91:1–2, 9); `gods-protection` — "He will cover you with his feathers. Under his wings you will take refuge," and "he will put his angels in charge of you" (91:3–4, 11–12); `fear-not` — "You shall not be afraid of the terror by night, nor of the arrow that flies by day" (91:5–7); `trust-in-god` — "my God, in whom I trust" (91:2); `presence-of-god` — God's own pledge: "He will call on me, and I will answer him. I will be with him in trouble" (91:15); `angels` — the charge-over-you text itself: "he will put his angels in charge of you," bearing the sheltered one up in their hands (91:11–12) — with the summary's own caution that the devil's quotation of these verses met "You shall not test the Lord, your God" (Matthew 4:6–7)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 92
**Summary:** An anonymous psalm headed "A song for the Sabbath day," and its content is what a sabbath does: stop and say what is true about God. "It is a good thing to give thanks to the LORD… to proclaim your loving kindness in the morning, and your faithfulness every night," with lute, harp, and lyre, "For you, LORD, have made me glad through your work." The senseless man cannot read the times — "though the wicked spring up as the grass, and all the evildoers flourish, they will be destroyed forever," while the LORD is on high forever more. The psalmist, anointed with fresh oil, has seen the turning of the tables — and the psalm closes with its famous planting: "The righteous shall flourish like the palm tree," planted in the LORD's house, "They will still produce fruit in old age… to show that the LORD is upright. He is my rock, and there is no unrighteousness in him."
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — "It is a good thing to give thanks to the LORD" (92:1); `praise` — "to sing praises to your name, Most High… For you, LORD, have made me glad through your work" (92:1–4); `joy-in-the-lord` — "you, LORD, have made me glad through your work. I will triumph in the works of your hands" (92:4); `divine-judgment` — "though the wicked spring up as the grass… they will be destroyed forever," and "your enemies shall perish. All the evildoers will be scattered" (92:7, 9); `aging-and-old-age` — the closing promise over the planted righteous: "They will still produce fruit in old age. They will be full of sap and green" (92:12–15)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 93
**Summary:** The first of Book IV's enthronement hymns, five verses of pure acclamation: "The LORD reigns! He is clothed with majesty!" — armed with strength, his throne "established from long ago," himself from everlasting, and the world held so firmly "It can't be moved." The one rival voice is the flood: the floods have lifted up their voice, the breakers pound — but "Above the voices of many waters, the mighty breakers of the sea, the LORD on high is mighty." The last verse turns from the throne room to its two exports: "Your statutes stand firm. Holiness adorns your house, LORD, forever more." Standing where it does — just after Psalm 89's fallen crown — the hymn answers Book III's crisis: whatever has happened to David's throne, the LORD's has not moved.
**Tags:** `praise` — the hymn is acclamation from its first line: "The LORD reigns! He is clothed with majesty!… the LORD on high is mighty" (93:1, 4); `god-reigns` — the enthronement acclamation entire: "The LORD reigns! He is clothed with majesty!" — a throne "established from long ago," mightier than the pounding floods (93:1–4); `trustworthiness-of-scripture` — the throne room's first export: "Your statutes stand firm. Holiness adorns your house, LORD, forever more" (93:5). (`praise` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary; the enthronement acclamation, logged then to the god-reigns gap row, is carried by that concept — adopted 2026-08-25 — above.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 94
**Summary:** An anonymous psalm that begins by handing the gavel back to its owner: "LORD, you God to whom vengeance belongs… Rise up, you judge of the earth. Pay back the proud what they deserve." The arrogant crush God's people, kill the widow and the alien, murder the fatherless, and reason, "The LORD will not see." The psalm's retort is one of Scripture's sharpest arguments: "He who implanted the ear, won't he hear? He who formed the eye, won't he see?" Then it turns pastoral: "Blessed is the man whom you discipline, LORD, and teach out of your law," for the LORD will not reject his people. The psalmist testifies from inside the storm — "When I said, 'My foot is slipping!' Your loving kindness, LORD, held me up. In the multitude of my thoughts within me, your comforts delight my soul" — and ends with the LORD as high tower and rock, bringing the wicked's own iniquity back on them.
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — "LORD, you God to whom vengeance belongs… Rise up, you judge of the earth," and the argument from the Maker of ear and eye (94:1–2, 8–10, 23); `the-lords-discipline` — "Blessed is the man whom you discipline, LORD, and teach out of your law, that you may give him rest from the days of adversity" (94:12–13); `peace-of-god` — "In the multitude of my thoughts within me, your comforts delight my soul" (94:19); `refuge-in-trouble` — "the LORD has been my high tower, my God, the rock of my refuge" (94:22); `gods-faithfulness` — "For the LORD won't reject his people, neither will he forsake his inheritance" (94:14, 18); `vengeance` — vengeance located in God and left there: "LORD, you God to whom vengeance belongs… Rise up, you judge of the earth. Pay back the proud what they deserve" (94:1–2); `justice-and-oppression` — the societal indictment: the arrogant crush God's people, kill the widow and the alien, and murder the fatherless — answered by the Maker who hears and sees: "He who implanted the ear, won't he hear? He who formed the eye, won't he see?" (94:5–10, 20–21). (The oppression of widow, alien, and fatherless, 94:5–6, is logged with the justice-and-oppression gap refs per the project ruling. 2026-08-25: that row's concept was adopted as a display tag and is applied above.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 95
**Summary:** An anonymous call to worship that ends with a warning label. First the summons, twice over: "Oh come, let's sing to the LORD. Let's shout aloud to the rock of our salvation!… Oh come, let's worship and bow down. Let's kneel before the LORD, our Maker" — because he is "a great King above all gods," holding the deeps, the heights, the sea he made, and the dry land his hands formed; and because "We are the people of his pasture, and the sheep in his care." Then, mid-verse, the shepherd's own voice: "Today, oh that you would hear his voice! Don't harden your heart, as at Meribah" — remembering the wilderness generation that tested God for forty years until he swore, "They won't enter into my rest." Worship, the psalm insists, is not only song but a heart that listens today. (Hebrews 3–4 preaches these closing verses at length, holding "today" open for every generation — a New Testament application.)
**Tags:** `worship` — "Oh come, let's worship and bow down. Let's kneel before the LORD, our Maker" (95:1–2, 6); `thanksgiving` — "Let's come before his presence with thanksgiving. Let's extol him with songs!" (95:2); `obedience-to-the-word` — "Today, oh that you would hear his voice! Don't harden your heart, as at Meribah" (95:7–11); `creation` — "The sea is his, and he made it. His hands formed the dry land," with the depths and heights in his hand (95:4–5); `god-reigns` — the summons's ground: "For the LORD is a great God, a great King above all gods" (95:3); `hardness-of-heart` — the shepherd's warning label: "Don't harden your heart, as at Meribah," remembering the generation that tested God until he swore "They won't enter into my rest" (95:8–11); `messianic-prophecy` — the psalm's closing oracle (95:7–11), preached at length by Hebrews 3–4, which holds its "today" open for every generation — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 96
**Summary:** A missionary enthronement hymn: "Sing to the LORD a new song! Sing to the LORD, all the earth" — and don't keep it in the sanctuary: "Proclaim his salvation from day to day! Declare his glory among the nations." The reason is a comparison: "For all the gods of the peoples are idols, but the LORD made the heavens," while honor, majesty, strength, and beauty surround him. The families of nations are told to ascribe him glory, bring an offering, enter his courts, and "Worship the LORD in holy array" — and the message for the world is three words: "Say among the nations, 'The LORD reigns.'" At that news, creation itself becomes the choir — heavens glad, sea roaring, fields exulting, "Then all the trees of the woods shall sing for joy" — because the King is coming as Judge: "He will judge the world with righteousness, the peoples with his truth."
**Tags:** `praise` — "Sing to the LORD a new song! Sing to the LORD, all the earth" (96:1–2); `nations-and-peoples` — "Declare his glory among the nations… Ascribe to the LORD, you families of nations," and "Say among the nations, 'The LORD reigns'" (96:3, 7, 10); `worship` — "Bring an offering, and come into his courts. Worship the LORD in holy array" (96:8–9); `sharing-your-faith` — "Proclaim his salvation from day to day! Declare his glory among the nations, his marvelous works among all the peoples" (96:2–3); `divine-judgment` — "he comes to judge the earth. He will judge the world with righteousness" (96:10, 13); `god-reigns` — the message for the world in three words: "Say among the nations, 'The LORD reigns'" (96:10); `idolatry` — the polemic comparison at the hymn's base: "For all the gods of the peoples are idols, but the LORD made the heavens" (96:4–5); `no-other-god` — the same comparison read as the hymn's exclusivity claim, beside `idolatry` per the both-tags ruling (the rivals' emptiness vs the LORD's uniqueness as Maker): "For all the gods of the peoples are idols, but the LORD made the heavens" (96:5)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 97
**Summary:** Another enthronement hymn, this one wrapped in storm: "The LORD reigns! Let the earth rejoice! Let the multitude of islands be glad!" He comes in clouds and darkness — but "Righteousness and justice are the foundation of his throne" — with fire before him, lightning lighting the world, and mountains melting "like wax at the presence of the LORD." Every idol-server is shamed ("Worship him, all you gods!"), while "Zion heard and was glad" at his judgments. The hymn then hands its hearers their assignment: "You who love the LORD, hate evil!" — for he preserves and delivers his saints — and its gift: "Light is sown for the righteous, and gladness for the upright in heart. Be glad in the LORD, you righteous people!"
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — "A fire goes before him, and burns up his adversaries on every side… The mountains melt like wax at the presence of the LORD" (97:2–5); `joy-in-the-lord` — "Let the earth rejoice!… Light is sown for the righteous, and gladness for the upright in heart. Be glad in the LORD" (97:1, 8, 11–12); `gods-protection` — "He preserves the souls of his saints. He delivers them out of the hand of the wicked" (97:10); `holiness` — "You who love the LORD, hate evil!" and thanks given "to his holy Name" (97:10, 12); `god-reigns` — the acclamation and its worldwide reach: "The LORD reigns! Let the earth rejoice! Let the multitude of islands be glad!" (97:1)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 98
**Summary:** "Sing to the LORD a new song, for he has done marvelous things!" — an anonymous psalm in which the news precedes the noise. God's right hand and holy arm "have worked salvation for him"; he "has made known his salvation," shown his righteousness openly "in the sight of the nations," and "remembered his loving kindness and his faithfulness toward the house of Israel," so that "All the ends of the earth have seen the salvation of our God." Then the orchestra assembles by sections — harp and melody, trumpets and ram's horn, "before the King, the LORD" — and finally creation itself: "Let the rivers clap their hands. Let the mountains sing for joy together." As in Psalm 96, the joy has a horizon: "he comes to judge the earth. He will judge the world with righteousness, and the peoples with equity."
**Tags:** `praise` — "Sing to the LORD a new song… Make a joyful noise to the LORD, all the earth!" (98:1, 4–6); `salvation` — "The LORD has made known his salvation… All the ends of the earth have seen the salvation of our God" (98:1–3); `gods-faithfulness` — "He has remembered his loving kindness and his faithfulness toward the house of Israel" (98:3); `divine-judgment` — "he comes to judge the earth. He will judge the world with righteousness, and the peoples with equity" (98:9)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 99
**Summary:** The last "The LORD reigns" hymn of this batch trades storm for holiness: "The LORD reigns! Let the peoples tremble. He sits enthroned among the cherubim." Three times a refrain rings like a bell — "He is Holy!… He is Holy!… for the LORD, our God, is holy" — around a King whose strength "loves justice" and who has executed justice and righteousness in Jacob. The middle of the psalm opens the archives: Moses, Aaron, and Samuel "called on the LORD, and he answered them," spoke to them in the pillar of cloud — "You are a God who forgave them, although you took vengeance for their doings." Twice the same double command returns — exalt and worship — first at his footstool, then at his holy hill: "Exalt the LORD our God. Worship at his footstool… Worship at his holy hill."
**Tags:** `holiness` — the triple refrain: "He is Holy!… for the LORD, our God, is holy" (99:3, 5, 9); `worship` — "Exalt the LORD our God. Worship at his footstool," repeated at his holy hill (99:5, 9); `prayer` — "Samuel was among those who call on his name. They called on the LORD, and he answered them" (99:6, 8); `forgiveness-of-sins` — "You are a God who forgave them, although you took vengeance for their doings" (99:8); `god-reigns` — the enthronement acclamation over trembling peoples: "The LORD reigns! Let the peoples tremble. He sits enthroned among the cherubim," a King whose strength "loves justice" (99:1–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 100
**Summary:** The batch closes with the Psalter's most famous doorway hymn, headed "A Psalm of thanksgiving": "Shout for joy to the LORD, all you lands! Serve the LORD with gladness. Come before his presence with singing." Its center is knowledge, not just noise: "Know that the LORD, he is God. It is he who has made us, and we are his. We are his people, and the sheep of his pasture." The traffic directions follow — "Enter into his gates with thanksgiving, and into his courts with praise" — and the closing verse gives the everlasting reason: "For the LORD is good. His loving kindness endures forever, his faithfulness to all generations." Five verses of commands and the reasons for them — all joy.
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — "Enter into his gates with thanksgiving… Give thanks to him, and bless his name" (100:4); `praise` — "Shout for joy to the LORD, all you lands!" and courts entered "with praise" (100:1, 4); `worship` — "Serve the LORD with gladness. Come before his presence with singing" (100:2–3); `joy-in-the-lord` — joy commanded: "Shout for joy… with gladness… with singing" (100:1–2); `gods-faithfulness` — "His loving kindness endures forever, his faithfulness to all generations" (100:5)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

# Tag gaps (for the book thread to consolidate into tag-gaps.md — this worker does NOT write that file)

Re-deduplicated for v4 (2026-08-23) against the current 131-id vocabulary and against `/mnt/project-files/research/bible-rollout/tag-gaps.md` **as it stood at re-check time**: a main table of 34 rows from nine book threads (Genesis, Obadiah, 2 John, Joel, Romans, 3 John, Micah, Colossians, Jonah), plus append blocks from eighteen more threads — the eight present at the v3 check (Joshua, Ruth, Hosea, Nahum, Amos, Habakkuk, Ephesians, Haggai) and ten landed since (Matthew, Job, Galatians, Malachi, Zephaniah, Philippians, 2 Corinthians, 1 John, Exodus, Jude) — which carry both merged refs into earlier rows and their own new rows, the Joshua lexicon-extension flags, and the checked-and-covered list. The v3 re-check moved `idolatry`, `lament`, and all justice/oppression bookkeeping to appends against **landed** rows (Micah's `idolatry` and unified `justice-and-oppression`, Joel's `lament` — the id under which Amos's staged `justice-and-oppression-of-the-poor` gap already merged); the v4 re-check adds five more appends owed to rows in the newer blocks — `prosperity-of-the-wicked` and `leviathan-and-behemoth` (Job), `unanswered-prayer` (2 Corinthians), `hardness-of-heart` (Exodus), and `confession-of-sin` (1 John). The shared log grows continuously as sibling threads append — that is expected, not an error — so a final re-dedupe happens at write time: whoever performs the final write must re-verify every entry below against the file as it stands then. Per the append rules, refs for already-listed themes are appends, never duplicate rows.

# Decisions record (delegated defaults — reversible on Jesse's word)

1. **No subdivisions in Psalms 51–100.** CONVENTIONS §4 takes section boundaries strictly from BSB headings (or structural markers the book itself provides), and the public-domain BSB places exactly **one** heading per psalm in this range — all fifty at verse 1 (verified against the ebible.org engbsb USFM, 2026-08-08 edition, staged for this thread). With no BSB-provided internal boundaries, every psalm stays whole — even the long composites (68's procession, 78's history, 89's hymn-then-lament), whose movements are described in prose instead. The Book II/III and III/IV seams (72→73, 89→90) are handled in prose, not sections, as batch 1 did for Book I/II.
2. **Vocabulary ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The taggable vocabulary is the 131 YAML file **basenames** under `ontology/concepts/` on main at b3f491d, including the 14 `pastoral-*` files whose internal `id:` fields differ — the basename is always the tag. The 9 PR #43 ids are usable, and **any use is a reversible delegated default; none is used in this batch** — each was considered and none met the honest-presence bar (see items 6–9).
3. **Pastoral-register ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The 14 pastoral-* concepts are the personal-crisis register and are applied only where a psalm's own register genuinely matches — individual refuge, personal restoration. Applied here: `pastoral-betrayal-and-marriage-crisis` (55), `pastoral-god-sees-my-suffering` (56, 69), `pastoral-hope-in-despair` (69, 77, 88), `pastoral-serious-illness` (88) — all individual laments. **Not** applied to national-scale material: the communal laments 74, 79, 80 and the covenant crisis of 89 carry no pastoral-* tag, and `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` was deliberately withheld from 68:5–6, 72:2–14, 76:9, 82:3–4, and 94:5–6 — that national-scale justice material is instead logged as refs to append to the landed `justice-and-oppression` row in tag-gaps.md (Micah's row, the unified id under which the staged `justice-and-oppression-of-the-poor` gaps merge — see the Tag gaps section). (Batch 1 applied the same ruling in its round-1 revision, dropping `pastoral-hope-in-despair` from the communal Psalm 44 — see its Decision 17; the two batches are aligned.)
4. **Divine-name rendering ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The pinned engwebp corpus renders the divine name "the LORD"; every quotation in this file follows the corpus rendering — "Yahweh" appears nowhere in this document except in this sentence recording the ruling.
5. **Superscriptions as text, attribution only as stated.** Continued from batch 1: Psalm 51's Bathsheba setting, 52's Doeg, 54's Ziphites, 56's Gath, 57's cave, 59's watched house, 60's Edomite war, 63's desert, 72's "By Solomon," 88's Heman, 89's Ethan, 90's Moses — all from verse 1. Anonymous psalms (66, 67, 71, 91–100) are left anonymous; Hebrews 4:7 introduces Psalm 95's words "through David," but the psalm itself is untitled, so no attribution is made and the NT connection is signposted only as application.
6. **Psalm 73 and `doubt` (PR #43) — not tagged.** The psalmist's near-fall is named by the psalm itself as envy ("I was envious of the arrogant," 73:3) and senselessness ("I was so senseless and ignorant," 73:22), argued through inside faith — "Surely God is good to Israel" stands as the psalm's first line. Tagging `doubt` would import a category the text doesn't use; `envy-and-jealousy` carries the crisis honestly.
7. **Psalm 51:11 and `trinity` / `holy-spirit-the-comforter` — not tagged.** "Don't take your Holy Spirit from me" names God's Spirit, but the psalm teaches neither the Trinity nor the Comforter of John 14–16; tagging either would read later revelation back into the text (the Genesis-1 `identity-in-christ` precedent).
8. **Doxologies and `benediction` (PR #43) — not tagged.** 72:18–19 and 89:52 are doxologies closing Books II and III — praise ascribed *to* God — not benedictions pronounced *over* people; same reasoning as batch 1's item on 41:13. Noted in prose instead.
9. **Psalm 89 / 72 and messianic ids — not tagged.** `christ-the-cornerstone`, `incarnation`, `deity-of-christ` were considered for the royal psalms and not applied: the psalms' own texts pray for and about Israel's king. NT applications (Acts 13:22–23; Eph 4:8 at 68:18; John 10:34 at 82:6; Matt 13:35 at 78:2; Matt 4:6 at 91:11–12; Heb 3–4 at 95:7–11) are signposted in the summaries as applications, and all are logged as messiah-in-the-psalms gap refs.
10. **Psalm 88 and `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — tagged, with care.** Psalm 88 never brightens; tagging "hope in despair" could overclaim, so the justification states the psalm's real shape: its only light is that every dark line is still addressed to "the God of my salvation" (88:1). That is precisely the concept's pastoral substance — despair that stays inside prayer — and a searcher in despair should find this psalm. Reversible if Jesse prefers the tag dropped.
11. **Psalm 92's Sabbath heading and `sabbath-rest` — not tagged.** The heading appoints the psalm "for the Sabbath day," but the psalm's content is thanksgiving and praise; it teaches nothing about sabbath rest. Heading-only presence does not meet the substance bar.
12. **Psalm 95:11 "my rest" and `sabbath-rest` — not tagged.** Hebrews 3–4 famously connects Psalm 95's rest to God's sabbath rest, and the summary signposts that; the psalm's own "rest" is the land-rest forfeited at Meribah. Tagging would read Hebrews' frame back into the psalm.
13. **Psalms 52 and 64 and `taming-the-tongue` — not tagged.** Both psalms depict the *wicked's* tongue (razor, sword, arrows); neither teaches controlling one's own. Batch 1's item 11 precedent (Psalm 12): `honesty` carries the deceit portrait in 52; 64's tongue material feeds the slander gap refs.
14. **Psalm 91's promises — described inside the psalm's own frame.** The sweeping protections of 91 ("no evil shall happen to you") are presented as the psalm presents them — the confidence of the one who dwells in the Most High's shelter — not as an unconditional formula (doctrinal guardrail: no blessing-as-entitlement). The summary carries the psalm's own counterweight: the devil quotes 91:11–12 and Jesus answers that shelter is not presumption (Matt 4:6–7).
15. **Psalm 82's "gods" — described, not adjudicated.** The summary keeps the text's own scene (God judging "among the gods" who judge unjustly and "shall die like men") without deciding between the standard readings (human judges/rulers; heavenly beings). John 10:34 is signposted as application.
16. **Psalm 83's imprecations — the repentance clause kept visible.** Uniquely among the curse-psalms, 83:16 prays "that they may seek your name"; the summary and tag line keep that clause visible so the psalm's own stated aim is not flattened into bare vindictiveness — while 83:17's "let them be confounded and perish" is not sanitized either.
17. **Psalm 67 and `sharing-your-faith` — not tagged.** The psalm prays that God's way be known among all nations; it depicts no one proclaiming. Psalms 66:16, 71:15–18, and 96:2–3 — where declaring is actually done or vowed — carry the tag instead; 67's intent lives in `nations-and-peoples` and `blessing`.
18. **Psalm 60 = 108 overlap noted.** 60:5–12 reappears as 108:6–13; flagged here so batch 3 can cross-reference rather than re-derive. Likewise 70 = 40:13–17 (stated in the summary) and 53 ≈ 14 (stated in the summary).
19. **Provenance split stated per chapter-set, not silently pooled.** 13 chapters verified against the pinned-snapshot fixture (byte-identical to current, 198 verses); 37 chapters current-edition only — see the header. No claim of pinned-text verification is made for the 37.

# Notes toward the book summary (for the book-summary worker)

- **The batch spans three book seams:** Book II ends at 72 (doxology 72:18–19 plus the unique colophon "This ends the prayers by David, the son of Jesse," 72:20); Book III (73–89) is the Asaph/Korah shelf, darkest of the five, ending in 89's covenant crisis and doxology; Book IV opens at 90 with Moses and answers 89 with the enthronement psalms — after "You have defiled his crown in the dust" (89:39), the "The LORD reigns" acclamations (93:1; 96:10; 97:1; 99:1 — all four enthronement instances fall in this batch; the phrase's only other WEB-Psalter occurrence is 9:7, and it does not recur in 101–150) are the Psalter's structural rebuttal, with 94–95 and 98 carrying the group's themes between them: David's throne may fall; the LORD's cannot.
- **Book III's communal register** is new in the Psalter at this scale: burned sanctuary (74), Jerusalem in heaps (79), the ravaged vine (80), the covenant seemingly renounced (89) — the book summary should note that the Psalter prays national catastrophe, not only private trouble.
- **Psalm 51 pairs with batch 1's penitential strand** (6, 32, 38) as its summit; 65:3's "Sins overwhelmed me, but you atoned for our transgressions" and 85:2's "You have covered all their sin" carry the forgiveness theme corporately.
- **The two great teaching-history psalms** begin here (78; 105–106 follow in batch 3): Israel's story retold as warning and as praise, with the next generation as the audience (78:4–8).
- **Psalm 88 deserves a sentence of its own** in the book summary: the Psalter includes one psalm with no dawn, and its existence is pastoral data — unrelieved darkness can still be prayer.
- **Enthronement + mission:** 96–100 fuse "The LORD reigns" with summons to all nations/all the earth — the Psalter's most missionary stretch; 67 and 87 (enemies reborn as Zion's citizens) belong to the same thread.
- **Genres represented in 51–100:** penitential (51), individual lament (54–57, 59, 61, 63–64, 69–71, 86, 88), imprecatory-heavy laments (58, 59, 69, 83), communal lament (60, 74, 79, 80, 85 in part, 89 in part), royal (61:6–7, 63:11, 72, 89), historical (78, 81 in part, 95 in part), wisdom/theodicy (73, 90), pilgrimage/Zion songs (84, 87; 65, 76), thanksgiving (65–66, 75, 92), hymn/enthronement (68, 93, 96–100), prophetic oracle within psalm (75, 81, 82, 95).
- **Doubles across the Psalter:** 53 ≈ 14; 70 = 40:13–17; 60:5–12 = 108:6–13 — worth one line in the book summary about how the collection reuses its own material.
- **"Loving kindness" density:** the WEB's covenant-love word saturates this batch (57, 63:3 "better than life," 85, 86, 89:1, 100:5); batch 1's suggestion of a one-time lay gloss stands.
- **Aging thread** (71; 92:14) and the Moses-psalm's prayer to count our days (90:12) give the book summary a life-span arc worth a sentence.

# Originality check (CONVENTIONS §7)

Method: every chapter summary was drafted fresh from the WEB text; after drafting, a mechanical normalized 5-gram scan (lowercased, punctuation stripped) was run over this whole document against the staged full texts of the two public-domain references actually consulted — Brooks' *Summarized Bible* (Psalms 51–100 section, archive.org OCR) and *Matthew Henry's Concise Commentary* (Psalms 51–100, CCEL) — with direct WEB scripture quotations excluded from the comparison.

- **biblesummary.info:** not consulted for this batch at all; no text was available to copy. Zero exposure, zero overlap.
- **BibleProject:** not consulted for this batch at all; no framing or wording drawn from it (`bibleproject-framing` accordingly appears in no Sources line). Zero exposure, zero overlap.
- **brooks-1919:** 12 shared 5-grams, every one a window of Bible phrasing both texts carry because they summarize the same psalms ("a banner to those who fear", "honey out of the rock", "God to whom vengeance belongs", "people of his pasture", "rock of our salvation", "there is a God who judges"), and all 12 also occur verbatim in the WEB text itself. After excluding scripture wording: **zero** shared 5-grams. No sentence lifted, no archaic wording carried over.
- **mhcc:** 35 shared 5-grams, all of the same kind — unquoted scripture wording flowing through both texts ("rock that is higher than", "the sheep of his pasture", "be glad in the LORD", "speak peace to his people", "neither from the east nor from the west", "the work of our hands"), every one also present verbatim in the WEB text. After excluding scripture wording: **zero** shared 5-grams. No sentence lifted, no archaic wording carried over. (Three drafting-stage overlaps were caught by this scan and reworded before delivery: a prose phrase on Psalm 86 — "the day when all…" — echoing Brooks' wording about the nations, and paraphrases of 51:17 and 54:3 that matched MHCC's conjoined wording — each replaced with the WEB's own quoted words.)

Archaism check: the draft was scanned for KJV-era forms (unto, thereof, hath, shalt, thee, thou, ye, doth, saith); none appear outside this sentence. The WEB's own renderings are used throughout (e.g., "loving kindness," "Sheol," "the LORD of Armies," "the LORD" for the divine name per the project ruling).

# Psalms 101–150 — chapter summaries with tags (batch 3 of 3, draft v5)

**Date:** 2026-08-23 · **Status:** drafting-worker output, revised through four harsh-critic rounds (round 1: all 17 objections resolved plus a drafter sweep; round 2: all 8 objections resolved plus the ordered exhaustive clause-by-clause verse-tracking pass, 18 further conversions; round 3: both objections resolved — the Ps 148 KJV echo and a stale status line — plus two consolidation-hazard advisories applied; round 4: both objections resolved quote-first — the Ps 111 and Ps 138 KJV echoes surfaced by the critic's rival-corpus n-gram detector — plus the Isaiah-landing write-time advisory applied to the Tag gaps section; see the revision notes before the Decisions record) — v5 itself NOT yet through a fresh critic round, nothing enters the engine or the repo. The book-level summary and Psalms 1–100 are other workers' batches; this file carries batch-level notes for the eventual book summary.

## How this batch was made

Every summary was drafted fresh from the WEB Bible text of Psalms 101–150, with two public-domain references consulted per chapter — Keith Brooks' *Summarized Bible* (1919) and *Matthew Henry's Concise Commentary* — and the public-domain Berean Standard Bible headings consulted for structure. BibleProject materials and biblesummary.info were not consulted for this batch at all. Nothing was copied from any source; a mechanical normalized 5-gram originality scan against Brooks and Matthew Henry (and a statement of non-consultation for the steering-only sources) is reported at the end of this file.

**WEB text provenance (per CONVENTIONS §3):** the batch text was extracted from the ebible.org engwebp verse-per-line publication declared in `pipeline/manifests/web.json` (the staged 2026-08-23 download shared by the Psalms batches). The downloaded zip's sha256 (`b6f55cc787b1…`) differs from the pinned snapshot's (`3458ca3442…`) — the known upstream drift. Verification against the pinned snapshot was therefore done through `pipeline/fixtures/web-subset.json` (generated from the pinned 2026-07-29 source): the fixture witnesses 9 of this batch's 50 chapters — **Psalms 101, 103, 116, 121, 136, 139, 146, 147, 150** (143 verses) — and every witnessed verse is byte-identical between the pinned fixture and the current edition. Those 9 chapters are verified against the pinned text; **the other 41 chapters are checked against the current 2026-08-23 edition only.** Per the project-wide rendering ruling, all quotations use the corpus's own rendering of the divine name — "the LORD" — throughout.

## Batch-level notes

- **Book IV / Book V.** The Psalter's last seam falls inside this batch: Book IV closes at Psalm 106 with its doxology — "Blessed be the LORD, the God of Israel, from everlasting even to everlasting! Let all the people say, 'Amen.' Praise the LORD!" (106:48) — and Book V runs from Psalm 107 to the end.
- **Superscriptions.** As in the earlier batches, the WEB folds each psalm's ancient heading into verse 1; headings are treated as text and used for attribution exactly as far as they state. Seventeen psalms in this range are "by David" (101, 103, 108–110, 122, 124, 131, 133, and 138–145, by their own headings), one is "by Solomon" (127), and the rest are left anonymous — including 102 ("A Prayer of the afflicted"), 104–107, and the praise psalms 111–118, 135, and 146–150, where no author is named.
- **One subdivision: Psalm 119.** The only psalm subdivided in this batch — and in this worker's understanding, the only one in the whole Psalter — is Psalm 119, whose 176 verses the BSB itself divides into the 22 stanzas of the Hebrew-alphabet acrostic (ALEPH through TAW), eight verses each. Those BSB-provided boundaries are followed exactly; see the Decisions record.
- **Songs of Ascents.** Psalms 120–134 each carry the heading "A Song of Ascents" — a named collection of fifteen short psalms, long associated with pilgrims going up to Jerusalem (the summaries note the collection; the pilgrim setting is presented as the traditional reading, since the headings themselves say only "Ascents").
- **Final hallel.** Psalms 146–150 each open and close with "Praise the LORD!" — five psalms of unbroken praise that end the Psalter.
- **Imprecatory language.** Psalm 109's sustained curse-list, Psalm 137:7–9 against Edom and Babylon, Psalm 139:19–22, and shorter passages (129:5–8; 140:9–11; 141:10) ask God to judge, shame, or destroy enemies. As in batch 1, summaries describe these prayers as what they are in the text — the wronged handing judgment to God rather than taking it — without sanitizing them and without presenting them as a model beyond what the text supports.
- **Historical psalms.** Psalms 105, 106, 135, and 136 retell Israel's story; every event is kept attributed exactly as the psalm itself states it — including where a psalm's selection or order differs from the Pentateuch's narration (105 lists eight of the plagues, in its own order, and the summaries follow the psalm).

---

# The fifty psalms

## Psalms 101
**Summary:** A psalm by David — a ruler's vow about the house he will keep and the company he will allow. He will sing of loving kindness and justice, live carefully and blamelessly at home ("I will walk within my house with a blameless heart"), and set no vile thing before his eyes. The vow then reaches outward: secret slanderers silenced, the arrogant not tolerated, the faithful of the land sought out to serve him, and practitioners of deceit banned from his house. It closes with a king's resolve: morning by morning, to cut off the workers of iniquity from the LORD's city.
**Tags:** `holiness` — "I will be careful to live a blameless life… I will walk within my house with a blameless heart," and "I will set no vile thing before my eyes" (101:2–4); `honesty` — deceit and falsehood barred from the house: "He who practices deceit won't dwell within my house. He who speaks falsehood won't be established before my eyes" (101:5, 7); `leadership` — a ruler's integrity charter for court and city: the faithful of the land sought out to serve him, deceit banned from his house, and "Morning by morning, I will destroy all the wicked of the land, to cut off all the workers of iniquity from the LORD's city" (101:2–8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 102
**Summary:** Headed "A Prayer of the afflicted, when he is overwhelmed and pours out his complaint before the LORD" — and the complaint is bodily and bleak: days consuming away like smoke, bones sticking to skin, food forgotten, enemies reproaching him all day, ashes eaten like bread. The sufferer is "like a sparrow that is alone on the housetop," and he traces even his misery to God's indignation. Then the psalm turns from failing days to the everlasting God: "But you, LORD, will remain forever" — the LORD will arise and have mercy on Zion at the set time, hear the groans of the prisoner, and be praised by a people yet to be created. It ends by setting frail flesh against the unchanging Creator: the heavens will wear out like a garment, "But you are the same. Your years will have no end" — words the New Testament later applies to the Son (Hebrews 1:10–12), a later application the psalm's own text does not make.
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Hear my prayer, LORD! Let my cry come to you… Answer me quickly in the day when I call" (102:1–2); `loneliness` — "I am like a pelican of the wilderness… like a sparrow that is alone on the housetop" (102:6–7); `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — a personal collapse prayed all the way through to "But you, LORD, will remain forever" and the certainty that God hears the destitute (102:1–11, 12, 17); `gods-faithfulness` — the everlasting God outlasting a perishing creation: "They will perish, but you will endure… But you are the same" (102:12, 25–27); `lament` — headed "A Prayer of the afflicted, when he is overwhelmed and pours out his complaint before the LORD": days like smoke, bones to skin, ashes eaten like bread (102:1–11); `mortality` — frail flesh against the unchanging Creator: days consuming away like smoke and cut short mid-course, while "you are the same. Your years will have no end" (102:3, 11, 23–27); `restoration-of-israel` — the turn to national hope: the LORD "will remain forever," will arise and have mercy on Zion at the set time, and will hear the groans of the prisoner, so that a people yet to be created will praise him (102:12–22); `messianic-prophecy` — "But you are the same. Your years will have no end" (102:25–27), the words Hebrews 1:10–12 later applies to the Son — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 103
**Summary:** David stirs his own soul to praise: "Praise the LORD, my soul! All that is within me, praise his holy name!" — and then lists the benefits not to be forgotten: sins forgiven, diseases healed, life redeemed from destruction, a crown of loving kindness, youth renewed like the eagle's. At the center stands the LORD's self-revealed character — "merciful and gracious, slow to anger, and abundant in loving kindness" — who "has not dealt with us according to our sins," who removes transgressions as far as the east is from the west, and who has a father's compassion on those who fear him, because "He remembers that we are dust." Human days pass like a flower of the field, but the LORD's loving kindness is from everlasting to everlasting; the psalm ends summoning angels, armies, and all his works to praise, before returning to where it began: "Praise the LORD, my soul!"
**Tags:** `praise` — the frame and the summons: "Praise the LORD, my soul!" widening to angels, armies, and all his works (103:1–2, 20–22); `gods-love` — "The LORD is merciful and gracious, slow to anger, and abundant in loving kindness," high as the heavens, tender as a father's compassion (103:8, 11, 13, 17); `forgiveness-of-sins` — "who forgives all your sins," and "As far as the east is from the west, so far has he removed our transgressions from us" (103:3, 12; existing anchor); `grace-not-earned` — "He has not dealt with us according to our sins, nor repaid us for our iniquities" (103:10); `pastoral-prayer-for-healing` — "don't forget all his benefits… who heals all your diseases" (103:2–3; existing anchor); `restoration` — "who redeems your life from destruction… so that your youth is renewed like the eagle's" (103:4–5; existing anchor); `slow-to-anger` — the Exodus 34 formula sounded again: "The LORD is merciful and gracious, slow to anger, and abundant in loving kindness" (103:8); `mortality` — the dust-frame of all that mercy: "He remembers that we are dust," and "As for man, his days are like grass" — the flower the wind passes over and its place remembers no more (103:14–16)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 104
**Summary:** A creation hymn that walks the whole world with its Maker: the LORD is clothed with honor and majesty, covered with light as with a garment, making the clouds his chariot and walking on the wings of the wind. The psalm tours his works in order — waters rebuked to their assigned places, springs for the wild donkeys, grass for the livestock, wine and oil and bread for man, cedars for the birds, high mountains for the wild goats, the moon for seasons, night for the prowling lions, day for man's labor, and the great wide sea where ships go and leviathan plays. All of them wait for God to give them their food in due season: "You open your hand; they are satisfied with good," and when he sends out his Spirit, the face of the ground is renewed. The singer's response is the psalm's own: "LORD, how many are your works! In wisdom, you have made them all" — and a vow to sing to the LORD as long as he lives.
**Tags:** `creation` — the world made and ordered end to end: "He laid the foundations of the earth," and "LORD, how many are your works! In wisdom, you have made them all" (104:5, 24); `gods-provision` — food from the earth for animal and man, "wine that makes the heart of man glad… You open your hand; they are satisfied with good" (104:14–15, 27–28); `providence` — the ongoing government of everything: seasons appointed, breath given and taken away, the ground renewed by his Spirit (104:19–23, 27–30); `praise` — "I will sing to the LORD as long as I live… Bless the LORD, my soul. Praise the LORD!" (104:33–35); `design-in-creation` — the creation-psalm's summary verse after its long tour of the made world: "LORD, how many are your works! In wisdom, you have made them all" (104:24; the in-wisdom orderedness register beside `creation`'s making register)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 105
**Summary:** A call to give thanks and make the LORD's doings known, addressed to the offspring of Abraham — because the LORD "has remembered his covenant forever," the oath sworn to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob promising the land of Canaan. The psalm retells the story as the covenant's long keeping: the patriarchs protected as wandering foreigners ("Don't touch my anointed ones!"), Joseph sent ahead of the famine and raised from shackles to rule Egypt, Israel multiplied there, Moses and Aaron sent with wonders — darkness, blood, frogs, flies and lice, hail, locusts, and the striking of the firstborn, as the psalm itself orders them. God brought his people out with silver and gold, spread a cloud, brought quails and the bread of the sky, and opened the rock — "For he remembered his holy word, and Abraham, his servant." The gift of the land lands on the psalm's final purpose: "that they might keep his statutes, and observe his laws. Praise the LORD!"
**Tags:** `covenant` — "He has remembered his covenant forever… the covenant which he made with Abraham, his oath to Isaac," confirmed to Jacob and Israel (105:8–11); `gods-faithfulness` — the whole retold history as promise-keeping: "For he remembered his holy word, and Abraham, his servant" (105:8, 42); `providence` — famine called for and a man sent ahead: "He sent a man before them. Joseph was sold for a slave," proved true at the set time (105:16–22); `thanksgiving` — "Give thanks to the LORD! Call on his name! Make his doings known among the peoples" (105:1–2); `remembrance-and-memorials` — remembering practiced as praise: "Remember his marvelous works" (105:5) — the whole psalm then performs that remembering, retelling the covenant's long keeping to the offspring of Abraham
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 106
**Summary:** Book IV's closing psalm opens with praise and a plea — "Remember me, LORD" — then turns into Israel's long confession: "We have sinned with our fathers. We have committed iniquity. We have done wickedly." The catalog runs from the Red Sea to Canaan: rebellion at the sea yet a rescue for his name's sake; craving in the desert and leanness of soul; envy of Moses and Aaron; the calf at Horeb — "they exchanged their glory for an image of a bull that eats grass" — despising the pleasant land; Baal Peor; Meribah; and finally mixing with the nations, serving idols, and sacrificing their own children. Judgment answers each turn — Moses stands in the breach to turn away wrath, and Phinehas stands up and executes judgment — yet "Nevertheless he regarded their distress, when he heard their cry" — he remembered for them his covenant and "repented according to the multitude of his loving kindnesses" — the psalm's own word for God turning from wrath. The psalm ends praying from scattering — "gather us from among the nations" — and closes Book IV with its doxology: "Blessed be the LORD, the God of Israel, from everlasting even to everlasting!"
**Tags:** `sin` — the national confession, itemized for generations: "We have sinned with our fathers. We have committed iniquity. We have done wickedly" (106:6–39); `repentance` — the psalm is itself the act of owning that history before God, praying from inside its consequences (106:6, 47); `gods-faithfulness` — "Nevertheless he saved them for his name's sake," and again "Nevertheless he regarded their distress" (106:8, 44–46); `covenant` — "He remembered for them his covenant, and repented according to the multitude of his loving kindnesses" (106:45); `divine-judgment` — the earth swallowing Dathan, fire in the company, the plague, the oath of overthrow, the hand of the nations (106:17–18, 26–27, 29, 40–42); `prayer` — "Remember me, LORD… Save us, LORD, our God, gather us from among the nations" (106:4–5, 47); `restoration-of-israel` — the confession's destination, prayed from scattering: "gather us from among the nations" (106:47)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 107
**Summary:** Book V opens with a summons: "Give thanks to the LORD, for he is good… Let the redeemed by the LORD say so." Four scenes of trouble follow, each resolving on the same two refrains — "Then they cried to the LORD in their trouble," and "Let them praise the LORD for his loving kindness": desert wanderers, hungry and thirsty, led to a city; prisoners in darkness and iron because they rebelled, their chains broken away and the gates of bronze broken; fools afflicted by their own disobedience, healed by his sent word at the gates of death; and sailors at their wits' end in a storm he stills to a calm, glad as he brings them to their desired haven. A coda widens the lens: the LORD turns rivers to desert for wickedness and desert to springs for the hungry, pours contempt on princes, and lifts the needy out of affliction. The last verse hands the whole psalm to the reader: "Whoever is wise will pay attention to these things. They will consider the loving kindnesses of the LORD."
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — "Give thanks to the LORD, for he is good," and the fourfold "Let them praise the LORD for his loving kindness" (107:1–2, 8, 15, 21–22, 31); `prayer` — the psalm's hinge, four times: "Then they cried to the LORD in their trouble, and he delivered them out of their distresses" (107:6, 13, 19, 28); `gods-provision` — "For he satisfies the longing soul. He fills the hungry soul with good," and the hungry settled to sow fields and plant vineyards (107:9, 35–38); `providence` — rivers turned to desert and desert to pools, princes humbled, the needy raised — the reversals traced to the LORD's hand (107:33–41)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 108
**Summary:** A psalm by David that opens in song before dawn: "My heart is steadfast, God… Wake up, harp and lyre! I will wake up the dawn," with thanks promised among the nations because God's loving kindness is great above the heavens. (Its two halves reappear, with small variations, from Psalms 57 and 60 — praise first, then the plea of a nation at war.) God's sanctuary-word claims Shechem, Succoth, Gilead, Manasseh, Ephraim, and Judah — and Moab, Edom, and Philistia besides — yet the present question stings: "Haven't you rejected us, God? You don't go out, God, with our armies." The psalm ends by planting its weight on the only help that isn't vain: "Through God, we will do valiantly, for it is he who will tread down our enemies."
**Tags:** `praise` — "I will sing and I will make music with my soul… I will sing praises to you among the peoples" (108:1–4); `prayer` — "save with your right hand, and answer us… Give us help against the enemy" (108:6, 12); `trust-in-god` — "for the help of man is vain. Through God, we will do valiantly" (108:12–13)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 109
**Summary:** David — "God of my praise, don't remain silent" — is under attack by lying tongues: surrounded with words of hatred, fought without a cause, rewarded evil for good and hatred for his love; his answer is "but I am in prayer." What he prays next is the Psalter's severest imprecation: a curse-list asking that his accuser be judged guilty, his days few, his office taken by another, his children fatherless, his name blotted out — the cursing the man loved returned on him like a garment. The psalm asks this of God rather than taking it: David himself is "poor and needy," wounded in heart, fading like an evening shadow, mocked by head-shaking onlookers, and his plea is "Help me, LORD, my God. Save me according to your loving kindness." He ends in promised thanksgiving, on the ground the psalm has been standing on all along: the LORD "will stand at the right hand of the needy, to save him from those who judge his soul." (Acts 1:20 later applies verse 8 to Judas' vacated office — a New Testament application the reader should know.)
**Tags:** `prayer` — "In return for my love, they are my adversaries; but I am in prayer," and "Help me, LORD, my God" (109:4, 26–28); `divine-judgment` — the imprecations are asked of the Judge, not enacted: "This is the reward of my adversaries from the LORD" (109:6–20); `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — the poor and needy man, broken in heart, with God stationed at his right hand: "he will stand at the right hand of the needy" (109:16, 22, 31); `thanksgiving` — "I will give great thanks to the LORD with my mouth. Yes, I will praise him among the multitude" (109:30); `slander-and-false-accusation` — the attack is verbal from the first line: lying tongues, words of hatred, a fight without cause, and the accuser's cursing worn like a garment (109:2–5, 20); `betrayal` — "In return for my love, they are my adversaries" — evil rewarded for good, and hatred for love (109:4–5); `vengeance` — the Psalter's severest imprecation, the curse-list of 109:6–20, is asked of God rather than taken: "This is the reward of my adversaries from the LORD" (109:6–20)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 110
**Summary:** A psalm by David of an enthronement he only reports: "The LORD says to my Lord, 'Sit at my right hand, until I make your enemies your footstool for your feet.'" From Zion the LORD sends out the rod of this lord's strength — rule among your enemies, a people offering themselves willingly in holy array — and then adds an oath he will not retract: "You are a priest forever in the order of Melchizedek," a king-priest after the pattern of Genesis 14. The final verses turn to battle: the Lord at his right hand will crush kings in the day of his wrath, judge among the nations, and, drinking of the brook on the way, lift up his head. This is the psalm the New Testament quotes more than any other — Jesus himself poses its riddle of David's "Lord" (Matthew 22:41–45), and Hebrews builds on the Melchizedek oath (Hebrews 5–7) — applications the reader should know; the psalm's own text speaks of David's Lord, enthroned and priest forever.
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — "He will crush kings in the day of his wrath. He will judge among the nations" (110:5–6); `messianic-prophecy` — the psalm the New Testament quotes more than any other: "Sit at my right hand, until I make your enemies your footstool for your feet" and the oath "You are a priest forever in the order of Melchizedek" (110:1, 4), posed by Jesus as the riddle of David's "Lord" (Matthew 22:41–45) and built on by Hebrews 5–7 — reported as later applications, exactly as the summary signposts. (`divine-judgment` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary; the messianic register, logged then to the messianic-prophecy row, is carried by that concept — adopted 2026-08-25 — above. The enthronement register remains logged on the god-reigns row's refs.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 111
**Summary:** An untitled hymn of whole-hearted thanks, sung "in the council of the upright, and in the congregation" — "The LORD's works are great, pondered by all those who delight in them," and remembered, because he has caused them to be remembered. The works reveal the worker: gracious and merciful, feeding those who fear him, always remembering his covenant, showing his people his power by giving them the heritage of the nations. His precepts share his character — sure, established forever, done in truth and uprightness — and he has sent redemption to his people and ordained his covenant forever: "His name is holy and awesome!" The psalm ends where wisdom begins: "The fear of the LORD is the beginning of wisdom," and his praise endures forever.
**Tags:** `praise` — the LORD's works and name praised in the congregation: "His praise endures forever!" (111:1–4, 10); `thanksgiving` — "I will give thanks to the LORD with my whole heart" (111:1); `covenant` — "He always remembers his covenant… He has ordained his covenant forever" (111:5, 9); `wisdom-from-god` — "The fear of the LORD is the beginning of wisdom. All those who do his work have a good understanding" (111:10); `fear-of-the-lord` — the psalm's landing: "The fear of the LORD is the beginning of wisdom," praised of a God who gives food to those who fear him (111:5, 10); `trustworthiness-of-scripture` — the works reveal reliable words: his precepts sure, established forever, done in truth and uprightness (111:7–8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 112
**Summary:** The companion portrait to Psalm 111: "Blessed is the man who fears the LORD, who delights greatly in his commandments." The psalm paints the God-fearer's life as blessing spreading outward — mighty offspring, wealth and righteousness in his house, light dawning in his darkness — presented as the shape of a life under God's favor, not a formula owed to anyone. This man deals graciously and lends, disperses and gives to the poor, and is not afraid of evil news: "His heart is steadfast, trusting in the LORD." The wicked man watches it, grieves, gnashes his teeth, and melts away — his desire perishes, while the righteous will be remembered forever. (Paul later quotes verse 9's generosity of the righteous when urging cheerful giving, 2 Corinthians 9:9.)
**Tags:** `blessing` — "Blessed is the man who fears the LORD… The generation of the upright will be blessed" (112:1–3); `generosity` — "It is well with the man who deals graciously and lends," and "He has dispersed, he has given to the poor" (112:5, 9); `trust-in-god` — "His heart is steadfast, trusting in the LORD" (112:7); `fear-not` — a fearlessness with a stated ground: "He will not be afraid of evil news… He will not be afraid in the end" (112:7–8); `fear-of-the-lord` — the whole portrait's subject: "Blessed is the man who fears the LORD, who delights greatly in his commandments" (112:1)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 113
**Summary:** The praise of the LORD's name from sunrise to sunset: his servants are summoned to praise a God who is high above all nations, his glory above the heavens — and whose greatness is proved by how far down he looks. "Who is like the LORD, our God, who has his seat on high, who stoops down to see in heaven and on the earth?" The psalm's answer is his signature move: he raises up the poor out of the dust and lifts up the needy from the ash heap, "that he may set him with princes," and settles the barren woman in her home "as a joyful mother of children. Praise the LORD!"
**Tags:** `praise` — "Praise, you servants of the LORD… From the rising of the sun to its going down, the LORD's name is to be praised" (113:1–3); `humble-exaltation` — "He raises up the poor out of the dust, and lifts up the needy from the ash heap, that he may set him with princes" (113:7–8); `waiting-for-a-child` — "He settles the barren woman in her home as a joyful mother of children" (113:9; existing anchor); `god-reigns` — enthroned height that stoops: "Who is like the LORD, our God, who has his seat on high, who stoops down to see in heaven and on the earth?" — high above all nations, his glory above the heavens (113:4–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 114
**Summary:** The Exodus retold as the world's startle: when Israel left Egypt and Judah became God's sanctuary, the sea saw and fled, the Jordan turned back, and the mountains skipped like rams. The psalm teases its own scenery with questions — "What was it, you sea, that you fled?" — and then gives the answer as a command to the whole earth: "Tremble, you earth, at the presence of the Lord, at the presence of the God of Jacob," the God who turned rock into a pool of water and flint into a spring.
**Tags:** `presence-of-god` — the psalm's own explanation for fleeing seas and skipping mountains: "Tremble, you earth, at the presence of the Lord" (114:7–8). (Only one honest tag from the current vocabulary.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 115
**Summary:** "Not to us, LORD, not to us, but to your name give glory" — a psalm that answers the nations' taunt "Where is their God, now?" with a double portrait. Israel's God is in the heavens and does whatever he pleases; the nations' idols are silver and gold with mouths that don't speak, eyes that don't see, ears, noses, hands, and feet that do nothing — "Those who make them will be like them." Against that deadness comes a threefold call — Israel, house of Aaron, all who fear the LORD: "trust in the LORD! He is their help and their shield" — met by a threefold assurance that the LORD remembers and will bless. The heavens are the LORD's heavens, but he has given the earth to the children of men; the dead don't praise him, "but we will bless the LORD, from this time forward and forever more."
**Tags:** `trust-in-god` — the triple summons: "Israel, trust in the LORD!… House of Aaron, trust in the LORD!… You who fear the LORD, trust in the LORD!" (115:9–11); `worship` — glory to the LORD's name alone, set against the made and mute idols of the nations (115:1–8); `blessing` — "The LORD remembers us. He will bless us… He will bless those who fear the LORD, both small and great" (115:12–15); `praise` — "we will bless the LORD, from this time forward and forever more. Praise the LORD!" (115:17–18); `idolatry` — one of the Psalter's fullest idol polemics: mouths that don't speak, eyes that don't see — "Those who make them will be like them" — against the God in the heavens who does whatever he pleases (115:3–8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 116
**Summary:** A rescued man's love song: "I love the LORD, because he listens to my voice" — and because when the cords of death surrounded him and the pains of Sheol got a hold of him, he called, "LORD, I beg you, deliver my soul," and the gracious LORD saved him. Now he talks to his own soul — "Return to your rest, my soul, for the LORD has dealt bountifully with you" — for his soul was delivered from death, his eyes from tears, his feet from falling, and he will walk before the LORD in the land of the living. The question "What will I give to the LORD for all his benefits?" is answered in public worship: the cup of salvation taken, vows paid before all God's people, the sacrifice of thanksgiving offered in the courts of the LORD's house. At the psalm's center stands its costliest line: "Precious in the LORD's sight is the death of his saints."
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — "What will I give to the LORD for all his benefits toward me?… I will offer to you the sacrifice of thanksgiving" (116:12–14, 17–19); `prayer` — "he listens to my voice, and my cries for mercy… Then I called on the LORD's name" (116:1–2, 4); `pastoral-serious-illness` — a personal brush with death prayed through: "The cords of death surrounded me," and "Precious in the LORD's sight is the death of his saints" (116:3, 8, 15; existing anchor); `rest-for-the-weary` — "Return to your rest, my soul, for the LORD has dealt bountifully with you" (116:7); `loving-god` — the pray-er's own declaration: "I love the LORD, because he listens to my voice, and my cries for mercy" (116:1–2); `oaths-and-vows` — the rescued man's vows paid in public: "I will pay my vows to the LORD, yes, in the presence of all his people" (116:14, 18)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 117
**Summary:** The Psalter's shortest psalm — two verses that reach the whole world: "Praise the LORD, all you nations! Extol him, all you peoples!" The reason is the pair of attributes the Psalter never tires of: "For his loving kindness is great toward us. The LORD's faithfulness endures forever." (Paul later quotes verse 1 as Scripture's own summons of the Gentiles to glorify God, Romans 15:11.)
**Tags:** `praise` — "Praise the LORD, all you nations!… Praise the LORD!" (117:1–2); `nations-and-peoples` — every nation and people summoned to Israel's God (117:1); `gods-faithfulness` — "The LORD's faithfulness endures forever" (117:2)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 118
**Summary:** A processional thanksgiving framed by one refrain: "Give thanks to the LORD, for he is good, for his loving kindness endures forever" — with Israel, the house of Aaron, and all who fear the LORD told to say so. The singer's testimony drives the middle: out of distress he called and was answered with freedom; "The LORD is on my side. I will not be afraid. What can man do to me?"; surrounded like bees, pushed hard, punished severely but not given over to death — "I will not die, but live, and declare the LORD's works." At the gates of righteousness the psalm turns to its famous image: "The stone which the builders rejected has become the cornerstone. This is the LORD's doing" — then to the day the LORD has made, the cry "Save us now, we beg you, LORD!" and the blessing on "he who comes in the LORD's name." The Gospels place these very lines on Palm Sunday's lips and Jesus applies the cornerstone to himself (Matthew 21:9, 42; also Acts 4:11; 1 Peter 2:7) — later applications the reader should know; in the psalm they belong to the delivered singer and the festal procession, binding the sacrifice with cords to the horns of the altar.
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — the frame: "Give thanks to the LORD, for he is good," opening and closing the psalm (118:1–4, 21, 28–29); `refuge-in-trouble` — "It is better to take refuge in the LORD, than to put confidence in man… than to put confidence in princes" (118:8–9); `fear-not` — "The LORD is on my side. I will not be afraid. What can man do to me?" (118:6); `joy-in-the-lord` — "This is the day that the LORD has made. We will rejoice and be glad in it!" (118:24); `christ-the-cornerstone` — the psalm's own cornerstone saying, which that locator concept's pack lists among its source texts: "The stone which the builders rejected has become the cornerstone" (118:22–23). (A PR #43 id, used as a reversible delegated default — see the Decisions record. *2026-08-25: ratified by Jesse 2026-08-25 — no longer reversible-pending-review.*); `messianic-prophecy` — the Palm Sunday lines and the cornerstone: "The stone which the builders rejected has become the cornerstone" and the blessing on "he who comes in the LORD's name" (118:22–26), placed on Palm Sunday's lips and applied by Jesus to himself (Matthew 21:9, 42; Acts 4:11; 1 Peter 2:7) — reported as later applications, exactly as the summary signposts
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 119
**Summary:** The Psalter's longest psalm is a 176-verse acrostic love poem about God's word: twenty-two stanzas of eight verses, one for each letter of the Hebrew alphabet, nearly every verse naming the word under one of its titles — law, testimonies, precepts, statutes, commandments, ordinances, word, promise. It opens with blessing on those whose ways are blameless and becomes one long braided prayer: teach me, open my eyes, revive me, incline my heart — prayed by a sufferer who is small and despised, slandered by princes, persecuted without cause, and who answers affliction by loving the law more: "It is good for me that I have been afflicted, that I may learn your statutes." Its best-known lines carry the psalm's whole theology — "I have hidden your word in my heart, that I might not sin against you"; "Your word is a lamp to my feet, and a light for my path"; "All of your words are truth" — and it closes, after 175 verses of devotion, with the psalmist still a sheep needing its shepherd: "I have gone astray like a lost sheep. Seek your servant, for I don't forget your commandments."
**Tags:** `delight-in-the-word` — "I will delight myself in your statutes… How I love your law!
