It is my meditation all day," sweeter than honey, better than gold (119:16, 47, 97, 103, 111); `studying-the-word` — sustained meditation as a practice: "I will meditate on your precepts, and consider your ways," through midnight risings and night watches (119:15, 48, 62, 97–99, 148); `obedience-to-the-word` — "I will observe your statutes… I will hurry, and not delay, to obey your commandments," sworn and kept under pressure (119:8, 57–60, 106, 112, 167–168); `guidance` — "Your word is a lamp to my feet, and a light for my path," and "Establish my footsteps in your word" (119:105, 133; existing anchor); `hope-in-god` — "You are my hiding place and my shield. I hope in your word," the refrain of hope set on what God has said (119:43, 49, 74, 81, 114, 147); `prayer` — the whole psalm is petition: "Teach me, LORD… Revive me according to your word… Let my cry come before you" (119:12, 25, 145–149, 169–170); `trustworthiness-of-scripture` — the reliability claim prayed throughout: "All of your words are truth," a word "settled in heaven forever," his statutes righteous and fully trustworthy, his promises thoroughly tested (119:86, 89–91, 138, 140, 142, 151, 160); `slander-and-false-accusation` — the psalmist prays as one slandered by princes and persecuted without cause, smeared by the proud yet keeping the precepts (119:23, 69, 161)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings
**Sections:**
- **119:1–8 — Aleph** (title: BSB) — Blessing rests on those whose ways are blameless, who seek God with a whole heart; the psalmist longs for that steadfastness and vows to keep the statutes he is still learning.
- **119:9–16 — Beth** (title: BSB) — A young man keeps his way pure by living according to God's word; the psalmist hides that word in his heart, rejoices in it as in all riches, and will not forget it.
- **119:17–24 — Gimel** (title: BSB) — A stranger on the earth prays for opened eyes to see wondrous things out of the law; though princes sit and slander him, the statutes remain his delight and his counselors.
- **119:25–32 — Daleth** (title: BSB) — With his soul laid low in the dust, the psalmist asks to be revived according to God's word; having chosen the way of truth, he runs the path of the commandments with a freed heart.
- **119:33–40 — He** (title: BSB) — A stanza of teach-me petitions: direct me in the path, turn my heart from selfish gain and my eyes from worthless things, and revive me in your righteousness.
- **119:41–48 — Waw** (title: BSB) — When God's loving kindness and salvation come, the psalmist will have an answer for his reproacher; he will walk in liberty, speak of the statutes before kings, and reach out his hands for the commandments he loves.
- **119:49–56 — Zayin** (title: BSB) — "Remember your word to your servant": God's word is the sufferer's comfort and life, his songs in the house where he lives, and his remembered name in the night.
- **119:57–64 — Heth** (title: BSB) — "The LORD is my portion": the psalmist turns his steps to the statutes, rises at midnight to give thanks, and befriends all who fear God, in an earth full of the LORD's loving kindness.
- **119:65–72 — Teth** (title: BSB) — Affliction reread as schooling: "Before I was afflicted, I went astray," and "It is good for me that I have been afflicted" — for the law of God's mouth is better than thousands of pieces of gold and silver.
- **119:73–80 — Yodh** (title: BSB) — The hands that made him can give him understanding; God's judgments are righteous and even the affliction was faithful, so he asks for comfort, tender mercies, and a blameless heart.
- **119:81–88 — Kaph** (title: BSB) — The stanza of fainting: soul and eyes fail waiting for salvation, "like a wineskin in the smoke," while the proud dig pits — yet he does not forsake the precepts, and asks to be preserved.
- **119:89–96 — Lamedh** (title: BSB) — "LORD, your word is settled in heaven forever": the God who established the earth keeps word and world alike — all things serve him — and "Unless your law had been my delight, I would have perished in my affliction."
- **119:97–104 — Mem** (title: BSB) — "How I love your law!" — meditation all day makes the psalmist wiser than enemies, teachers, and the aged, and God's promises are sweeter than honey to his mouth.
- **119:105–112 — Nun** (title: BSB) — "Your word is a lamp to my feet, and a light for my path": afflicted, his soul continually in his hand, he has sworn to obey and takes the testimonies as his heritage and heart's joy forever.
- **119:113–120 — Samekh** (title: BSB) — Loyalty against the double-minded: "You are my hiding place and my shield"; evildoers are told to depart, the straying are dross, and the psalmist's flesh trembles in awe of God's judgments.
- **119:121–128 — Ayin** (title: BSB) — The oppressed servant asks his God to guarantee his well-being: "It is time to act, LORD, for they break your law" — therefore he loves the commandments more than pure gold and hates every false way.
- **119:129–136 — Pe** (title: BSB) — "The entrance of your words gives light": the psalmist pants with longing for the commandments, asks for God's face to shine on him, and weeps streams of tears because the law is not observed.
- **119:137–144 — Tzade** (title: BSB) — "You are righteous, LORD": the statutes are righteous and fully trustworthy, the promises thoroughly tested; though he is small and despised, in trouble and anguish, the commandments are his delight.
- **119:145–152 — Koph** (title: BSB) — Whole-heart calling before dawn and through the night watches: persecutors draw near who are far from the law, "You are near, LORD," and all the commandments are truth.
- **119:153–160 — Resh** (title: BSB) — "Consider my affliction, and deliver me": plead my cause, revive me — the refrain prayed three times in this stanza — for "All of your words are truth" and every righteous ordinance endures forever.
- **119:161–168 — Sin and Shin** (title: BSB) — Persecuted by princes without cause, the psalmist stands in awe of God's words, rejoices over them like great plunder, praises seven times a day, and testifies: "Those who love your law have great peace."
- **119:169–176 — Taw** (title: BSB) — The closing plea: let my cry come before you, let my lips utter praise, let your hand help me — and the last word of the longest psalm: "I have gone astray like a lost sheep. Seek your servant."

## Psalms 120
**Summary:** The first Song of Ascents — the opening of the fifteen-psalm pilgrim collection (120–134) — starts far from home, in distress: "In my distress, I cried to the LORD. He answered me." The trouble is verbal: lying lips and a deceitful tongue, against which the psalmist asks deliverance, and for which he foresees the answer the psalm itself gives: "Sharp arrows of the mighty, with coals of juniper." He is worn out by his address: living in Meshech, dwelling among the tents of Kedar, too long at home with one who hates peace. The last line states the pilgrim's predicament in one bare contrast: "I am for peace, but when I speak, they are for war."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "In my distress, I cried to the LORD. He answered me" (120:1); `honesty` — the plea against a lying world: "Deliver my soul, LORD, from lying lips, from a deceitful tongue" (120:2–3); `lament` — the collection opens in distress: "In my distress, I cried to the LORD. He answered me," worn out by a hostile address (120:1, 5–6); `slander-and-false-accusation` — the trouble is verbal: "Deliver my soul, LORD, from lying lips, from a deceitful tongue" (120:2–3); `sojourners-and-strangers` — the pilgrim far from home, living in Meshech and dwelling among the tents of Kedar, too long with one who hates peace: "I am for peace, but when I speak, they are for war" (120:5–7)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 121
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents that begins with lifted eyes and a traveler's question: "I will lift up my eyes to the hills. Where does my help come from?" The answer names the maker of heaven and earth, then hands the singer over to a second voice of assurance that repeats one word — keeps, keeper, keep — six times: he will not allow your foot to be moved; he who keeps Israel neither slumbers nor sleeps; the LORD is your keeper and your shade; sun and moon will not harm you. The keeping is total and unending: "The LORD will keep you from all evil. He will keep your soul… your going out and your coming in, from this time forward, and forever more."
**Tags:** `gods-protection` — the keeper psalm itself: "He who keeps you will not slumber… The LORD will keep you from all evil" (121:3–8); `refuge-in-trouble` — help and shelter located in the LORD alone, "your shade on your right hand" (121:1–8; existing anchor); `trust-in-god` — the question answered with confidence: "My help comes from the LORD, who made heaven and earth" (121:1–2)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 122
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents by David that begins with shared gladness — "I was glad when they said to me, 'Let's go to the LORD's house!'" — and arrives with feet standing in Jerusalem's gates. The city is compact and purposeful: the place where the tribes go up together, by ordinance, to give thanks to the LORD's name, and where the thrones of judgment of David's house are set. The pilgrim's response is intercession: "Pray for the peace of Jerusalem," peace within her walls and prosperity in her palaces — sought for the sake of brothers, companions, and the house of the LORD our God.
**Tags:** `worship` — going up to give thanks at the LORD's house: "Let's go to the LORD's house!" and the tribes ascending "according to an ordinance for Israel" (122:1, 4); `gathering-together` — worship as a company: glad together at the summons, feet together in the gates, tribes up together (122:1–4); `prayer` — "Pray for the peace of Jerusalem… I will seek your good" (122:6–9); `zion-city-of-god` — the pilgrim arrives in the city itself: feet standing in Jerusalem's gates, the compact city where the tribes go up, with peace prayed over her walls and palaces (122:2–9)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 123
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents prayed entirely with the eyes: "I lift up my eyes to you, you who sit in the heavens." The psalm's one image carries it — as servants' eyes look to the hand of their master, and a maid's to the hand of her mistress, "so our eyes look to the LORD, our God, until he has mercy on us." The waiting is not comfortable: the community has endured much contempt, its soul filled to the brim with the scoffing of those at ease and the contempt of the proud — which is exactly what drives the doubled plea, "Have mercy on us, LORD, have mercy on us."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Have mercy on us, LORD, have mercy on us, for we have endured much contempt" (123:3); `hope-in-god` — eyes fixed like a servant's on the master's hand, "until he has mercy on us" (123:1–2); `lament` — a community's complaint of contempt: "Have mercy on us, LORD, have mercy on us, for we have endured much contempt," souls filled with the scoffing of those at ease (123:3–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 124
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents by David that runs history backward through one conditional: "If it had not been the LORD who was on our side" — twice, with Israel told to say it — then men's kindled wrath would have swallowed us up alive, and the proud waters would have gone over our soul. Because the LORD was on Israel's side, the psalm turns to blessing: "Blessed be the LORD, who has not given us as a prey to their teeth." The escape is pictured small and glad — a bird out of the fowler's broken snare — and the psalm ends in the collection's recurring confession: "Our help is in the LORD's name, who made heaven and earth."
**Tags:** `gods-protection` — deliverance told twice over: "If it had not been the LORD who was on our side," and "Our soul has escaped like a bird out of the fowler's snare" (124:1–7); `praise` — "Blessed be the LORD, who has not given us as a prey to their teeth" (124:6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 125
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents about unmovable people: "Those who trust in the LORD are as Mount Zion, which can't be moved, but remains forever." The geography preaches — as the mountains surround Jerusalem, so the LORD surrounds his people, from this time forward and forever more — and the psalm adds a promise with a pastoral reason: the scepter of wickedness won't remain over the allotment of the righteous, so that the righteous won't be pushed toward doing evil themselves. It closes with a two-way prayer — do good to the good and upright in heart; but those who turn to crooked ways the LORD will lead away with the workers of iniquity — and a benediction-like final word: "Peace be on Israel."
**Tags:** `trust-in-god` — "Those who trust in the LORD are as Mount Zion, which can't be moved" (125:1); `gods-protection` — "As the mountains surround Jerusalem, so the LORD surrounds his people" (125:2)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 126
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents remembering a restoration almost too good to be true: "When the LORD brought back those who returned to Zion, we were like those who dream" — mouths filled with laughter, tongues with singing, and even the nations saying, "The LORD has done great things for them." Israel agrees: "The LORD has done great things for us, and we are glad." From that memory the psalm prays for the next restoration — "Restore our fortunes again, LORD, like the streams in the Negev" — and closes with its sowing promise: "Those who sow in tears will reap in joy" — the weeping sower who carries seed will certainly come again with joy, carrying his sheaves.
**Tags:** `restoration` — "When the LORD brought back those who returned to Zion," and the prayer "Restore our fortunes again, LORD" (126:1, 4); `joy-in-the-lord` — laughter and singing at what the LORD has done, and the promise "Those who sow in tears will reap in joy" (126:2–3, 5–6); `restoration-of-israel` — the national register of the psalm's memory and prayer: the LORD "brought back those who returned to Zion," and the community asks the same again — "Restore our fortunes again, LORD, like the streams in the Negev" (126:1, 4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 127
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents by Solomon on what only the LORD can make succeed: "Unless the LORD builds the house, they who build it labor in vain. Unless the LORD watches over the city, the watchman guards it in vain." Anxious overwork gets the same verdict — vain to rise early and stay up late eating the bread of toil, "for he gives sleep to his loved ones." The psalm then names the house's true wealth: children are a heritage of the LORD, the fruit of the womb his reward — like arrows in a mighty man's hand, so that the man with a full quiver is happy, not disappointed when he speaks with enemies in the gate.
**Tags:** `providence` — every human enterprise dependent on the LORD's building and watching: "Unless the LORD builds the house, they who build it labor in vain" (127:1); `rest-for-the-weary` — the verdict on anxious toil: "It is vain for you to rise up early, to stay up late… for he gives sleep to his loved ones" (127:2; existing anchor); `parenting` — "Behold, children are a heritage of the LORD. The fruit of the womb is his reward" (127:3–5; existing anchor); `blessing` — the happy man whose quiver is full — household good received as the LORD's gift (127:3, 5)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 128
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents pronouncing blessing on "everyone who fears the LORD, who walks in his ways" — drawn as a household scene: eating the labor of your own hands, a wife like a fruitful vine in the innermost parts of the house, children like olive shoots around the table. The psalm presents this as the shape of the LORD's blessing on the God-fearing life — "Behold, this is how the man who fears the LORD is blessed" — a covenant picture, not a bargain. It ends as a spoken blessing over the pilgrim: may the LORD bless you out of Zion, may you see the good of Jerusalem all your days and your children's children — "Peace be upon Israel."
**Tags:** `blessing` — "Blessed is everyone who fears the LORD… Behold, this is how the man who fears the LORD is blessed" (128:1–4); `benediction` — a blessing pronounced over the worshiper: "May the LORD bless you out of Zion… may you see your children's children" (128:5–6). (A PR #43 id, used as a reversible delegated default — see the Decisions record. *2026-08-25: ratified by Jesse 2026-08-25 — no longer reversible-pending-review.*); `fear-of-the-lord` — the beatitude's subject from first line to last: blessing pronounced on "everyone who fears the LORD, who walks in his ways," and "Behold, this is how the man who fears the LORD is blessed" (128:1–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 129
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents in which Israel is told to say its own survivor's testimony: "many times they have afflicted me from my youth up, yet they have not prevailed against me." The affliction is drawn with a farming image turned brutal — plowers plowed on my back and made their furrows long — and the deliverance with the same tools: "The LORD is righteous. He has cut apart the cords of the wicked." The psalm then prays against all who hate Zion: let them be like housetop grass that withers before it grows up, filling no reaper's hand — never earning the harvest blessing passersby speak over a field, "The blessing of the LORD be on you."
**Tags:** `gods-protection` — a scarred but standing people: "yet they have not prevailed against me," because "The LORD is righteous. He has cut apart the cords of the wicked" (129:2, 4); `justice-and-oppression` — national affliction survived under a righteous Judge: "many times they have afflicted me from my youth up," plowers on Israel's back — and "The LORD is righteous. He has cut apart the cords of the wicked" (129:1–4). (`gods-protection` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary; the national affliction-and-justice theme, logged then as a vocabulary gap, is carried by the concept adopted 2026-08-25.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 130
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents from the bottom: "Out of the depths I have cried to you, LORD" — a cry for attentive ears that immediately concedes the real problem: "If you, LORD, kept a record of sins, Lord, who could stand?" The pivot is one of Scripture's great "but"s: "But there is forgiveness with you, therefore you are feared." What follows is the posture forgiveness makes possible — "I wait for the LORD. My soul waits. I hope in his word," longing for the Lord more than watchmen long for the morning — and the psalm ends by turning private waiting into public counsel: Israel, hope in the LORD, for with him is loving kindness and abundant redemption, and "He will redeem Israel from all their sins."
**Tags:** `forgiveness-of-sins` — "If you, LORD, kept a record of sins, Lord, who could stand? But there is forgiveness with you" (130:3–4); `hope-in-god` — "I wait for the LORD. My soul waits. I hope in his word… Israel, hope in the LORD" (130:5–7); `prayer` — "Out of the depths I have cried to you, LORD… Let your ears be attentive to the voice of my petitions" (130:1–2); `salvation` — "Abundant redemption is with him. He will redeem Israel from all their sins" (130:7–8); `lament` — the cry from the bottom: "Out of the depths I have cried to you, LORD" — complaint, confession, and watching for morning in one prayer (130:1–2, 5–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 131
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents by David, three verses of practiced smallness: his heart is not arrogant, his eyes not lofty, and he does not concern himself with great matters or things too wonderful for him. The psalm's one picture is hard-won calm: "Surely I have stilled and quieted my soul, like a weaned child with his mother" — a child no longer demanding, simply content to be with her. The quiet is then handed to the whole community as counsel: "Israel, hope in the LORD, from this time forward and forever more."
**Tags:** `contentment` — "I have stilled and quieted my soul, like a weaned child with his mother," and the refusal of matters too great (131:1–2); `peace-of-god` — a soul stilled and quieted rather than striving (131:2); `hope-in-god` — "Israel, hope in the LORD, from this time forward and forever more" (131:3)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 132
**Summary:** The longest Song of Ascents asks God to remember David and all his affliction — his sworn vow to find the LORD a dwelling before he would enter his own house or sleep — and relives the ark's homecoming: "we heard of it in Ephrathah… Arise, LORD, into your resting place, you, and the ark of your strength," with priests clothed with righteousness and saints shouting for joy. The center is the LORD's answering oath to David: "I will set the fruit of your body on your throne," conditioned for his sons on keeping the covenant — and grounded in the LORD's own choice: "For the LORD has chosen Zion. He has desired it for his habitation. 'This is my resting place forever.'" There he promises blessed provision, bread for the poor, salvation-clothed priests — and a future for the dynasty: "I will make the horn of David to bud there. I have ordained a lamp for my anointed," whose crown will shine while his enemies wear shame. (Peter later cites the throne oath as fulfilled in the resurrection of Christ, Acts 2:30 — a New Testament application beyond the psalm's own text.)
**Tags:** `covenant` — the sworn, two-sided center: "The LORD has sworn to David in truth… If your children will keep my covenant… their children also will sit on your throne" (132:11–12); `worship` — the vow for a dwelling and the procession to it: "We will go into his dwelling place. We will worship at his footstool" (132:2–5, 7); `gods-faithfulness` — the LORD's own chosen and promised future for Zion and David's line: "This is my resting place forever… I will make the horn of David to bud there" (132:13–18); `oaths-and-vows` — David's sworn vow to find the LORD a dwelling before entering his own house, answered by the LORD's own oath: "The LORD has sworn to David in truth" (132:2–5, 11); `zion-city-of-god` — the psalm's ground: "For the LORD has chosen Zion. He has desired it for his habitation. 'This is my resting place forever.'" (132:13–16); `messianic-prophecy` — the throne oath and the budding horn (132:11, 17), cited by Peter as fulfilled in the resurrection of Christ (Acts 2:30) — reported as the NT's application per the summary's signpost
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 133
**Summary:** A Song of Ascents by David in praise of unity: "See how good and how pleasant it is for brothers to live together in unity!" Two images carry the whole psalm, both of good things running down from above: the precious oil on the head, that ran down on the beard — Aaron's beard — and came down on the edge of his robes, and the dew of Hermon coming down on the hills of Zion. The final line locates the source: "for there the LORD gives the blessing, even life forever more."
**Tags:** `harmony-with-others` — the psalm of unity itself: "how good and how pleasant it is for brothers to live together in unity!" (133:1); `blessing` — unity as the place where "the LORD gives the blessing, even life forever more" (133:3)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 134
**Summary:** The last Song of Ascents is an exchange of blessings at the temple by night. The pilgrims call to the night shift — "Praise the LORD, all you servants of the LORD, who stand by night in the LORD's house!" — telling them to lift up their hands in the sanctuary and praise the LORD. The answer comes back over the departing worshiper as a blessing: "May the LORD bless you from Zion, even he who made heaven and earth" — the collection closing with heaven's maker blessing from the city the pilgrims came to.
**Tags:** `worship` — night-watch service in the sanctuary: "Lift up your hands in the sanctuary. Praise the LORD!" (134:1–2); `praise` — the summons to the LORD's servants to praise him (134:1–2); `benediction` — the returned blessing over the worshiper: "May the LORD bless you from Zion, even he who made heaven and earth" (134:3). (A PR #43 id, used as a reversible delegated default — see the Decisions record. *2026-08-25: ratified by Jesse 2026-08-25 — no longer reversible-pending-review.*)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 135
**Summary:** A praise psalm for the servants standing in the LORD's house: praise the LORD, for he is good, and sing to his name, for the LORD has chosen Jacob for himself. His greatness is argued from sovereignty — "Whatever the LORD pleased, that he has done, in heaven and in earth" — seen in clouds, lightning, and wind from his treasuries, and from history exactly as the psalm tells it: he struck Egypt's firstborn, sent signs on Pharaoh, struck many nations and killed mighty kings — Sihon, Og, and all the kingdoms of Canaan — and gave their land as a heritage to Israel. Against this stand the idols of the nations, silver and gold that cannot speak, see, hear, or breathe — "Those who make them will be like them" — and the psalm ends with the houses of Israel, Aaron, and Levi, and all who fear the LORD, blessing the LORD from Zion.
**Tags:** `praise` — "Praise the LORD, for the LORD is good. Sing praises to his name," through to "Blessed be the LORD from Zion" (135:1–3, 19–21); `providence` — "Whatever the LORD pleased, that he has done," in heaven, earth, seas, and storms (135:6–7); `worship` — the living God served in his house, set against the dead idols of the nations (135:1–2, 13–18); `idolatry` — the other of the Psalter's two fullest idol polemics: the nations' idols are silver and gold that cannot speak, see, hear, or breathe — "Those who make them will be like them" (135:15–18)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 136
**Summary:** The Psalter's great antiphon: twenty-six verses, every one answered by the same refrain, "for his loving kindness endures forever." The verses walk from who God is (the God of gods, the Lord of lords, the alone-doer of great wonders) through creation (the heavens made by understanding, the earth spread out, sun, moon, and stars set to rule) into the Exodus story as the psalm tells it — Egypt's firstborn struck, Israel brought out with strong hand and outstretched arm, the Red Sea divided, Pharaoh's army overthrown in it, the wilderness crossing, and great kings struck and mighty kings killed — Sihon and Og — their land given as a heritage to Israel his servant. The closing turn makes the history personal and universal at once: "who remembered us in our low estate… and has delivered us from our adversaries… who gives food to every creature" — so give thanks to the God of heaven.
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — the psalm's whole action: "Give thanks to the LORD, for he is good… Oh give thanks to the God of heaven" (136:1–3, 26); `gods-love` — the refrain itself, twenty-six times: "for his loving kindness endures forever" (136:1–26); `creation` — "to him who by understanding made the heavens… who made the great lights" (136:5–9); `gods-faithfulness` — mercy that reaches into the present tense: "who remembered us in our low estate… and has delivered us from our adversaries" (136:23–24); `gods-provision` — "who gives food to every creature" (136:25)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 137
**Summary:** The exiles' psalm: "By the rivers of Babylon, there we sat down. Yes, we wept, when we remembered Zion." Harps hang on the willows because captors demand entertainment — "Sing us one of the songs of Zion!" — and the captives answer with the psalm's aching question: "How can we sing the LORD's song in a foreign land?" What follows is fierce loyalty — "If I forget you, Jerusalem, let my right hand forget its skill" — and then the psalm's notorious ending: a plea that the LORD remember Edom's "Raze it!" on Jerusalem's day, and a beatitude over Babylon's destroyer, down to the dashing of Babylon's little ones against the rock. The text presents this as the raw voice of the violated addressing the judge — repayment measured by Babylon's own deeds, "as you have done to us" — spoken to God rather than enacted; the summary records it without softening it and without commending it beyond what the text itself does.
**Tags:** `divine-judgment` — judgment on Jerusalem's destroyers asked of and left with the LORD: "Remember, LORD, against the children of Edom," and "Daughter of Babylon, doomed to destruction" (137:7–9); `lament` — the exiles' communal lament: "By the rivers of Babylon, there we sat down. Yes, we wept, when we remembered Zion" — harps hung, songs demanded (137:1–4); `sojourners-and-strangers` — the captives' own question: "How can we sing the LORD's song in a foreign land?" (137:4); `zion-city-of-god` — Jerusalem held above the highest joy: "If I forget you, Jerusalem, let my right hand forget its skill" (137:1, 5–6); `vengeance` — the notorious ending's repayment plea, measured by Babylon's own deeds — "as you have done to us" — spoken to the Judge rather than enacted, exactly as the summary records it (137:7–9). (`divine-judgment` was this psalm's only honest tag from the pre-2026-08-25 vocabulary; the lament, exile, and imprecation registers, logged then as vocabulary-gap refs (see the Decisions record), are carried by the concepts adopted 2026-08-25.)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 138
**Summary:** David gives whole-hearted thanks "before the gods" — bowing toward God's holy temple for a loving kindness and truth in which God has exalted his Name and his Word above all. The testimony is timed: "In the day that I called, you answered me. You encouraged me with strength in my soul" — and it scales up: "All the kings of the earth will give you thanks, LORD, for they have heard the words of your mouth," and "they will sing of the ways of the LORD, for the LORD's glory is great!" The psalm's center of gravity is the LORD's strange altitude — "though the LORD is high, yet he looks after the lowly; but he knows the proud from afar" — and its close is walking confidence: in the middle of trouble you revive me; "The LORD will fulfill that which concerns me… Don't forsake the works of your own hands."
**Tags:** `thanksgiving` — "I will give you thanks with my whole heart… and give thanks to your Name for your loving kindness and for your truth" (138:1–2); `prayer` — "In the day that I called, you answered me. You encouraged me with strength in my soul" (138:3); `gods-faithfulness` — "The LORD will fulfill that which concerns me. Your loving kindness, LORD, endures forever" (138:2, 8); `humble-exaltation` — "though the LORD is high, yet he looks after the lowly; but he knows the proud from afar" (138:6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 139
**Summary:** David's meditation on being fully known: "LORD, you have searched me, and you know me" — sitting, rising, thoughts from afar, every word on his tongue known altogether — knowledge so lofty he cannot attain it. There is nowhere to outrun it: heaven, Sheol, the wings of the dawn, the uttermost sea, even self-chosen darkness — "even there your hand will lead me," and the night shines as the day to God. The knowing goes back before birth: "you formed my inmost being. You knit me together in my mother's womb… I am fearfully and wonderfully made," every ordained day written in God's book before one of them existed. After an abrupt, fierce turn against the bloodthirsty — David asking God to kill the wicked and declaring he hates those who hate God, a hatred laid out before the searcher of hearts rather than acted out — the psalm ends by volunteering for the very search it began with: "Search me, God, and know my heart… See if there is any wicked way in me, and lead me in the everlasting way."
**Tags:** `presence-of-god` — "Where could I go from your Spirit?… If I ascend up into heaven, you are there" (139:7–10; existing anchor); `creation` — "you formed my inmost being. You knit me together in my mother's womb… I am fearfully and wonderfully made" (139:13–14; existing anchor); `providence` — "In your book they were all written, the days that were ordained for me, when as yet there were none of them" (139:16; existing anchor); `pastoral-pregnancy-and-child-loss` — the unborn child seen, formed, and written in God's book (139:13–16; existing anchor — see the Decisions record); `testing` — the invited examination: "Search me, God, and know my heart. Try me, and know my thoughts" (139:23–24); `guidance` — "even there your hand will lead me," and "lead me in the everlasting way" (139:10, 24); `running-from-god` — the flight that cannot outrun him: "Where could I go from your Spirit? Or where could I flee from your presence?" — heaven, Sheol, the wings of the dawn, the uttermost sea, and self-chosen darkness all fail (139:7–12); `design-in-creation` — design brought down to the individual person: "For you formed my inmost being. You knit me together in my mother's womb." and "I am fearfully and wonderfully made" (139:13–14, fixture-verified; anchored for deliberate personal formation, not for any embryology claim — the map's caveat carried)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 140
**Summary:** David prays for rescue from violent men who devise mischief, gather for war, and sharpen their tongues like a serpent — "Viper's poison is under their lips" (words Paul later gathers into his indictment of humanity, Romans 3:13). The proud have hidden snares, cords, and traps by his path, so he plants his identity first — "I said to the LORD, 'You are my God'" — and remembers armor already worn: "you have covered my head in the day of battle." He asks God to refuse the wicked their desires and to let the mischief of their own lips cover them, burning coals and miry pits closing the boomerang arc. The ending is settled: "I know that the LORD will maintain the cause of the afflicted, and justice for the needy," and the upright will dwell in God's presence.
**Tags:** `gods-protection` — "LORD, keep me from the hands of the wicked… you have covered my head in the day of battle" (140:4, 7); `prayer` — "Listen to the cry of my petitions, LORD" (140:6); `pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — a hunted man's certainty: "I know that the LORD will maintain the cause of the afflicted, and justice for the needy" (140:12)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 141
**Summary:** An evening prayer by David: "Let my prayer be set before you like incense; the lifting up of my hands like the evening sacrifice." The petitions guard the praying man himself before they touch his enemies: set a watch before my mouth, keep the door of my lips; don't incline my heart to any evil thing or let me eat the delicacies of the wicked — and let the righteous strike me in kindness, a reproof received like oil on the head. Only then does the psalm face the snares: with eyes on the LORD — "I take refuge in you. Don't leave my soul destitute" — he asks to be kept from the traps of the workers of iniquity, and that the wicked fall together into their own nets while he passes by.
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Let my prayer be set before you like incense; the lifting up of my hands like the evening sacrifice" (141:1–2); `taming-the-tongue` — "Set a watch, LORD, before my mouth. Keep the door of my lips" (141:3; existing anchor); `refuge-in-trouble` — "For my eyes are on you, LORD, the Lord. I take refuge in you" (141:8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 142
**Summary:** "A contemplation by David, when he was in the cave" — prayer from a dead end. He cries with his voice to the LORD, pours out his complaint, and tells him his troubles; his spirit is overwhelmed, the path is snared, and the look to his right finds the worst of it: "there is no one who is concerned for me. Refuge has fled from me. No one cares for my soul." So the cry goes where refuge remains: "You are my refuge, my portion in the land of the living" — deliver me from persecutors too strong for me. The psalm ends asking to be brought "out of prison" for a purpose beyond escape: to give thanks to God's name, surrounded again by the righteous, "for you will be good to me."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "I cry with my voice to the LORD… I pour out my complaint before him. I tell him my troubles" (142:1–2); `loneliness` — "there is no one who is concerned for me… No one cares for my soul" (142:4); `refuge-in-trouble` — "I cried to you, LORD. I said, 'You are my refuge, my portion in the land of the living'" (142:5); `lament` — complaint poured out from a dead end: "I pour out my complaint before him. I tell him my troubles," with spirit overwhelmed and no one who cares (142:1–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 143
**Summary:** David prays as a hunted and honest man: hear me in your faithfulness — and "Don't enter into judgment with your servant, for in your sight no man living is righteous" (a verdict Paul's teaching later echoes, Romans 3:20). The enemy has struck his life down to the ground and made him live in dark places like those long dead; his spirit is overwhelmed, his heart desolate — so he reaches backward and upward at once, remembering the days of old, meditating on God's doings, spreading out his hands: "My soul thirsts for you, like a parched land." The petitions come quickly, morning-aimed: let me hear your loving kindness in the morning, show me the way to walk, teach me to do your will — "Your Spirit is good. Lead me in the land of uprightness" — revive me, and deal with my enemies, "for I am your servant."
**Tags:** `prayer` — "Hear my prayer, LORD. Listen to my petitions… Hurry to answer me" (143:1, 7); `guidance` — "Cause me to know the way in which I should walk… Teach me to do your will… Lead me in the land of uprightness" (143:8, 10); `hunger-for-god` — "I spread out my hands to you. My soul thirsts for you, like a parched land" (143:6); `pastoral-hope-in-despair` — an overwhelmed spirit and desolate heart praying toward morning loving kindness (143:3–4, 7–8); `trust-in-god` — "Cause me to hear your loving kindness in the morning, for I trust in you" (143:8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 144
**Summary:** David blesses "the LORD, my rock, who trains my hands to war" — his loving kindness, fortress, high tower, deliverer, and shield — then marvels that such a God bothers with man at all: "LORD, what is man, that you care for him?… Man is like a breath. His days are like a shadow that passes away." He prays for a theophany in miniature — part your heavens, touch the mountains, throw out lightning — to rescue him from the great waters and from foreigners whose mouths speak deceit, and he promises a new song on the ten-stringed lyre to the God who gives salvation to kings. The final movement prays the kingdom's peace into pictures: sons like well-nurtured plants, daughters like palace pillars, full barns, teeming flocks, laden oxen, no breaking in and no outcry in the streets — "Happy are the people whose God is the LORD."
**Tags:** `refuge-in-trouble` — the cascade of names: "my rock… my fortress, my high tower, my deliverer, my shield, and he in whom I take refuge" (144:1–2); `gods-protection` — "rescue me, and deliver me out of great waters, out of the hands of foreigners" (144:7, 10–11); `praise` — "Blessed be the LORD, my rock," and "I will sing a new song to you, God" (144:1, 9); `blessing` — the prayed-for flourishing of sons, daughters, barns, and streets: "Happy are the people whose God is the LORD" (144:12–15); `mortality` — the marveling question under the war-psalm: "LORD, what is man, that you care for him?… Man is like a breath. His days are like a shadow that passes away" (144:3–4)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 145
**Summary:** "A praise psalm by David" — the Psalter's last acrostic, and the doorway to its closing hallelujahs: "I will exalt you, my God, the King… Every day I will praise you." Its claims stack generation on generation: one commends God's works to another, for "Great is the LORD, and greatly to be praised! His greatness is unsearchable." At the center is the old confession made spacious — "The LORD is gracious, merciful, slow to anger, and of great loving kindness. The LORD is good to all" — and a kingdom everlasting, with the LORD faithful in all his words. The psalm then watches that kingness at work: upholding all who fall, raising all who are bowed down, opening his hand to satisfy every living thing, near to all who call on him in truth, hearing the cry of those who fear him, preserving all who love him — but destroying all the wicked. It ends handing the microphone to everyone: "Let all flesh bless his holy name forever and ever."
**Tags:** `praise` — "I will exalt you, my God, the King… Every day I will praise you" (145:1–3, 21); `gods-love` — "The LORD is gracious, merciful, slow to anger, and of great loving kindness. The LORD is good to all" (145:8–9); `gods-provision` — "The eyes of all wait for you… You open your hand, and satisfy the desire of every living thing" (145:15–16); `gods-faithfulness` — "The LORD is faithful in all his words, and loving in all his deeds" (145:13); `prayer` — "The LORD is near to all those who call on him… He also will hear their cry, and will save them" (145:18–19); `god-reigns` — praise addressed to the King: "I will exalt you, my God, the King" — an everlasting kingdom whose glory one generation commends to another (145:1, 11–13); `slow-to-anger` — the Exodus 34 formula's second Psalter sounding: "The LORD is gracious, merciful, slow to anger, and of great loving kindness" (145:8)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 146
**Summary:** The final hallel opens: "Praise the LORD! Praise the LORD, my soul" — a lifelong resolve, immediately paired with a warning about misplaced confidence: "Don't put your trust in princes, in a son of man in whom there is no help," whose spirit departs, who returns to the earth, whose thoughts perish that very day. Happiness belongs instead to the one "who has the God of Jacob for his help, whose hope is in the LORD" — maker of heaven, earth, and sea, keeper of truth forever. The psalm then lists what this God characteristically does: executes justice for the oppressed, feeds the hungry, frees prisoners, opens blind eyes, raises the bowed down, loves the righteous, preserves foreigners, upholds the fatherless and widow — and turns the way of the wicked upside down. It closes on his unending reign: "The LORD will reign forever; your God, O Zion, to all generations. Praise the LORD!"
**Tags:** `praise` — "While I live, I will praise the LORD. I will sing praises to my God as long as I exist" (146:1–2, 10); `hope-in-god` — "Happy is he who has the God of Jacob for his help, whose hope is in the LORD, his God" (146:5; existing anchor); `trust-in-god` — trust redirected from perishing princes to the LORD who keeps truth forever (146:3–6); `justice-and-oppression` — what this God characteristically does: "who executes justice for the oppressed; who gives food to the hungry. The LORD frees the prisoners," upholding the fatherless and widow (146:7–9); `god-reigns` — the closing acclamation: "The LORD will reign forever; your God, O Zion, to all generations" (146:10)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 147
**Summary:** "Praise the LORD, for it is good to sing praises to our God; for it is pleasant and fitting to praise him" — and the psalm interleaves two registers of reasons. The LORD of Israel's story builds up Jerusalem, gathers the outcasts, heals the broken in heart and binds up their wounds; the LORD of the cosmos counts the number of the stars and calls them all by their names, his understanding infinite. His preferences confound power: no delight in the horse's strength or a man's legs — "The LORD takes pleasure in those who fear him, in those who hope in his loving kindness." Zion is told to praise the God who strengthens the bars of her gates, blesses her children, makes peace in her borders, and fills her with the finest of the wheat; his word runs swiftly through snow, frost, hail, thaw, and wind — and the same word goes to Jacob as statutes and ordinances: "He has not done this for just any nation."
**Tags:** `praise` — "it is good to sing praises to our God; for it is pleasant and fitting to praise him" (147:1, 12; existing anchor); `gods-provision` — rain, grass, food for livestock and calling ravens, peace and the finest of the wheat (147:8–9, 14); `humble-exaltation` — "The LORD upholds the humble. He brings the wicked down to the ground" (147:6); `providence` — weather run by his sent word: "He sends out his word, and melts them. He causes his wind to blow" (147:15–18); `zion-city-of-god` — the city built and blessed: "The LORD builds up Jerusalem," and Zion is told to praise the God who "has strengthened the bars of your gates" and blessed her children within her (147:2, 12–14); `restoration-of-israel` — the regathering register: "He gathers together the outcasts of Israel," and "He heals the broken in heart, and binds up their wounds" (147:2–3); `fear-of-the-lord` — the pleasure-verse at the psalm's center: "The LORD takes pleasure in those who fear him, in those who hope in his loving kindness" (147:10–11)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 148
**Summary:** Praise summoned from the top of creation to the bottom: first the heavens — angels and heavenly armies, sun, moon, and shining stars, the heavens of heavens — told to praise the LORD's name, "for he commanded, and they were created," fixed by a decree that will not pass away. Then the earth answers: sea creatures and depths, lightning, hail, snow, clouds, and stormy wind fulfilling his word, mountains, hills, fruit trees, cedars, animals wild and tame, small creatures and flying birds — and finally people, all of them: kings and all peoples, princes and judges, young men and maidens, old men and children. One reason gathers everything: "for his name alone is exalted. His glory is above the earth and the heavens" — and one closing note brings it home: he has lifted up the horn of his people, "a people near to him. Praise the LORD!"
**Tags:** `praise` — the universal summons, heavens first and earth answering: "Praise the LORD from the heavens!… Praise the LORD from the earth" (148:1–4, 7–13); `creation` — "Let them praise the LORD's name, for he commanded, and they were created. He has also established them forever and ever" (148:5–6)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 149
**Summary:** "Sing to the LORD a new song" — Israel is told to rejoice in him who made them, the children of Zion to be joyful in their King, with dance, tambourine, and harp, "For the LORD takes pleasure in his people. He crowns the humble with salvation." Even bedtime joins in: the saints sing for joy on their beds. Then the psalm turns martial: high praises of God in their mouths and a two-edged sword in their hand, to execute on the nations "the written judgment" — vengeance and punishments, kings bound with chains and nobles with fetters of iron — an honor the psalm assigns to all his saints. The summary takes the text as it stands: Israel's praise and Israel's warfare joined in one calling under its King, with the judgment executed being the one God has written.
**Tags:** `praise` — "Sing to the LORD a new song, his praise in the assembly of the saints" (149:1–3); `joy-in-the-lord` — "Let Israel rejoice in him who made them… Let the saints rejoice in honor. Let them sing for joy on their beds" (149:2, 5); `humble-exaltation` — "the LORD takes pleasure in his people. He crowns the humble with salvation" (149:4); `divine-judgment` — the written judgment executed on the nations, their kings bound (149:6–9); `vengeance` — the executed "written judgment" — vengeance on the nations commanded under Israel's King — described as the psalm's own assignment, without generalizing the warrant beyond the text (149:6–9)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

## Psalms 150
**Summary:** The Psalter's last word is a fountain of imperatives — thirteen calls to praise in six verses. Where: in his sanctuary, and in his heavens. Why: "for his acts of power… according to his excellent greatness." How: with the sounding of the trumpet, harp and lyre, tambourine and dancing, stringed instruments and flute, loud cymbals and resounding cymbals — the whole orchestra of Israel's worship. Who: "Let everything that has breath praise the LORD! Praise the LORD!" — the book of prayers ending with every living breath turned to praise.
**Tags:** `praise` — the final doxology entire: "Let everything that has breath praise the LORD!" (150:1–6); `worship` — praise located in the sanctuary and voiced with trumpet, strings, pipe, dance, and cymbals (150:1, 3–5)
**Sources:** brooks-1919; mhcc; bsb-headings

# Decisions record (delegated defaults — reversible on Jesse's word)

1. **Psalm 119 subdivided — the batch's only subdivision.** CONVENTIONS §4 takes boundaries strictly from BSB headings. The public-domain BSB (engbsb USFM, 2026-08-08 edition) places one titled heading per psalm across 101–150 — with a single exception: Psalm 119 carries, in addition to its main heading, twenty-two acrostic stanza headings (USFM `\qa` markers: ALEPH, BETH … SIN and SHIN, TAW) at exactly the eight-verse stanza starts (1, 9, 17, … 169). These are BSB-provided structural headings that also mark the book's own acrostic structure, so Psalm 119 is subdivided on precisely those 22 boundaries, which exactly partition 1–176. No other psalm in this range receives sections.
2. **Stanza titles rendered "Aleph," not "ALEPH."** The BSB acrostic headings are set in capitals — except the conjunction in "SIN and SHIN," which the BSB prints lowercase; this draft normalizes them to title case ("ALEPH" → "Aleph," "SIN and SHIN" → "Sin and Shin") without changing any word, and marks them `(title: BSB)`. The WEB's own letter renderings embedded in the verse text (VAV, TZADHE, QOPH, TAV — and the current edition's "KAPF" at the 119:80 seam, an apparent upstream typographical variant) are not used as titles and are not quoted; titles follow BSB per CONVENTIONS §4.
3. **Vocabulary ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The taggable vocabulary is the 131 YAML file **basenames** under `ontology/concepts/` on main at b3f491d, including the 14 `pastoral-*` files whose internal ids differ. Every tag in this batch was validated against that list.
4. **PR #43 ids used, each marked (project-wide ruling).** Three uses, all reversible delegated defaults: `christ-the-cornerstone` on Psalm 118 — the psalm's own text carries the cornerstone saying (118:22), and the concept pack itself lists Ps 118:22 among its corpus-deferred source anchors, which distinguishes this from batch 1's declined cases (there the packs did not anchor the psalm); the Christ-identification is signposted as NT application in the summary, never asserted as the psalm's own claim. `benediction` on Psalms 128 and 134 — both close with a blessing spoken over the worshiper ("May the LORD bless you out of Zion," "May the LORD bless you from Zion"), which is that locator's substance (a benediction pronounced over people, the very line batch 1 used to *decline* the tag at 41:13 and 28:9); the pack's own anchors are elsewhere (Num 6; NT closings), so this is presence-based, flagged for review. The other seven PR #43 ids were considered and not used. *2026-08-25: ratified by Jesse 2026-08-25 — no longer reversible-pending-review.*
5. **Pastoral-register ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** `pastoral-*` ids are the personal-crisis register and are applied only where the psalm's own register matches. Kept (all seven instances): 102 (`pastoral-hope-in-despair` — an individual afflicted man's prayer), 103 (`pastoral-prayer-for-healing` — an individual's own-soul thanksgiving standing on the pack's own 103:2–3 anchor, parallel to batch 1's kept Psalm 30), 109 and 140 (`pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — individual laments of a poor and needy man), 116 (`pastoral-serious-illness`), 139 (`pastoral-pregnancy-and-child-loss` — individual meditation carrying that pack's own anchor verses), 143 (`pastoral-hope-in-despair`). Dropped under the ruling despite genuine thematic presence: **Psalm 137** (`pastoral-grief-and-loss` — communal exile grief; ruling names this psalm), **Psalm 146** (`pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — a communal hymn cataloging national-scale justice), **Psalm 147** (`pastoral-near-to-the-brokenhearted` — a communal Zion hymn, even though 147:3 is that pack's existing anchor; the search anchor still serves queries — display tags follow the register ruling), and **Psalm 129** (`pastoral-refuge-and-justice` — "Let Israel now say"). All dropped material is logged as refs for the shared log's existing Micah `justice-and-oppression` row and Joel `lament` row (see Tag gaps). *2026-08-25: the tag-application pass (see the record at the end of this document) applied the adopted concepts those logs pointed to — `justice-and-oppression` on 129 and 146, `lament` and `sojourners-and-strangers` on 137, and `restoration-of-israel` on 147:2–3 — so the dropped registers now surface under the adopted display vocabulary.*
6. **Divine-name rendering ruling applied (project-wide, adopted 2026-08-23).** The pinned engwebp corpus renders the divine name "the LORD"; every quotation in this batch uses that rendering, verified mechanically against the corpus text.
7. **Psalm 103 and `providence` — dropped by the six-tag cap despite the existing anchor at 103:19.** "His kingdom rules over all" is one clause; the chapter's main themes (praise, steadfast love, forgiveness, unearned mercy, healing, renewal) fill the cap with stronger presences. Reversible by swapping out `grace-not-earned` or `restoration`. *2026-08-25: the tag-application pass added `slow-to-anger` and `mortality` under the conventions addendum's 8-tag ceiling; `providence` remains off — it is not on the approved worklist, and this item's one-clause judgment stands.*
8. **Psalm 110 carries one tag.** The psalm's own text yields `divine-judgment` (110:5–6) honestly. Its enthronement, priesthood, and Davidic-Lord themes have no vocabulary home that would not read NT categories back into the OT text (`deity-of-christ`, `incarnation` considered and declined); they are logged instead under Zechariah's landed `messianic-prophecy` row and Isaiah's landed `god-reigns` row (see consolidation hazards 5 and 6), and the NT's use of the psalm is signposted in the summary. *2026-08-25: the tag-application pass added `messianic-prophecy` — now an adopted display concept — on the NT quotations this item logs; `divine-judgment` is no longer the psalm's only tag.*
9. **Psalm 137 carries one tag (`divine-judgment`).** With `pastoral-grief-and-loss` excluded by the register ruling (item 5), the honest remainder is the psalm's plea that the LORD remember Edom and its declaration over Babylon ("doomed to destruction"). The imprecatory ending is described in the summary as the text presents it — the violated handing repayment to God, measured "as you have done to us" — without softening and without commendation beyond the text. The grief and exile themes route to the shared log's existing Joel `lament` row and Genesis `sojourners-and-strangers` row (see Tag gaps). *2026-08-25: the tag-application pass applied the adopted concepts `lament`, `sojourners-and-strangers`, `vengeance`, and `zion-city-of-god`; the psalm now carries five tags, each within this item's described frame.*
10. **Psalm 108's relationship to Psalms 57 and 60 stated as observation.** The summary notes that 108's two halves reappear, with small variations, from Psalms 57:7–11 and 60:5–12 (verified word-by-word against the batch-2 WEB extraction). This is an intra-Psalter textual fact, kept out of the tag justifications (which must stay in-chapter) and stated without any documentary theory.
11. **Psalm 112 and prosperity framing.** The psalm's wealth-and-riches portrait is summarized as the shape of the blessed God-fearer's life, explicitly "not a formula owed to anyone," per the doctrinal guardrail (DOCTRINAL-BASIS exclusion of prosperity-gospel framing). Same discipline at 128 ("a covenant picture, not a bargain") and 144:12–15.
12. **Psalm 122 and `gathering-together` — tagged.** The concept's substance (God's people assembling) is genuinely depicted: a summons accepted together, tribes going up together by ordinance. Tagged on in-chapter presence; the pack's own anchors are NT assembly texts, so flagged as presence-based for review.
13. **Psalm 128 and `godly-marriage` — not tagged.** The fruitful-vine wife belongs to a household blessing picture; the psalm teaches no marriage instruction. Same line batch 1 drew at Psalm 45.
14. **Psalm 149:6–9 described, not moralized.** The two-edged-sword verses are summarized as the psalm's own assignment to Israel under its King — executing "the written judgment" — without either spiritualizing the sword away or generalizing the warrant beyond the text.
15. **New Testament quotations — signposted, never asserted as the psalm's own claim.** Where the NT quotes or applies a psalm in this batch (102:25–27 → Heb 1:10–12; 109:8 → Acts 1:20; 110:1, 4 → Matt 22:41–45, Heb 5–7; 112:9 → 2 Cor 9:9; 117:1 → Rom 15:11; 118:22–26 → Matt 21:9, 42, Acts 4:11, 1 Pet 2:7; 132:11 → Acts 2:30; 140:3 → Rom 3:13; 143:2 → Rom 3:20), the summary marks it explicitly as a later application. Quotations not noted in-summary (104:4 → Heb 1:7; 116:10 → 2 Cor 4:13; 118:6 → Heb 13:6; 135:14 → Heb 10:30 region) were judged below the signposting threshold to keep summaries at 3–5 sentences; reversible.
16. **Hallel traditions handled as tradition.** The batch notes describe 113–118 only by what the text shows (praise psalms; 117's universal summons) and mark the pilgrim reading of the Songs of Ascents as traditional; no liturgical-calendar claims (Passover usage etc.) are asserted anywhere, since the psalms' own text does not state them.
17. **Provenance split stated per chapter-set, not silently pooled.** 9 chapters verified against the pinned-snapshot fixture (byte-identical to current); 41 chapters current-edition only — see the header.

# Notes toward the book summary (for the book-summary worker)

- **The Psalter's ending is engineered praise:** Book IV closes at 106:48; Book V gathers thanksgiving (107, 118, 136), torah devotion (119), pilgrimage (120–134), David redivivus (138–145), and then five hallelujah psalms (146–150) that end the book with "Let everything that has breath praise the LORD!" A book that opens with the blessed man meditating (1) ends with everything that breathes praising — a shape worth stating.
- **Psalm 119 as the Psalter's torah centerpiece** mirrors Psalms 1 and 19 (batch 1); the book summary can join the three.
- **The Songs of Ascents (120–134)** are the Psalter's one labeled sub-collection; note also the acrostics (111, 112, 119, 145 in this batch).
- **"His loving kindness endures forever"** (106; 107; 118; 136) — the WEB's rendering of the covenant-love refrain — pairs with batch 1's note on "loving kindness" as the book's most repeated attribute.
- **Kingship crescendo:** the enthronement psalms of batch 2 (93–99) are answered here by 110 (the LORD's oath to David's Lord), 132 (the Davidic covenant), 145 ("my God, the King"), and 146:10 ("The LORD will reign forever") — the book summary's messianic thread should keep the batch's discipline: text first, NT application signposted.
- **The imprecatory question** concentrates in this batch (109; 137; 139:19–22): a one-sentence book-level orientation (the wronged handing judgment to God) will spare each chapter re-arguing it.
- **Historical memory as praise:** 105 (covenant kept), 106 (sin confessed), 135–136 (both together) give the book summary its clearest statement that Israel praises by retelling.
- **The poor, the prisoner, the barren, the brokenhearted** (102, 107, 113, 116, 146, 147) — Book V keeps returning to God's downward attention; pairs with batch 1's note on God's sight and hearing of the sufferer.

# Originality check (CONVENTIONS §7)

Method: every chapter summary was drafted fresh from the WEB text; after drafting, a mechanical normalized 5-gram scan (lowercased, punctuation stripped) was run over this whole document against the staged full texts of the two public-domain references actually consulted — Brooks' *Summarized Bible* (Psalms 101–150 section, archive.org OCR) and *Matthew Henry's Concise Commentary* (Psalms 101–150, staged per chapter) — with direct WEB scripture quotations excluded from the comparison.

- **biblesummary.info:** not consulted for this batch at all; no text was available to copy. Zero exposure, zero overlap.
- **BibleProject:** not consulted for this batch at all; no framing or wording drawn from it (`bibleproject-framing` accordingly appears in no Sources line). Zero exposure, zero overlap.
- **brooks-1919:** 1 shared 5-gram (re-run on the revised v5 text), scriptural stock only ("the LORD for he is," from the refrain "Give thanks to the LORD, for he is good") — Bible phrasing both texts carry. No sentence lifted, no archaic wording carried over.
- **mhcc:** 109 shared 5-grams (re-run on the revised v5 text; the round-3 Ps 148 fix removed the "praise the name of the LORD" overlap and the round-4 Ps 111 fix removed the "the works of the LORD" overlap the round-4 critic correctly re-classified as a rival echo), all but 5 being unquoted WEB scripture wording flowing through both texts ("as far as the east is from the west," "keep the door of my lips," "idols are silver and gold"); the remaining 5 are near-scriptural stock ("all who fear the LORD," "hands in the sanctuary and," "in the LORD for with") or windows crossing verse-number tokens in the staged source ("in the congregation 111 1," "at his right hand 109"). The longest runs are consecutive windows of single scriptural phrases, not sentences. No sentence lifted, no archaic wording carried over.

Archaism check: the draft was scanned for KJV-era forms (unto, thereof, hath, shalt, thee, thou, ye, doth, saith); none appear outside this sentence. The WEB's own renderings are used throughout (e.g., "loving kindness," "Sheol," "the LORD").

# Consolidated tag gaps — the single Psalms plan for the final tag-gaps.md write

This section merges the three batches' Tag gaps sections into **one** plan and resolves every consolidation flag the batches raised. The per-batch Tag gaps sections above are preserved as audit trail but are **superseded by this section**; the final write executes this plan only. **Everything below is subject to a final re-dedupe at write time** against the live shared file, under the CONVENTIONS §9 addendum (atomic end-of-file append only; post-write byte verification that pre-existing content is unchanged and the block is present; survival re-audit at final delivery).

**Assembly-time re-verification (2026-08-23):** the live `/mnt/project-files/research/bible-rollout/tag-gaps.md` was re-read in full at assembly. It has kept growing past every count the batches recorded — the coordinator's brief estimated ~1,858 lines with the Daniel appends; at this assembly's read it stood at **2,022 lines**, with the Daniel block joined by John (Gospels+Acts), Hebrews-delivery, Revelation-delivery, and Deuteronomy blocks (and it demonstrably grew *during* this assembly's reads — sibling threads are still appending). Verified against that state:

- **All 25 append-target rows below exist** in the live file under their stated owner books.
- **None of the four proposed new-row ids exists as a row anywhere in the live file** — they appear only as the Proverbs block's held contingencies ("Held for the Psalms write — CONTINGENT") and the Ecclesiastes block's `aging-and-old-age` hand-off, both of which explicitly instruct the Psalms write to fold their refs in.
- **The newest blocks create no collisions:** Daniel's new rows are `spiritual-warfare` and `end-times`; Deuteronomy's are `circumcision-of-the-heart` and `warfare`; Hebrews minted no colliding id and Revelation minted no new row at all. No Zion/Jerusalem row exists anywhere in the file.
- **One adjacency found and recorded** (not a collision): Isaiah's live `power-of-gods-word` row (the word's own permanence and efficacy — "God's word does not return void"; Jeremiah refs merged) sits near the proposed `trustworthiness-of-scripture` row (the reliability/flawlessness claim — "is God's word reliable"). The batches checked this row at their own dedupes and kept the proposals distinct; the cross-note is carried on the row below for the curator.

## C. The `god-reigns` / `kingdom-of-heaven` adjacency — the deliberate call (reversible)

The Isaiah `god-reigns` row and Matthew `kingdom-of-heaven` row each carry a cross-note deferring a joint decision (one concept, two, or an extension) — a deferral the Zechariah thread's Zech 14:9 note also joined — and batch 3's flag 6 required the Psalms consolidation to resolve the adjacency deliberately rather than by silent merge. **This consolidation's call: keep them as two separate rows, with the cross-note retained on both.** The registers are genuinely different — `god-reigns` is the OT divine-kingship acclamation (the enthronement declaration "The LORD reigns," whose five WEB-Psalter occurrences are 9:7; 93:1; 96:10; 97:1; 99:1, all appended to the Isaiah row in §A.4), while `kingdom-of-heaven` is Matthew's NT kingdom-announcement teaching register ("kingdom of heaven meaning," "parables of the kingdom"). Collapsing them would invite exactly the read-back the conventions bar in both directions: OT enthronement texts tagged into an NT-announcement concept, or Matthew's teaching read back onto the Psalter. A future concept-pack curator remains free to design one concept spanning both registers at pack time — this call governs only the gap log's rows. Recorded as a reversible delegated call per the Decisions-record pattern; Jesse can overturn it with one word, and the rows' retained cross-notes preserve the merge option.

## D. Superseded and withdrawn proposals (record only — nothing to write for these)

- Batch 1's proposed `lament` row → superseded by the landed Joel `lament` row (refs consolidated in §A.1).
- Batch 1's proposed `god-reigns` row → superseded by the landed Isaiah `god-reigns` row (refs in §A.4; call in §C).
- Batch 1's proposed `messiah-in-the-psalms` row → superseded by the landed Zechariah `messianic-prophecy` row (refs in §A.5).
- The alternative id `the-lords-anointed` → withdrawn (it names a live 1 Samuel row of a different register — the touch-not-the-LORD's-anointed narrative).
- Batch 2's staged `idolatry` row → superseded by the landed Micah `idolatry` row (refs in §A.3).
- The staged `justice-and-oppression-of-the-poor` id → superseded by the landed unified Micah `justice-and-oppression` row (refs in §A.2), the same path Amos's identically-named staged gap took.

## E. Write-time protocol (binding, per CONVENTIONS §9 and its addendum)

1. Re-read the live tag-gaps.md immediately before writing; **final re-dedupe of every entry above** against the file as it stands then (it grew from ~1,858 to 2,022 lines during this assembly alone; more blocks will land).
2. Write the Psalms block as **one atomic end-of-file append** — never a whole-file rewrite; appends to existing rows go to the named rows' Where columns per the file's own append rules.
3. After writing, re-read and verify pre-existing bytes are unchanged and the Psalms block is present; note in the block that the Proverbs contingencies and the Ecclesiastes `aging-and-old-age` hand-off are resolved by the minted rows, so later readers of those blocks can trace the fold.
4. At final delivery, run the survival audit: grep the live file for the Psalms refs and re-apply any lost rows, re-deduped and verified present exactly once.
